<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse on the "Ornaments" or "Adornments" of a musician, following the discussion of a musician's character and social conduct.</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<note>original: "De la Musica del Cerone"</note>

...and such that when conversing with common people, he is as harmonious and consonant as when he is singing or composing a smooth and sweet piece of music.

## Chapter in which certain notices are placed for the greater clarity and satisfaction of the reader. Chap. II.

<margin>
Comparison.

The more perfect a Musician is, the more he is disfigured by whatever small defect he may have.
</margin>

**I**t seems at first glance that it would be more appropriate to place these *Ornaments* <note>Atavios: literally "trappings" or "adornments," here referring to the social and technical habits of a musician</note> at the end rather than at the beginning of the work; for what I have done seems as much out of order and reason as a father who seeks to dress and adorn his daughter before she is even born. But consider that many times it is better not to know how to sing, nor compose, nor play, etc., than to know it with vices that disfigure—all the more so because in a preeminent Singer or Musician, every tiny defect seems great, and makes him lose the honor and reputation he has gained for being a skilled Singer or excellent Composer. Just as on the face, spots or warts look worse and disfigure more than large scars on other parts of the body, so do small defects and vices appear worse when seen and recognized in one who is esteemed as a good Singer and perfect Musician, due to the opinion many hold of his skill (which is worthy of being clean of every defect and bad habit). These defects do not seem so large in the rabble and the "little musicians" of low quality. It will not be, I say, so far off the path or without purpose to have begun this book with this section on *Ornaments*, just as in the judgment of the wise, the clever demonstration of that witty painter was not misplaced, who began to paint a fox starting from the tail, with a sign that said:

-> *Because of who you are, this is fitting.* <-
<note>original: "Per esser chi tu sei, così conviene"</note>

<margin>Pausanias, excellent painter.</margin>
And perhaps he wished to allude to the Latin proverb, *The tail bears witness to the fox* <note>original: "Cauda de vulpe testatur"</note>. To this purpose, it will not be unreasonable to relate what Plutarch writes of another painter. He says, then, that a painter named Pausanias made an agreement with a man to paint him a horse that was with its legs upward, because that suited his purpose and intention. But the painter, forgetting this, painted it running. The one who commissioned the painting becoming indignant at this, the painter said smiling: "Turn the board over, and you will find it to your liking." And so it was, for as soon as he turned the board on which the horse was painted, it remained with its legs upward; and thus what previously looked very bad to him, now looked very good, merely by giving it a turn. In the same manner may he do who desires to read these *Ornaments* at the end: for by turning the book around, what is the beginning shall be the end; and he who does not like them at the end either, let him put them wherever it seems best to him and more to his taste, as long as he remains content and entirely satisfied.

<margin>Notices so that each one may take the documents that suit him with entire satisfaction.</margin>
Note that in the discourse of this treatise I sometimes use the word *thou*, other times *you*, and other times *Your Mercies* <note>Vs. Ms. / Vuestras Mercedes: a formal address</note>, and this distinction is made with respect to the quality of the person to whom the specific advice being given pertains.

Note also that when I go about stating the parts and conditions that a good Master must have, and the manner in which he must proceed with his disciples, how and in what way; and when I advise something to the Chapel Masters <term>Maestro de Capilla: the director of music in a cathedral or court</term>, it is understood that the disciple must strive to have such parts and conditions when he becomes a Master. Thus, he must take advantage of some notices first as a disciple, and others later as a Master. Let the discreet reader further note that in many and diverse places I use the term MUSICIAN improperly, only so as not to depart from the common way of speaking. Take notice also that under this name, "Master of Music," I speak only of the Masters who give lessons and keep a public school for singing, as well as for counterpoint and composition; and not of the Chapel Masters, nor of those who compose privately.

<margin>Look/Note.</margin>
Likewise note, that under this term *Sacristan* or *Choir Boy*, I mean all those who give themselves out to be great Musicians without being so. But let the Reader who does not understand Latin note that there is no Latin word that (where necessary) does not go explained. Finally, I pray and beseech everyone that if in the progress of reading this Treatise...

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Which concerns Ornaments and moral harmonies.</header>

(particularly regarding Ornaments <note>original: "Atauios," referring to musical embellishments or graces</note>) should you feel some blow coming upon you—as sometimes happens when reading the writings of others—you either develop a "good stomach" by saying, "the author in this passage means someone else and not me"; or you dodge it with skill, as if that morsel tasted bitter. Or you act as some modern spirits do, who, not wishing to understand the literal sense of the text, give a mystical meaning to the words of others; with their exposition, they extol the value of the authors in such a way that they seem to have said things further away than the East Indies and things higher than the rooftops. But I pray you, do not act like the monkey, who breaks the mirrors where he sees his deformed features and his ugly disposition: for I shall never show that any of you is an ugly Ape or a black raven, provided you are a beautiful marten or a white ermine. To conclude this present chapter, I give notice that I should not be considered lacking in memory if, in the course of the work, I repeat the same thing several times; I do this for the sake of those new to Music. It is enough that in this case I serve them as a laborer, bringing materials to their hand for the work. They, as those desirous of being good Masters, should know how to take advantage of everything that is said; for the purpose of making them such, I communicate it so many times. And if it should appear (as I said) that some things remain without explanation, assume that I knew no more; or consider that I did it so that others might catch the prey that I have flushed out.

<margin>The method used by some to give a contrary meaning to what they read when they feel touched by the reading.</margin>
<margin>The monkey breaks the mirrors. A warning.</margin>

# Why this treatise was given the title of MASTER or MELOPEO. Cap. III.
<note>Melopeo: from the Greek 'melopoia,' the art of composing melody.</note>

<margin>This present book supplies what is lacking where Masters are absent.</margin>

**B**efore saying anything else, it seemed appropriate to me to put in this present chapter a few reasons that moved me to entitle this present treatise THE MASTER, so that it may be understood that the said title is not as out of place, nor as arrogant, as it seems. The first thing that moved me to title it so was that the villages and places of Spain are so lacking in Music Masters; and considering that the few who are in them, for the most part, do not fully know what is necessary to know: and if they do know, not all want to teach faithfully (as I said in the Preamble) to those who truly desire to know this Art. This present book, then, supplies everything that the said Masters lack; because it teaches liberally what they say sparingly: it speaks (so to speak) from the heart what they maliciously keep silent: it discovers with love what they (perhaps) hide out of hatred; and finally, because it teaches with great clarity what they show with various obscurities.

<margin>Difference between the manner of writing used by those who have written on Music and the manner used here by the author.</margin>

Also because the manner of teaching is as if a Master were teaching personally with words; namely, with long reasons, with simple words, with examples from fables and histories, with witty sayings, with grave maxims, with appropriate similarities, with long digressions, with familiar concepts, and finally with such diversities that it resembles a new Italian *ensalada* <note>A musical genre (literally "salad") consisting of a medley of different styles or tunes.</note>. This way of writing is appropriate so that the new disciple may understand better and become capable of what is being said more easily. It was not possible to put this mixture in writing under the name of *Art of Music*, nor any other title, without giving everyone just cause to mock me. And since my intent is directed only to the benefit of the reader, and to no other end, it seemed to me that I could not keep silent about what I say without harm to the one who reads: nor was it convenient for me to write such trifles in a book that would be held as a *Compendium* or *Art of Music*, for that would be outside the customs of those who ordinarily write on this subject. Because while they write succinctly and briefly, they do not speak face-to-face with the new disciples, but rather write for professional people who already know much: but I, although I write, consider that I do not write, but rather that I speak to beginners and to those who know little and presume much. To conclude, it must be presupposed that all this which I write here is as if I do not write it, but truly teach it with living voices and without writing.

B <meta>catchword: So [Tam.]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the introductory material of Pedro Cerone’s musical treatise, focusing on the relationship between student and text, and defining the intended audience for the work.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>From the Music of Cerone, Book I.</header>

It must also be presupposed that when the Reader reads, he does not merely read, but rather listens with his ears and hears what his Master tells him. Considering all these particulars, I say that the title of MASTER justly suits this book (since it performs its office); and also because the human desire, as much sated by antiquities as it is curious for novelties, seems to require no other title more properly than this one or something similar. I advise those who read this treatise that if they do not understand it at once, they should not lose heart; for by having their own wit, practice, and this book as their Master, I say that without having any other Master besides, it will be possible to understand the necessary Music.

<margin>What the ladder of Guido is.</margin>
It is true that to intone the points correctly—that is to say, the voices—it is necessary to have someone show how to perform at least the ladder of Guido <note>The "Guidonian Hand" or hexachord system used for teaching sight-singing.</note>: which is the ascent and descent of the six syllables, namely *Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La*. For with only one's natural ability, however perfect it may be, it is nearly impossible to know how to intone them justly and as is fitting; unless one wished to make use of the Monochord of the ancients, made for this purpose.

<margin>In his Grammar.</margin>
Nor can I give sufficient advice for this, assuming that the voice (as Manutius <note>Aldus Manutius, the famous Renaissance printer and scholar.</note> says) cannot be written down. And take good care to learn to intone these first voices from good Masters: because a bad habit in the beginning is worse than a continuous fever.

---

## For whom the Author writes. Chapter IV.

<margin>To tell a story to a deaf man (proverb); to plow the shore.</margin>
**I** do not intend to have written this treatise for those who are already professors, because I am certain they will not receive into their heads more than what they learned in their tender years; and that giving them rules is like sowing in the sand, or singing to the deaf. Nor do I say I wish to give rules to the famous and excellent Masters, of whom I know there are many in these Kingdoms, because (according to the proverb) it would be like wanting to instruct Minerva <note>original: "Sus Minerva docet" — literally "the pig teaches Minerva," a Latin proverb for an ignorant person trying to teach an expert.</note>, and with much reason it could be said to me, *The pig teaches Minerva*: knowing as I do that it is more fitting for them to give, rather than receive, musical advice and precepts.

<margin>Proverb from Jerome's epistle to Ruffinus. The Author writes for beginners.</margin>
Rather, I write principally for beginners and those new to Music, who desire to know clear and true reasons. But if by chance this should fall into the hands of one of those already professed, I beg him not to look at what is contrary to the precepts he learned as a child; but rather to look without passion at which is better: that which his Masters taught him in times past, or this which I show him at present. The wise disciple listens to what he hears said willingly and with great attention, and then receives what seems best to him; even if it be a new thing to him, and one he never heard his Master say.

<margin>Signs of a disciple eager to learn.</margin>
But the fool does not want to hear a word that goes against the advice and rules of his Master; nor does he want to receive the opinions of others, beyond those shown to him in the beginning. Hard stones and hard iron will soften before these headstrong men soften their hard opinions and come to receive the common view.

<margin>Signs of a disciple who will make little progress.</margin>
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
Just as a wet candle sputters and will not catch the light: so the man who is obstinate and hardened in the opinion of his Master becomes fatigued and distressed by hearing anything contrary to what he was taught, and never wishes to admit the truth. I want all those who read the present treatise to know that I write for men: and not for all, but for those who have understanding: and not for all those who have understanding, but for the dispassionate, who desire to know the truth. I set down my view, founded on the opinion of the best Masters, for those who wish to take it: and those to whom it seems not to the purpose may remain with their own, or seek another to their pleasure and content; and in this way we shall all remain in accord and at peace.

Although it is very much the truth that I have composed this work principally for those new to Music, nevertheless I desire they give me license to say that it is also composed with the intention that certain men may benefit from it who profess to be accomplished and perfect Masters: those who observe an order in composing inherited by custom and usage, and not by certain science.

<margin>Composers who compose by custom and usage.</margin>
Because if they wished to make a Song <term>Cantilena: a vocal melody or polyphonic composition</term> of the Second or Fourth Tone on its natural strings, I believe (and from what I have asked many, I do not believe I am wrong) they would not know what order to maintain. But I have seen...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the necessity of theoretical knowledge (Art) over mere practical experience (Use) in musical composition. It follows a discussion on the foundations of harmony.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Which concerns Ornaments and moral consonances.</header>

## Which concerns Ornaments and moral consonances.

There are many who compose in a good manner, and others who with great promptness add a third or fourth part over the book <note>This refers to "singing over the book," an improvisational technique where singers added harmonies to a written melody on the fly</note>; performing various other very singular refinements acquired through long study and continuous practice. But when it comes to asking them for some musical reasons, each of them remains **like a donkey at the lyre** <note>original: "Tanquã asinus ad lyram." A classical proverb describing someone who is tone-deaf or lacks understanding of the arts</note>; nor do they know how to respond anything else, except that usage taught them so, and that they do not believe they are required to know more than what is used in practice. To this purpose, a Latin writer says: **Many compose songs not out of diligence, but through a certain long-standing habit, perhaps because they have lived in choir stalls since their cradles; but they are ignorant of what it is they have composed.** <note>original: "Multi nō ex industria sed diuturna quædam consuetudine, quia fortè à cunabulis in cantorijs vixerunt, cantica condunt; sed quid sit quod considerunt, ignorant."</note>

<margin>The response of those who compose by habit and not by art.</margin>
There are many Musicians (he says) who, not by industry but by long custom—having perhaps been raised since childhood in singing—compose songs; but they do not know what it is they composed. Those who are not satisfied with what has been said, which is from Ornithoparchus <note>Andreas Ornithoparchus, a 16th-century music theorist</note>, should chew well on these other words, spoken on a similar occasion by Quintilian.

<margin>Ornithoparchus, at the beginning of his Music. Quintilian in his teachings.</margin>
> **By their ineptitudes, lacking the majesty of art and fashioned by habit rather than talent, they dishonor, corrupt, and defile; using whatever signs they please, weighing neither value nor measure, they strive to provide the sweetness of song for the ears of fools rather than for the judgment of the learned through the majesty of art.** <note>original: "Ineptijs suis, citra artis maiestatem, vsu potius quam ingenio fabricatis, dehonestant, corrumpunt, deffedant, quibusque signis pro libitu utentes, nec valorem, nec mensuram librantes, carminis suauitate stultorum auribus, potius quam artis maiestate, doctorum iuditio parare student."</note>

He speaks in this manner: There are some who, saying or writing foolishness without considering whether it fits the art of its genre—not leveled with the weight of reason but carried by their own taste and crude opinion—offend the ears of the Readers. They write whatever occurs to them immediately without discerning it; nor looking at the value and moderation that things placed in a book must have, they compose certain songs with which they wish more to please the ears of foolish idiots than the judgment of learned and expert Musicians. And he goes on to say in another place: **Songs not rooted in the fonts of musical law are to be laughed at, no matter how much they may be in consonance.** <note>original: "Ridenda sunt cantica in iovis Musicæ fontibus non radicata, etiam quantum consona sint."</note>

Certainly, those songs and compositions are amusing and ridiculous (he says) which do not have a good foundation in the precepts of Music, even if the voices happen to harmonize well.

<margin>Take note.</margin>
What is the cause, O Quintilian? **Because the craftsman does not decorate the Art, but the Art decorates the craftsman.** <note>original: "Quoniam non artifex artem, sed ars artificem decorat."</note>

<margin>Note.</margin>
Because (he responds) the craftsman does not honor the Art, but the Art honors the craftsman. These are words to bring much confusion and shame to those who pretend, with such blind and irrational compositions, to honor Music; for they see clearly that he says: while they think they are honoring Music with their compositions, it is Music that honors them. And let them notice he says that even if the voices harmonize well, if the compositions are made without reason and foundation, they are amusing and ridiculous.

But it is to be noted here that, as almost all our knowledge proceeds from the external senses (which is what we hear sung), and things of speculation and art we neither hear, nor taste, nor recognize so easily, it follows that many (especially those of coarse understanding) do not wish to accept as good anything except what seems harmonious to the external sense.

<margin>In what the error of those who compose without art consists.</margin>
And from this they come to discover their ignorance and clumsiness; imagining that the principal mistress of Music is the ear, the common opinion of the masses, or something similar. Therefore, I wish to lead such people by the hand, and little by little with the discourse of this treatise, declare to them the dignity, efficacy, and value of Art: because from this they will rise to understand the value of usage without Art.

<margin>Book 4 on the definition of good and evil.</margin>
Those who despise it should hear Cicero as he declares its greatness and excellence, saying: Art is a captain or leader more certain than nature, even if it be the nature of men clear in understanding; certainly, it is one thing to pour out words in a poetic mode as they come to the mouth, and another thing is to know how to distinguish what you say with art and understanding.

> **Therefore, the science of Music is as much more distinguished in the knowledge of reason than in the performance of the work and in the act, as the body is surpassed by the mind.** <note>original: "In tanto igitur præclarior est scientia Musicæ in cognitione rationis quam in opere efficiendi atque in actu, in quanto corpus mente superatur."</note>

<margin>The perfection and ease of Art and its benefit are discussed in chapter 10 of the Cursus. In book 1 of his music...</margin>
In this much, then (says Bede <note>The Venerable Bede, an English monk and scholar</note>), is the science of Music more enlightened in the knowledge of reason than in the finishing of the work without it, as much as the body is excelled by the understanding. Let him who perfectly desires to profess this Art be diligent in knowing what befits a good and true Musician, so as to be able to give a reason for what he composes: and let him ensure that **no works of the hands exist unless they are guided by reason**; <note>original: "Manuum nulla sint opera nisi ratione ducantur"</note> that the hands do no work that is not governed by reason.

<meta>catchword: reason.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that true musical mastery requires theoretical understanding ("Art") rather than just practical skill ("Use"). The author cites classical and medieval authorities like Quintilian, Cicero, and Bede to shame those who compose by ear without knowing the underlying rules of harmony.</summary>

# Of the Music of Cerone, Book I.

reason. Because finally (as Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, a Roman rhetorician</note> says) *I will grant you only as much as you have proven to me by reason:* <note>original: "Tantum tibi tribuam: quantum mihi ratione comprobaueris"</note> I will praise you for what you have composed only as much as you give me a strong reason why what you compose is good.

---

### How and in what manner new works can be composed. Chapter V.

<margin>Chapter 1. Reasons given by certain idle people, showing that it is neither a matter of skill nor honor to set oneself to writing new works, by saying that there are none.</margin>
When someone, through a lack of knowledge, cannot give a full account of what he knows or what he pretends to be held as, by publishing his studies to the world, at the very least he does not fail to give his opinion when he hears that such-and-such a person has composed a book of Masses or Motets, or that another has written a new Art of Music. He always comes out with that saying from Ecclesiastes: *There is nothing new under the sun, nor is anyone able to say; Behold, this is fresh: for it has already preceded us in the ages that were before us.* <note>original: "Nihil sub Sole nouum, nec valet quicquam dicere; Ecce hoc recens est: iam enim praecessit in saeculis, quae fuerunt ante nos." Reference to Ecclesiastes 1:9-10.</note> The meaning of which is: under the sun there is no new thing; no one can say, "look here, this is new," because it already passed and was seen in the centuries that were before we were born. With this authority, which is so true, they wish to make the ignorant believe that everything written and composed daily is nothing, since it has already been written and said by the ancients. It is as if to say, if it were a matter of great skill, they too would occupy themselves in composing works and would publish their abilities and studies to the world through the printing press. And so, with these "shoutings of Sacristans" <note>A colloquialism for empty or pretentious talk by minor church officials.</note> they come to hide their true idleness—not to say ignorance—under the true mantle of Ecclesiastes. To answer this, I speak in this manner.

<margin>Response to the above.</margin>
Regarding the natures created at the beginning of the world and their successions, in what manner they come and pass, we already know by experience: for we know that man is born and man dies; the nature of the one being born is the same as that of the one who died. The same is said of birds, fish, and all animals; likewise of trees, herbs, and other natures. Nothing is found that has not been before; nothing is past that has not afterwards been remade anew. Listen to Ecclesiastes and you will see it clearly says, *Those things which are to be, already have been:* <note>original: "Quae futura sunt, iam fuerunt"</note> and in the second chapter it shows this more at length, saying: *What is that which was? The very thing that shall be. What is that which was done? The very thing that is to be done.* <note>original: "Quid est quod fuit? ipsum quod futurum est. Quid est quod factum est? ipsum quod faciendum est."</note> <margin>Chapters 1 and 2.</margin> Nor can a man say anything that has not been said at other times and in other ages; and this is by the common opinion of the wise, among whom is Terence, who said: *Nothing is said that has not been said before.* <note>original: "Nihil est dictum, quod prius non sit dictum."</note> <margin>Terence. Aristotle in the 2nd [book of] Metaphysics.</margin> And also as Aristotle relates, saying: The views, opinions, and sayings of the wise that seem newly found have existed in authority long before; but through floods of water, or fires, or the death of men, their books have rotted and been corrupted, and their names, sayings, deeds, and sentences were given over to oblivion. The same will happen to us as well; here the same Ecclesiastes tells us: *There is no memory of earlier things among us, nor indeed will there be any remembrance of those things which are to come afterward among those who shall be in the end.* <note>original: "Non est priorum memoria apud nos, sed nec eorum quidem quae postea futura sunt, erit recordatio apud eos, qui futuri sunt in nouissimo."</note> <margin>As cited above.</margin> And so (returning to the first point), the said things afterwards appear newly invented when man brings them to light once more; invented or, to speak more properly, renewed with the understanding, enlightened (through God) by the nature of the rational soul. This understanding will never fail as long as the same nature lasts; which, giving death to one and life to another, makes the man of today the same as he who was a thousand years ago, in terms of substance and nature. <margin>The office of nature.</margin> *A generation passes away, and a generation arrives;* <note>original: "Generatio praeterit, & generatio aduenit"</note> and consequently the sentences, sayings, and works of these men of the present time are the same as those made and said in the centuries that were before. Or to be more accurate, we must say with the glorious Doctor of the Church, Saint Jerome, that by prescience (which is a thing known beforehand) or by the predestination of God (which is an ordering or deliberation), those things which are to come are already done; and for this reason one can very well affirmatively say, *Nothing is new under the sun.* <margin>Prescience and Predestination. In what manner that which is to come is already done.</margin> And if the authority of this glorious Doctor is not accepted, it would be necessary to say that Judas Iscariot committed treason several times, and that Christ our Redeemer suffered...

<summary>Cerone addresses critics who claim that composing new music is pointless because 'nothing is new under the sun.' He argues that while the substance of human nature and ideas is eternal, each generation 'renews' knowledge through the rational soul and divine enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>musical composition, Ecclesiastes, originality vs. tradition, Aristotle, Saint Jerome, philosophy of nature, music theory, 17th century music</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of "newness" in musical and spiritual compositions, likely following a section on the antiquity of sacred music.</meta>

# Regarding Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>1 Peter 3:18; Romans 6:9-10 <note>The original margin cites 1 Peter 1 and Romans 6. The text refers to Christ dying once for sins.</note></margin>
...to say for us sinners many times: which is against Catholic doctrine, for we read in the Epistle of Saint Peter: *Christ died once for our sins* <note>original: "Christus semel pro peccatis nostris mortuus est"</note>; and in that of Saint Paul, where speaking of the Resurrection, we read: *Christ, rising from the dead, dies no more; death shall no longer have dominion over him, for in that he died to sin, he died once* <note>original: "Christus Resurgens ex mortuis, iam non moritur: Mors illi ultra non dominabitur, qui enim mortuus est peccato, mortuus est semel"</note>.

<margin>In what way things can be new regarding ourselves.</margin>
But speaking in the way that is commonly used, we will say that everything we do or say in our time, which has never been said or done by others (that we know of), though that saying or deed is old regarding the ancients, we say it is new regarding ourselves: and that which is past, through our studies and conversations, we make present. Also, because a thing that is old to one who knows it, sometimes happens to be new to one who does not know it, when they come to learn it. Whether it be invented and found through the speculation and study of the one who composes, or through another's work (speaking commonly), we will say that work and composition is new; and this is because it is considered as a whole regarding the entire invented work and composition, and not only the parts that composed and gave being to the book. And for greater clarity, we will say a book is new in respect to the union of its parts; and old in respect to the parts separated: so that the compositions made in our days are old in respect to the disjunction of all the parts, and new in respect to the union of them.

<margin>In what way new compositions are old. In another way which is with mystical exposition. St. Augustine. St. Jerome. Note.</margin>
Although it is true that regarding certain verses of the Psalms of the Royal David, with which he commands us to sing new songs to God, the Holy Doctors give them another sense with mystical <term>expositions|interpretations that seek a deeper, spiritual meaning beyond the literal text</term>, saying: Whoever wishes to sing these songs, let him love eternal things, so that he may be made new: because the old man sings an old song, namely, of earthly things: and the new man sings a new song, that is, of spiritual things. Also, by the Singer correcting his bad life, amending his customs, and renewing his spirit, he will sing a new song to the Lord: and also because all good meritorious works, which guide us to the new life of grace and glory, are called new songs.

<margin>Another way, which is with a literal sense. Psalms 67, 95, and 32. <note>Modern numbering: Psalms 68, 96, and 33.</note></margin>
With all this, we will say with a simple sense that the Holy King commands us to give thanks to God, telling us, *Sing to the Lord a new song, for he has done marvelous things* <note>original: "Cantate Domino canticum nouum, quia mirabilia fecit"</note>. Sing, he says, to the Lord a new song, because he did wonderful things: and in another place, *Sing to the Lord a new song, sing to the Lord all the earth* <note>original: "Cantate Domino canticum nouum, cantate Domino omnis terra"</note>: Sing to the Lord a new song, sing to the Lord all the earth: and also, *Sing to him a new song; play well to him with a loud voice* <note>original: "Cantate ei canticum nouum: bene psallite ei in vociferatione"</note>. Sing a new song; sing well to the Lord with a good voice.

<margin>Psalm 149.</margin>
Moreover, in Psalm one hundred and forty-nine he says, *Sing to the Lord a new song; his praise is in the assembly of the Saints* <note>original: "Cantate Domino canticum nouum; laus eius in Ecclesia Sanctorum"</note>. Sing to the Lord a new song, in the congregation of the Saints is his praise.

<margin>Psalm 143. <note>Modern numbering: Psalm 144.</note></margin>
And not only does the holy Prophet exhort us to sing new songs to the Lord, but in his own person he says, *O God, I will sing a new song to you* <note>original: "Deus canticum nouum cantabo tibi"</note>: my God, to you I will sing a new song: and in another psalm he said, *And he put a new song in my mouth: a hymn to our God* <note>original: "Et immisit in os meum canticum nouum : carmen Deo nostro"</note>.

<margin>Psalm 39. Revelation chapter 4. <note>Modern numbering: Psalm 40; Revelation 5.</note></margin>
Also in the Apocalypse of Saint John, Apostle and Evangelist, in the fourth chapter we read: *When he had opened the book, the four living creatures and the twenty-four elders fell down before the Lamb, each having harps and golden bowls, and they sang a new song before the throne of God and the Lamb, saying: You are worthy, Lord, to open the book, etc.* <note>original: "Cum aperuisset librum, quatuor animalia, & viginti quatuor seniores ceciderunt coram Agno, habentes singuli cytharas & phialas aureas, & cantabant canticum nouum ante sedem Dei & Agni dicentes: Dignus es Domine aperire librum, &c."</note>

<margin>Christmas Hymn. Lent Hymn.</margin>
->The Holy Church in the Hymn for the Nativity of Our Lord sings:<-
->*On the day of your Birth,*<-
->*We sing together a new hymn.*<-
->And in that of Lent, namely in the Lauds, it concludes in this manner, saying:<-
->*May the universe of things,*<-
->*O merciful Trinity, adore you,*<-
->*And may we, made new through forgiveness,*<-
->*Sing a new song.*<-

<margin>In the book of Judith chapter 16. New songs.</margin>
We read of Judith that after cutting off the head of Holofernes, Captain of King Nebuchadnezzar, she offered the arms and instruments of war to the temple, and that she composed a new song in praise of God. We read also that when the strong Samson killed the thousand Philistines with the jawbone of an ass, he composed a new song.

It cites numerous biblical passages from the Psalms, Revelation, and the Apocrypha to justify the creation of new liturgical music.</summary>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Conclusion.</margin>
Finally, look at the entire discourse of Holy Scripture, and you will see that every time the children of Israel received some particular mercy from God, they composed new songs and praises never before sung.

<margin>Others of a more pleasant humor, who say there are not so many varieties in Music.</margin>
In the same way, then, that the children of Israel composed songs and works that were new and never before sung, we also can compose new Music, invent new things, and form new books. This is in respect to the union or composition of all the parts contained in the newly joined and formed book (as I have said), and not in respect to the disjunction of the parts in their singularity, which are most ancient. Just as to compose and form thousands upon thousands of sentences, however long they may be, the number of 22 letters is sufficient and enough—and we always see that the same quantity remains to be able to form and compose others—so also with 6 notes <note>The hexachord system: Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La.</note> and with 7 musical intervals, as many pieces of music as one wishes can be formed. We will always have those same elements in our hands to be able to compose others, and this is because they are musical elements. With this we shall conclude: that the compositions we make and the works we write are old in respect to the separation of all the parts, and new in respect to their union. And to those obstinate in their own authority, it suits them well to join with those who say there are not so many novelties in Music, since it is all enclosed in six brief syllables (namely): Ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la. Once joined, they can embark upon the river of forgetfulness, heading the way of the smoke <note>A metaphorical way of saying their arguments will vanish or be forgotten.</note>. It would be a long task to write all that I feel on this matter, and for that reason it will be better to remain silent than to say little of that which can only be said at great length.

---

### How this treatise and the others must be read in order to draw profit from them. Chapter VI.

<margin>Advice 1. From the life of St. Thomas, written by Juan Garro.</margin>
**I**n this following chapter, it seemed to me that it would be well to provide some advice for whoever wishes to take advantage of this present treatise and draw some fruit from it; for the reader himself must be both Master and Disciple, with the help, however, of the Sovereign Master and His most glorious Mother, the most holy Virgin. And for the beginning of a lesson, it will be very appropriate each time you take it up to first make the sign of the Holy Cross and pray one of the prayers placed at the beginning of the work; or at least you shall pray one *Hail Mary* in praise of Our Lady. For we also read of Saint Thomas Aquinas that he never set himself to study except after having offered a prayer. <margin>He who wants to draw profit from what he reads must have patience.</margin> I will endeavor to be as clear as possible; nevertheless, if there should be someone who does not fully understand my discourse, let him have patience; and that which he does not understand, let him read more times. By reading it with patience and attention, it will come of itself to reveal its meaning. <margin>Advice 2. Plutarch, Book 1, On Nourishment.</margin> For this proceeds from nothing else but that writings cannot have that force and vivacity possessed by words coming from our mouths: for it is a certain thing that, *That teaching is best which is explained by the living voice* <note>original: "Illa doctrina est optima, qua viua voce explicatur."</note>. And it is often said that he who reads the writings of others speaks with the dead; wishing to imply that those words read in books have little life, or are in the likeness of one who has no life. They say that a doctor wanted to test an arquebus <note>An early type of portable gun.</note> he had bought, and he fired at a very thick book of medicine that he had inherited from his father. Saying that he had "passed through it" <note>A pun on "passing through" a book (reading it) and a bullet passing through it.</note>, a very young son of his said: "In truth, my grandfather did not pass through it so quickly." The same (I say) can be done by one who desires to pass quickly and all at once through this book, but he will not draw profit from having passed through it; for neither did the young doctor profit by passing through his book all at once. But the old man profited very much, because he passed through it and studied it little by little. <margin>Advice 3.</margin> Thus the third piece of advice shall be that each day of study you read a single chapter, and no more, even if it be very easy and brief. I warn discreet readers of this, because one ordinarily finds very "well-read" young men who pride themselves on passing through a book, however large it may be, four, five, or more times in a week; and at the end of all this time, having drawn no profit from it, or very little, they pay the author with...

<summary>Cerone argues that musical novelty comes from the arrangement of ancient elements and provides pedagogical advice, emphasizing prayer, patience, and slow, deliberate study over rapid reading.</summary>

# Regarding Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.

### Regarding Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.

<margin>Aristotle, Book 6 of the *Topics*, chapter 2.</margin>
...false and clipped currency, saying: "He does not know what he is saying," "It is very obscure," and "He does not let himself be understood by anyone." My response: Do such people not know that *everything unfamiliar is obscure?* <note>original: "Omne inconsuetum est obscurum?"</note> Do they not notice that everything, even if easy, is very difficult to one who does not know it?

<margin>What the lesson ought to be like.</margin>
Do they not consider that, according to the saying of Cato, *to read and not to understand is to neglect?* <note>original: "Legere & non intelligere negligere est"</note> Take care, then, to go through it little by little, reading only one chapter and no more every time you take a lesson; always minding the saying of Horace: *Everything superfluous flows away from a full breast.* <note>original: "Omne super uacaneum pleno de pectore manat." This suggests that if the mind is overfilled, it cannot retain new information.</note>

<margin>In Book 1 of his *Poetics*.</margin>
Just as those who drink when they have no thirst, and eat when they have no hunger, later come to vomit even what they ate and drank with true hunger and thirst; so the greedy, often coveting the superfluous and that which is beyond their use, later find themselves unable to retain the necessary. And this lesson must not be hurried or skimmed, but very calm and attentive: applying to it not only the will to know what the author says,

<margin>Advice 4.</margin>
but much more the understanding, to comprehend what one reads. Furthermore, when you pass through certain treatises or arts of Music, take care that you do not overlook any advice regarding that which can benefit you for the amendment and correction of your compositions. Instead, always stop and linger on that which you find most tasteful and most profitable,

<margin>One must consider what is read to draw greater profit. Comparison.</margin>
even if you do not reach the end of the chapter or what you had determined to study: because a single passage deeply considered is more profitable than many passages treated at length and skimmed. For just as it is a great pleasure and a lovely thing while sailing to pass by many cities, yet stay to dwell in the best of them all; so also it is very useful and profitable while reading to pass quickly with the intention through many passages, but to stop and remain to speculate on the best of them all. For certainly, just as the bee, alighting on the flowers, works and fashions the sweet honey, while other birds draw no other profit from there but to see the color; so also some turn over the arts and minor crafts of Music for delight and pastime alone.

<margin>Profitable things must be set aside.</margin>
But you (as I said), when you find something profitable in what you read, or come across some passage that seems to you to be good and worthy of learning, set it apart and collect it, so that leaving behind the color and the scent, you remain with the sweet honey.

<margin>Advice 5.</margin>
If any difficulty should present itself to you, pray and call upon the Mother of God in your favor; for without doubt, what you cannot reach with the strength of your wit, you shall reach by means of Prayer: as we read in the life of Brother Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and polymath who was the teacher of Thomas Aquinas.</note>, which he did when he found himself embarrassed by some difficulty in his study of Philosophy.

<margin>Villegas in Life 175. Albertus Magnus, when a novice friar, was about to leave the habit; the vision that Albertus Magnus had.</margin>
Since I have touched upon these few words of this grave Doctor, to encourage myself and likewise to give daring to those of dull wit, it will be well that I tell another particular story found in his life written by Jacobus de Susato, and related by Master Alonso de Villegas in the third part of the *Flos Sanctorum* <note>A famous collection of hagiographies or "Lives of the Saints."</note>. They say of him, then, that being sixteen years old he entered the sacred Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans.</note>, in which at the beginning he was discontented and had the temptation to leave the habit; and this was because he found himself of a rude wit, and though working hard in study, he profited little. He had a certain vision one night while sleeping: it seemed to him that he placed a ladder against the wall of the monastery to leave and go away; and climbing up it, he saw at the top four venerable matrons, though one seemed the Lady over the others. Coming near them, one of them seized him and threw him down from the ladder, forbidding his exit from the monastery. He persisted in wanting to climb again, and the second matron dealt with him like the first. He wished to climb a third time, and the third matron asked him the cause of why he climbed that ladder and showed a desire to leave the monastery.

<margin>Albert's response to the third matron: and the cause why he wanted to leave the monastery.</margin>
He, with a shameful face, responded: "I am going, My Lady, because I see that others of my sort, being in it, profit in the study of Philosophy, and I labor in vain. The shame that I suffer for this cause and occasion makes me leave the religious life." The matron said to him: "That Lady whom you see there (pointing to the fourth) is the Mother of God and Queen of the heavens, of whom we three are handmaidens; commend yourself to her, for we shall pray to her to be an intercessor to her most blessed Son, so that He may give you a docile wit, so that you may profit in study." This, said Albert, "I will do gladly; I pray you, be my intercessors." He approached the Virgin and proposed his petition; the three matrons interceded for him, and the sacred Virgin asked him: "In which science do you wish to be wise? In Theology or natural Philosophy?" He responded: "Lady, that...

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Note the response that Our Lord gave to Brother Albert.</margin>
"The subject I am currently studying is Philosophy; in that, I would wish to know something, and in what follows from it." The Sovereign Lady <note>Referring to the Virgin Mary in the vision of Albertus Magnus</note> said: "What you desire shall be granted to you; what is necessary for you to do is to labor in study and accompany it with devotion and prayer." He proposed to do so, and the vision disappeared.

<margin>In the Timaeus. Lessons must be accompanied by Prayer.</margin>
From this story, it is understood that among the other qualities our study must have to be profitable, the first and most principal is that it be accompanied by Prayer and devotion, asking for help from heaven. To this, not only does this authority exhort us, but even that of the Gentiles and Pagans, such as *Plato*, who said: *In all things, both the greatest and the least, the help of God must be implored.* <note>original: "In omnibus tam maximis quam minimis debet implorari auxilium Dei."</note> And so the ancients, to say that we should implore God's help in our affairs, said proverbially: *Without divine help we are worth nothing* <note>original: "Sine ope diuina nihil valemus"</note>; from this Homer came to say in the first book of the Iliad: *Every mortal desires the help of the gods.* <note>original: "Mortalis diuum auxilium desiderat omnis."</note> And this is regarding the general; but regarding the particular, to show that before we begin our works, we should ask God for help so that He may favor us in that action, they said: *God helps those who act.* <note>original: "Dei facientes adiuuant."</note> Because of this, Virgil was moved to say in the first book of the Georgics: *In the first place, venerate the Gods* <note>original: "In primis venerare Deos"</note>; and Martial said, *At dawn I pray to the Gods* <note>original: "Luce Deos oro"</note>; which Marcus Varro usurped in his book on Agriculture, writing in this manner: *And since God helps those who act, I will first invoke the Gods.* <note>original: "Et quoniam Dei facientes adiuuant, primus inuocabo Deos."</note> Returning then to our particular purpose, I say that without doubt, with this same accompaniment, those who are of dull wit will reach the sciences they desire: for we see that this Albert being such a one, by this means came to know so much that, because of the great heights he reached in both human and divine letters, they gave him the name of "the Great" <note>Albertus Magnus</note>.

<margin>Advice 6.</margin>
Furthermore, it is fitting that one commits what he reads to memory in such a way as if he were to give an account of it to the Master: practicing within himself the substance of what he has read, not moving forward to take another new lesson until he is Master of the first.

<margin>Fable 1.</margin>
By doing this, on one hand, he will know how dangerous the swift race of the light deer is; and on the other, how secure the slow step of the heavy Elephant is. And to those who act otherwise than what was said above, I certify that they will receive nothing of profit from their studies, any more than the rooster did from the precious stone he found in the dunghill while looking for food; who would have preferred to find a grain of barley or wheat than the rich diamond: just as the first fable of the wise Aesop teaches us. Just as the aim of the author is for the reader to profit from his lesson, so it was fitting to give these second pieces of advice as well, so as not to lose the way: because by experience we see that some, after many years of study, profit little in this honorable exercise; and others are impatient, spending the whole day reading books of Music without ever stopping to speculate a little on what they read. And so to these, as to the rest, it was fitting to give these warnings, so that their labors may be more fruitful.

## Which age is the most disposed for learning; and of men already advanced in days who apply themselves to Music. Chapter VII.

<margin>Boys are easy to teach.</margin>
<margin>In Plato. St. Augustine against the Academics.</margin>
**T**he most prepared age of all there are, and the most disposed to receive what is taught, and that which gives us the most spirit and confidence to be able to teach it, is youth: because truly this age is pure, soft, pleasant, without cares, and without grief or labors. For this reason, Marsilio Ficino came to say: *It is necessary for everyone to practice his art immediately from boyhood* <note>original: "Oportet vnumquemque artem suam statim à puero exercere"</note>; The Hermit Doctor <note>St. Jerome</note> approves the same, saying: *To attain the use of the liberal arts is most difficult, unless we give ourselves to them most diligently from childhood.* <note>original: "Artium liberalium vsum assequi, difficilimum est, nisi à pueritia illis quam diligentissime operam damus"</note> To which Arts they can be set at eight years old, as custom shows us and the doctrine of Aristotle teaches us; who in the 7th book of his Politics says: *Children after the seventh year are to be handed over to the teacher.* <note>original: "Pueri post septimum annum præceptori tradendi sunt."</note> For just as quickly, and with the same labor with which children are taught and cling to what is bad, they will be taught what is good and correct. And as a rooted bad habit is difficult to uproot, so also a good habit acquired in childhood becomes natural, and is followed afterward without any labor or grief. Know then that just as when...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the education of youth, specifically focusing on the receptivity of the young mind and the suitability of music as a study.</meta>

# On Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>They remember what they learn.</margin>
When you water a garden, the water that flows through the irrigation ditch soaks into the earth through which it passes: likewise, when the Master teaches anything to a child, everything he says to him is soaked into his soul. Besides boys being very quick to learn, we must also note that what is once imprinted on them is remembered forever. <margin>In the 6th book of his *Natural Philosophy*.</margin> Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and influential philosopher/physician.</note> gives the reason and says the cause of this is that those who have a rested mind, free from the burden of labors, have a more tenacious memory; and for this reason, what boys learned in their childhood stays with them for many years, because they have no distress from thoughts and labors. <margin>Exercising the memory is profitable, and failing to exercise it is harmful.</margin> But Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas.</note> gives another reason, in my view much more effective, which is that things that cause a notable movement in a man remain more firmly in his memory, such as things that are new and wonderful. And since for boys most of the things they see and feel are very new to them and seem full of wonder, for this cause they remain firm in their memory, and they come to possess a great memory. <margin>On Moral Virtue.</margin> However, let the Masters take care to make them exercise it, so that they may profit more in virtue, memory being the storehouse of the sciences. <margin>On Benefits.</margin> *Before all else, the memory of boys must be exercised* (says Seneca) *for it is, as it were, a kind of pantry of discipline.* <note>original: "Ante omnia puerorum memoria est exercenda... ea enim est quasi quedam cellapentaria discipline."</note> And Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note> says, *Memory acquires strength and is increased by exercise; however, by idleness it languishes and is diminished.* <note>original: "Memoria exercitatione vires acquirit, & augetur: otio autem languescit & minuitur."</note> <margin>Comparison.</margin> In such a way that the boy we teach is a blank slate <term>tabula rasa: a clean slate or erased tablet</term>, where nothing is painted; or he is like a mass of wax, which is ready for us to seal as we wish. <margin>Seneca, as cited above.</margin> *Just as a seal is impressed upon soft wax, so is doctrine in the minds of boys.* <note>original: "Quemadmodum sigillum mollibus imprimitur ceris, ita & doctrina in puerorum animis."</note> By which it is seen that those who set themselves to study at this age turn out very excellent in that science in which they were employed: and if they are to make profit in any art, it seems they must make much more in Music; and this by the approval of Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero.</note>, who in the second book of *On Laws*, says <margin>Cicero, *On Laws*. Boys are quick to learn Music.</margin> *Nothing flows so easily into tender and soft minds as the various sounds of singing.* <note>original: "Nihil tam facile in animos teneros atque molles influere quam varios canendi sonos."</note> But those who have already entered manhood and set themselves to these exercises make no profit, or very little; rather, they give occasion for people to mock them. <margin>From horses to donkeys. Proverb.</margin> But what do I say of this kind of musician? I have seen many already mature men of many days leave their first profession entirely and give themselves to learning Music, to whom it often happens that they are held for fools and men of little understanding: for they put themselves at the risk of resembling that famous and excellent doctor, who (leaving aside his Galen and Avicenna) gave himself to composing verses with such misfortune that, to make fun of him, they told him that instead of a good doctor, he was studying to achieve the name of a bad poet. <margin>Good doctor, and bad poet.</margin> *Instead of an elegant doctor, you desire to be called a bad poet;* <note>original: "Pro eleganti medico, malus poeta dici cupis;"</note> this was said to Periander by Archidamus, son of Agesilaus: as written by Plutarch in the *Laconian Apophthegms*. <margin>Conditions of those who learn Music when already of age.</margin> There is never a lack of such people, from the fourth to the eighth <note>Likely referring to musical intervals or levels of skill.</note>, who set themselves in competition with perfect Singers and excellent Composers, to see if a cuckoo could get the upper hand over a nightingale in song. These latecomers ordinarily are frightened by their own skill: they listen to themselves in what they say, play, or sing with such delight of their own ears that, in comparison to them, Arion and Orpheus <note>Mythological masters of music.</note> would bray like donkeys, even when they were most in tune in their Music. <margin>Great Musician.</margin> These people very quickly sit in the Professor's Chair, where very slowly they say less than they presume, more than they know, and much more than it is fitting for them to say. <margin>Astrologer mocked by a Philosopher.</margin> Just as it is told that while an Astrologer discoursed very seriously on the movement of the celestial Spheres and the turning and circling of the stars, a Philosopher, cutting off the words in his mouth in an act of mockery, asked him how long it had been since he had come down from heaven: so, on similar occasions, one could ask these new Amphions <note>Amphion: a son of Zeus who moved stones with his lyre.</note> how long it has been since they returned from Salamanca <note>The University of Salamanca, a prestigious center of learning.</note>, and what is being done in that university. <margin>Do you come from the Academy? Proverb.</margin> These people ordinarily dare to speak of those things of which they know neither little nor much, nor can they give more reason for them than a parrot; and for this reason, they often fall into the same error as Alexander the Great; <margin>Alexander the Great rebuked by Apelles the painter.</margin> who, discoursing on painting in the house of the famous Apelles, and saying a thousand impertinences and things contrary to the art: the discreet painter whispered in his ear that he should cease that reasoning, or speak softly; because his young apprentices were laughing at him. <margin>Pliny book 35, chapter 10. In the book of the friend and the flatterer.</margin> Plutarch wants him to have done the same with Megabyzus, who was a powerful and principal man; who, being seated beside him and wanting to speak of lines, and traces, and shadows, he said to him: *Do you see these boys of mine, who are grinding these colors? They were very attentively watching you while you were silent, marveling at your purple clothing and your gold ornament: and now they laugh at you after you began to speak—* <margin>Megabyzus rebuked by the said Apelles.</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the pedagogical theory that childhood is the ideal time for learning, particularly music, due to the "blank slate" nature of the young mind. It warns against the vanity of those who attempt to master complex arts like music or painting late in life without proper foundation, using classical anecdotes to illustrate the folly of such pretension.</summary>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

speak of that which you have not learned. In the same place, one reads of a Musician who, without
<margin>Philip rebuked by a Musician with great prudence.</margin>
much ceremony, commanded the King of Macedonia, Philip—father of Alexander the Great—to be silent; for the King wished to contradict and contend with him regarding the plucking of the strings of a musical instrument. The musician stopped his mouth, saying: *May God never bring such misfortune upon you, O King, that you should know this better than I:* appropriately claiming, with good breeding, the advantage in the art without offending the King; whom he held as greater and better than one who ought to contend with a musician. Likewise, we read in the eighth book of Athenaeus that while
<margin>Ptolemy rebuked by a kithara player.</margin>
Ptolemy, King of Egypt, was disputing with Stratonicus the kithara player <note>original: "Stradonio cytharedo"</note> about playing the kithara, the player said: *A King’s business is the scepter, another’s is the plectrum.* <note>original: "Aliud est Rex sceptrum, aliud plectrum." A plectrum is a tool used to pluck strings.</note> And from this originated the proverb: *Let each man exercise himself in the art which he knows.* However, as the common proverb
sings: *He who has learned must pull the oar:* it is a fitting and much-praised thing that each one uses the arts he knows, and not those he does not. Returning to what was left aside, I say that these are the ones who dare to give advice on how he who desires to direct music well should behave. When I consider this, I realize that the Chapel Masters <term>Maestro de Capilla: the director of music in a cathedral or court</term> have more than enough reason to be annoyed with those who, having never directed a Chapel nor knowing by experience what is required, run with headlong advice and blind reasons wanting to teach them without being asked. Finally, let them remember the answer of the great Pyrrhus, King of the Epirotes; who, seeing one who had never been to war offering
<margin>Answer of King Pyrrhus.</margin>
to teach him the art of ordering an army well, replied to him: *I have no need of a Captain who has not heard the sound of the trumpet.* These same people often neglect to do what is most necessary for their houses and most profitable for themselves, only to spend the whole day among guitars, small guitars, and large guitars <note>original: "quitarras, quitarriñas, y quitarrones"</note>: passing their miserable lives like cicadas: living always hungry and barefoot; for they
<margin>Singers who live the life of cicadas.</margin>
put into practice the saying of Plautus, *A terrestrial supper:* or as Diphilus the Comic poet said, *A bloodless supper:* eating usually bread and cheese and radishes: and so that every man may understand more or less their miserable lives, sometimes they eat only two "pittances"; namely, bread and a knife: using a walk as their salad and sauce to incite the appetite. This excessive and foolish affection seems to me to be no new thing but very old, for one reads in the 6th part of the *Spanish Forest* <note>A famous 16th-century collection of anecdotes and jokes by Melchor de Santa Cruz.</note> that a certain Juan Fernandez de Heredia, hearing once a great musician of the vihuela <term>vihuela: a guitar-shaped string instrument favored by the Spanish Renaissance nobility</term> who was very poor, and for this reason wore the soles of his shoes unstitched, said to him: *By the life of him who loves you
<margin>Chapter 10 of Music.</margin>
dearly, of the many stitches <note>A pun on "puntos," which means both musical notes/points and stitches.</note> you give every day on that vihuela, give a few to those soles.*
All their intent seems to be in this exercise of Music; without taking account of
<margin>Musician mocked.</margin>
other business, and without taking the slightest grief for the hardships that befall them during the day; in the manner of that other Musician, who, understanding that his house was burning, and at the same time hearing the dissonances of the voices of the people shouting "Fire! Fire! Water! Water!"; remained so offended by the dissonance of those voices that, without
<margin>Careless musician.</margin>
taking grief for the loss of his house, he went with much care and great anguish tuning those dissonances, so that the noise of the voices might be harmonious and pleasing to the ear. To such an extreme has this kind of madness reached, that to say someone is crazy, it suffices to say he is a Musician. Indeed, it was not out of place for that page who, having
<margin>"Crazy" and "Musician" sound like the same thing.</margin>
eaten the brains of a half-head of a kid goat on the way to the table, covered his fault with such an answer: for when asked by his Lord: *How is it this head has no brains?* the rascal replied with much grace: *Know, Your Mercy, that it was a Musician.* It is true that the common folk, improperly and without further consideration, call
<margin>Conclusion.</margin>
some of these "clod-hoppers" <note>original: "çancarones," referring to unskilled or clumsy people</note> and even simple singers "Musicians": which greatly prejudices the reputation, fame, and honor of honest, virtuous, and true Musicians. It is fitting, then, that Musicians and Singers who have brains and understanding be more watchful and more discreet than those I mentioned; taking care of their houses; doing their exercises and taking up their studies at convenient hours, and not at all times. It is also fitting that the late-comers do not presume to teach the Chapel Masters, even if they are young, whenever they themselves have not exercised in practice; nor pride themselves on knowing a thing they never tried. Likewise, we shall conclude that the age most prepared for learning is that of boys, who usually learn with great ease. And to those who with their vain imaginations think they are already great Masters, when it has not even been fifteen days since they were small disciples; together with him who went to bed at night dull and ignorant and believes he woke up the next day a very skillful and great Musician, I wish to say to them finally:

->Farewell, farewell, vain dreams, remain as a mocked mockery, for you are as much as nothing.<-

<summary>Cerone uses historical anecdotes of kings and musicians to argue that music requires practical experience rather than just theoretical advice, while mocking the poverty and "madness" of obsessive, unlearned musicians.</summary>
<keywords>Philip of Macedon, Ptolemy, Pyrrhus, musical expertise, vihuela, Maestro de Capilla, musical anecdotes, poverty of musicians</keywords>

->Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies. On how docility and memory are two necessary parts for learning. Chap. VIII.<-
<sig>C 2</sig>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.
## On how docility and memory are two necessary parts for learning. Chap. VIII.

<margin>What a docile heart is.</margin>
<margin>The indocile heart.</margin>
<margin>The nature of talent, and the cause why there are such diversities of talents.</margin>
<margin>Averroes and Pontanus.</margin>
<margin>In Thras. in lib. Caduc.</margin>
**W**ho doubts that to learn quickly it is necessary to be of a docile mind and of good memory? What docility is, Speusippus says in the definitions of Plato: *Docility is the talent of the soul for perceiving quickly* <note>original: "Docilitas est animae ingenium ad velociter percipiendum"</note>. A <term>docile heart</term> <note>The author uses "heart" (coraçon) interchangeably with "mind" or "spirit" in the classical sense of the seat of understanding.</note> is one that learns with ease: a docile heart (to speak of it fully) is one that allows itself to be taught, and is a friend to counsel, and is not stubborn nor wedded to its own opinion. Conversely, an indocile heart is one that does not learn with ease, but with difficulty. *Indocility is slowness in learning*, says the aforementioned; and to say it with more clarity, I say that the indocile person is one who lacks talent: given that, *Talent is the speed of learning, or a virtue implanted in nature* <note>original: "Ingenium est discendi velocitas, vel virtus insita in natura"</note>. Here we must first know that for as many men as there are, there are as many talents; one being of more subtle talent than another: and this because of the diversity in bodily compositions, because one has blood more hot than another, and another has blood colder than the other: as can be believed from what Averroes wrote in *epistle 95*, who writes thus: *Some are of a mobile and upright talent, some of a slow and dull one: certainly one is more talented than another* <note>original: "Non nulli sunt ingenio mobili, & erecto, non nulli tardo, & hebeti: utique alius alio est ingeniosior"</note>. And Giovanni Pontano in the 4th book he wrote on Prudence, where he says: *Because of the diversity of the bodily composition, there occur so many varieties of talents; so that as many men as there are, there are also as many talents* <note>original: "Propter diversitatem concreationis corporere eveniunt tam multae ingeniorum varietates; ut quod homines, tot etiam fint ingenia"</note>. In the same book he says: *Those prevail most in talent in whose heart and blood there is greater and more agile heat: those, on the contrary, grow sluggish in whom the blood is thickened by cold and becomes very slow.*

<margin>Memory is the mother of the muses; how those who are quick to learn are of weak memory: and vice versa.</margin>
<margin>In the book on memory and reminiscence.</margin>
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
<margin>Another Comparison.</margin>
<margin>Talents to be left aside entirely because of dullness and foolishness. Proverb.</margin>
The second part that a man must have to learn quickly is that he have a good memory, for lacking it, he lacks one of the principal parts. For according to Plato: *Memory is the mother of the Muses*: whose strength Plutarch describes better, saying: *Memory is said to be the mother of the Muses, as if they suggest and demonstrate that no thing from nature can beget and nourish as much as memory* <note>original: "Memoria Musarum mater esse traditur, quasi insinuent & demonstrent, nullam à natura rem tantum gignere, ac nutrire posse, quantum memoria"</note>. Commonly those of sharp talent are quick in learning, but weak in retaining: the coarse ones, on the contrary, learn with difficulty but afterwards sustain and retain what is learned. *The same people are not gifted with memory and reminiscence* (says Aristotle) *but for the most part, those who receive slowly are more mindful, while those who receive quickly are more reminiscent* <note>original: "Non iydem sunt memoriae & reminiscentia praediti... sed plerunque; qui tarde suscipiunt, sunt magis memores, qui autem velociter, magis reminiscuntur"</note>. This happens in men (says Plutarch) as in vessels that have a very narrow mouth, through which they are very difficult to fill, and by consequence, they are also in less danger of spilling and emptying themselves: so it is with those of coarse talent; they are difficult to receive, and difficult also to let go of what is received. But those who have a good nature and are of sharp talent are like vessels that have a very wide and open mouth, which are easily filled, and easily can be spilled. Saint Thomas [Aquinas] says to this purpose, that from the diverse bodily dispositions (as has been said) come the diverse abilities in the operations of the soul: that just as we see that things which are hard are impressed or marked with difficulty, such as metal and stone; yet they sustain and maintain them much more than other things that are easily impressed, such as chalk, wax, and dough: so it happens here, that as memory is the guard of what was learned, those of coarse talent, what they were able to impress once in it, they preserve more in that hardness where it was placed with difficulty: but to the sharp and subtle, not having impressed it with such fatigue, it can fall away from them more quickly. So that one should not reject the one who is difficult to learn, provided he is not of those of dull talent, and in everything hard and rustic and foolish. Which will be easily known in his manner of dealing, because in his customs he will be very coarse, and in his speech very clumsy: and in such a way that, using the ancient refrain, it can be said to him, *You came from the house of Patroclus*: and it is that Patroclus was a coarse man, of little [wit] and a fool, who gave rise to the proverb among the learned to call such an asinine generation of persons "coming from the blood and house of Patroclus."

<summary>This page discusses the two essential faculties for learning: docility (the willingness and speed to be taught) and memory. It explains how physical temperament affects intelligence and contrasts the quick-learning but forgetful mind with the slow-learning but retentive mind.</summary>
<keywords>docility, memory, talent, Aristotle, Plutarch, Averroes, education, temperament, Muses, Patroclus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the natural aptitude required for learning, specifically leading into the study of Music.</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>A parrot that said the Creed in Latin.</margin>
Those of whom I am speaking are so many parrots, and perhaps worse than parrots. I say worse, because Ludovico Celio <note>Ludovicus Caelius Rhodiginus (1469–1525), an Italian scholar</note> says that a parrot, which belonged to Cardinal Ascanio, recited the entire Creed in Latin in his presence without missing a single word, small or large. Let us see now if it is true that there are some people more dull and more coarse than parrots. They write that Calvisius Sabinus was endowed by nature with a memory so weak that at times he forgot the name of Ulysses, at times that of Priam, and at times that of Achilles, even though he had them well in memory shortly before.
<margin>Seneca.</margin>
<margin>Cicero.</margin>
Others, treating similar subject matter, give the example of Curio the Elder, who had so little memory that sometimes, while in court, he would forget the entire case, as if he did it on purpose to mock the judges.
<margin>Pliny.</margin>
And Pliny recounts that Herodes Sophista had a son with a memory so weak that he could never retain in his head the first elements or first characters of his language; that is to say, the Latin Alphabet.

<margin>Other temperaments to be set aside due to coldness and laziness.</margin>
There is a third kind of men who, although they are not entirely crude or coarse, are nevertheless not fit for the study of letters, being by nature very cold and very diminished in their operations. It seems as if their noses are always bleeding <note>A Spanish idiom for someone lacking spirit or energy</note>; for they perform all their works with such coldness, and in all their actions they are so drowsy, that in them the proverb of Diogenianus is verified: *You sleep the sleep of Endymion* <note>original: "Endymionis somnum dormis"</note>; who was a very negligent man, lazy, and without skill; a man of little account, and indeed a sleeper in every way, who, in order to be idle and do nothing, asked Jupiter to give him a perpetual sleep.
<margin>Proverb.</margin>
<margin>Laertius, Book 2.</margin>
<margin>Aristotle, Book 10 of Ethics; Cicero, Book 5 On Ends and Book 1 Tusculan Disputations.</margin>
When the ancient authors wanted to signify the great difficulty these people have in learning, they explained it with that proverbial way of speaking, saying: *it is like trying to make an ass run with a bit in its mouth.* Horace touches upon this with much grace in these verses:
<margin>You teach an ass to run with a bit. Proverb.</margin>
<margin>Horace, Satires, Book 1, Satire 1.</margin>

> *Unhappy man, you waste your labor, as if one*
> *Should teach a little donkey to run in the field, obeying the bit.*
> <note>original: "Infelix operam perdas, ut siquis asellum / In campum doceat, parentem currere freno."</note>

Others would say: *You sow in rocks; you sow in clean sands* <note>original: "In saxis seminas: arenas mundas seminas"</note>; when one takes in hand a sterile work from which no profit or fruit is to be expected. They also said: *You teach iron to swim* <note>original: "Ferrum natare doces"</note>, when one taught something to another whose nature repelled it; to one of those, I say, to whom it could be said in capital letters: *You are blind in ear, mind, and eyes alike.* <note>original: "Tu simul & aure, & mente, & oculis cæcus es."</note> And for confirmation of the inability and wasted time, they said proverbially: *A beetle will sooner make honey, and a gnat make milk.*
<margin>On Tranquility of Mind.</margin>
Therefore, let those who are composed of such matter not waste their time, but rather strive to employ themselves according to their natural inclination. This is the advice of Plutarch, who says: *Everyone ought to use his own talent for those things for which he was made by nature, and not do violence to it by dragging it to another study of life.* <note>original: "Quilibet debet uti suo ingenio ad ea, ad quæ ipse natura factus est, neque id ad aliud vitæ studium trabendo, huic vim facere"</note> And concluding everything, I say:

<margin>See the end of Chapter 26 of this part, which is for this purpose.</margin>
->To persist against nature,<-
->Is to lack reason;<-
->To kick against the goad<-
->Is for greater misfortune.<-

->Not everything is for everyone,<-
->Let each one consider<-
->Where his capacity reaches,<-
->Which is the true way.<-

<margin>Minerva, Goddess of talents.</margin>
->Look at him who loses most sleep<-
->(If Minerva failed him)<-
->Let them not say he attended,<-
->Like the ass at the lute.<-
<note>A reference to the proverb "Asinus ad lyram," meaning someone totally unsuited for a task.</note>

->Let each one be content<-
->To have the luck he has;<-
->Not everything suits everyone<-
->Whatever he can, wants, or tries.<-

## ->Who is considered to know how to sing: and how young men should be taught Music. Chapter VIIII.<-

**T**here is no doubt that the tender age requires being preserved in the occupations of virtue, so that it is not corrupted by the air of bad habits; because otherwise, small clusters of worms are bred in it, and later they become poisonous maggots of great vices. But to avert such danger,
