# Of Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>In his Music. By command of the ancient sages, young men must learn Music, as well-born and well-disciplined children. 8. Politics.</margin>
<unclear>Prestige</unclear>, among occupations, there must be that of Music. For we know that <term>Socrates</term>, and <term>Plato</term>, and all the <term>Pythagoreans</term> (the authority for this is <term>Boethius</term> <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was a standard textbook</note>) ordained a law that all boys and girls should be taught in the art of Music, not to incite them with it toward shameful movements (by which this art is made vile) but to rule and govern such movements with the rule and prudential dictates of reason. The cause of this law was because the nature of youths is restless and desirous of delightful things, and for this reason, it does not receive grave and severe discipline. It is necessary, then, for youths to be raised in honest and moderate delights, which are powerful enough to bring about an honorable old age, and this will be caused by honest Music. <term>Aristotle</term> in the books of the Republic <note>Referring to the *Politics*</note> says: *Music is of value for the passing of time and for delight; and therefore it is fitting for boys to study Music.* <note>Original Latin: "Musica valet ad deductionem temporis, & ad delectationem; & ideo pueris conuenit studere Musicæ."</note> Where he expressly commands that boys learn Music: not that of the theaters, made to satisfy the plebeians and common people, but that which befits noble and high-born people, which is the true kind.

<margin>Mus. d planar 157.</margin>
<term>Plutarch</term> in his *Music* says that the first thing the Greeks anciently showed to their children when they were boys was Music: because by its means they came to be of a sweet spirit and full of modesty. *That ancient Greece deservedly bestowed the greatest zeal and labor upon all things so that, especially, adolescents might be educated in Music. For they thought it necessary that their soft and tender minds be composed and tempered toward modesty and moderation by Music.* <note>Original Latin: "Veterem illam Græciam studium, operamque rerum omnium merito impendisse maximam, vt in primis adolescentes Musica erudirentur. Eorum enim animos molles ac teneros ad modestiam atque moderationem Musica compositi, & temperati oportere existimabant."</note> It being true then that *Music composes soft and tender minds toward modesty,* as the sages wish, and among them <term>Plato</term>, who in his *Politics* says: *Music can create a certain character of the soul, and for this reason it should be applied to the discipline of boys, and boys should be established in it.* <note>Original Latin: "Musica potest animi morem aliquem facere, & hac de causa ad puerorum disciplinam est adhibenda, & in ea pueri sunt constituendi."</note> For this reason, then, fathers of families must ensure that their children learn honest Music, which befits noble and high-born people.

<margin>Politics 8.</margin>
<margin>Universal error committed by teachers and parents in showing children theatrical and lascivious Music. Arist. Ethics 5.</margin>
How very much the teachers err in teaching, and even more the parents in consenting to their children being taught with dishonest Music, cannot be said in few words: but let them know that when they are old, they will desire the same. But the youth raised in honest Music and delighting in it will, in old age, desire no other delight. So the philosopher affirms, saying: In those things we wish to delight in which we delighted in our youth; and the ancient and modern Latin writers are accustomed to say: *To know from the straw,* <note>Original Latin: "Ex stipula cognoscere," a proverb meaning to judge the whole harvest by a single stalk, or a man's life by his youth.</note> to say that from old age one must judge what a man was like in his youth.

<margin>We are all bound to learn Music, as the Christians we are.</margin>
Leaving aside all the precepts and moral philosophical laws, I say (as Christians that we are) that there are many causes that should awaken us, and with reason invite us to the love of Music. The most powerful and principal cause why we should know how to sing is to employ Music in the service and praises of <term>God</term>. To this the Angels incite us, who in the Church Triumphant praise God with song; and the Saints in the [Church] Militant (in imitation of the celestial) ordained that there should be song.

<margin>Music is a good means to praise God.</margin>
God does not only wish to be served by the Christian Religion with the preaching of the holy Gospel, but with praising and blessing Him, for which Music is no small or poorly chosen means, as much of voices as of well-sounding instruments, according to the understanding of man. Of this, those ancient Poets <term>Orpheus</term>, <term>Pindar</term>, and others like them are good witnesses, who in sweetly composed meters, and sung to the sound of musical instruments, praised the divinity they then believed in, as <term>Saint Augustine</term> relates in the 18th [book] of the *City of God*.

<margin>Lib. 18. c. 14.</margin>
<margin>Psalm 150.</margin>
But we have graver examples and more trustworthy testimonies in the Holy Scripture, of those who knew and adored the one and true God; and sang praises to Him with melody of voices, and of flutes, and of <term>vihuelas</term> <note>A Spanish stringed instrument similar to a lute or guitar</note>; following the doctrine of the Royal Prophet <note>King David</note> who says: *Praise the Lord with the timbrel and choir; praise him with strings and the organ. Praise him with well-sounding cymbals; praise him with cymbals of joy: let every spirit praise the Lord.* <note>Original Latin: "Laudate Dominū in tympano & choro; laudate eum in chordis, & organo. Laudate eum in cymbalis benefonantibus; laudate eum in cymbalis iubilationis: omnis spiritus laudet Dominum."</note> Praise the Lord (he says) with <term>atabales</term> <note>Kettledrums</note> and with choirs: praise him with stringed instruments and with organs. Praise him with bells of good sound, praise him with bells of joy: let every spirit praise the Lord. So did the good prophet even as a child, who while walking and grazing his father's flock, with his harp praised the Lord: and after being anointed as King of Israel, he instituted a great number of singers, who with tuned voices

<summary>This page argues for the moral necessity of music education for youth, citing classical philosophers like Plato and Aristotle, and transitions into the spiritual importance of music in Christian worship as exemplified by King David.</summary>
<keywords>music education, morality, Plato, Aristotle, King David, Saint Augustine, liturgical music, noble arts, youth discipline</keywords>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

...and with sweet and diverse instruments, they should praise the name of the Lord. Finally,
<margin>Revelation 19 and 5.</margin>
Music is so fitting for praising God that the glorious Evangelist **Saint John**, desiring to declare to us the office <note>The "office" refers to the assigned duty or spiritual work</note> of the Saints in heaven, says: *I heard voices in Heaven like the sound of mighty rivers, and like the sound of terrible thunders, and like musicians playing their harps, and they sang a new song before the throne of God and the Lamb.* Furthermore, he says, *A voice came out of a terrible thunder, saying: Give praise to our God, all you his saints: and you that fear God, little and great.* <note>original Latin: "Laudem dicite Deo nostro omnes sancti eius: & qui timetis Deum pusilli & magni."</note> All of this is described with great grace and sweetness by the Licentiate **Miguel Sanchez**, in the quatrains of a Sonnet of his, speaking in this manner:

->Whatever breast in voice high or grave, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; The Angel, who holds the greater part,<-
->Blesses the holy hand of his God &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Of this office, his praise he sings;<-
->Who formed him, by whose great mercy &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; In whose imitation there he raises<-
->He only asks for love, with which to praise Him. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; His voice, man, as best he can or knows.<-

Sacred Scripture thus affirms that the office of the Angels is to sing before the Divine Majesty: and those who hold the office of Angels should be called imitators of Saints and singers of God. Considering this alone, all Christians should strive to learn this Angelic art, to be able to sing praises to the Lord with their voices. But because nowadays people do not have such consideration, but rather affirm with the voice of the common folk that singing is an occupation for persons of little importance, in this chap-
<margin>Particularly the Clerics, friars, and all are obliged to know Music.</margin>
ter it is necessary that we speak particularly of who is bound and to whom it is fitting to know how to sing; and this shall be by Ecclesiastical precept, and not by moral laws. All Religious <note>Members of religious orders, such as monks or friars</note> are bound to know how to sing at least <term>Plainsong</term> <note>Cantollano: The traditional monophonic, unmeasured chant of the Church</note>, to be able to satisfy the obligation they have to perform the office in song on solemn feasts in the Choir: denoting the celestial rejoicing and joy that the Saints always have in the Church Triumphant.
<margin>What they owe to singing in the choir. See chapter 29 of the Ceremonies.</margin>
Those who hold a title in any Church, there is no doubt but that they are obliged to go to sing the divine office in their Churches. Many beneficiaries <note>Clergy who receive income from church property</note> consume the Ecclesiastical rents, yet they do not deign to know how to sing, as if it were not becoming of them to solemnize the feasts of the Saints with rejoicing, singing divine praises with their voices: instead, they permit laymen to enter the Choir in place of the Religious, and allow married men to perform the office of the Cleric: it only remains now to dress them in the <term>surplice</term> <note>sobrepelliz: A white liturgical vestment with wide sleeves</note>, so that these, etc.

One reads of the religious **King David**, that he loved Music for God to such a degree that he not only ordered that there be four thousand Singers, and among these three Masters of the
<margin>Book 1. chapters 23 and 25. Chronicles.</margin>
Chapel <note>original: "Paralipom." (Paralipomenon), the Latin name for the Books of Chronicles</note> (who were Asaph, Heman, and Ethan) to sing divine praises in the
<margin>Three Chapel Masters and four thousand Singers.</margin>
tabernacle of God at the time of the sacrifices: but he determined it was not a thing unworthy of Royal Majesty to sing and together dance before the Ark of the Lord: and even though he was despised by his wife Michal for employing Music for God, he
<margin>David despised by Michal his wife.</margin>
did not abandon the good work: rather with much patience and much suffering, he answered her saying: *I will play and make myself even more contemptible than I have been, and I will be humble in my own eyes.* <note>original Latin: "Et ludam & vilior fiam plus quam factus sum, & ero humilis coram oculis meis."</note>
<margin>2 Samuel, chapter 6.</margin>
Now, if a King so powerful, so wise, and so Holy did not disdain to sing and leap through the streets before the Ark; why should that beneficiary or that other Canon be ashamed to sing in the Church before the Most Holy Sacrament? Should
<margin>By singing, the Clerics do on earth what the Angels do in Heaven.</margin>
these Gentlemen not notice that the Clerics do on earth what the Angels exercise with great fervor in heaven: for perhaps if they noticed it, they would not entirely abandon this holy exercise. We already know that the form held by the Roman Church (for the most part) in the divine office is to say it in song. I do not know now how those Clerics fulfill their profession who do not know how to sing (I speak of those who are not occupied in the exercise of letters) for I hold that they are unworthy of the distributions, honor, and rent that the Clerics receive, being so negligent that they do not know how to sing divine praises. The first commandment of our Evangelical law obliges us to love God, and for the purpose of loving and serving Him was human nature created, and for the purpose of knowing Him is all science ordained. That Music is particularly necessary to the
<margin>To what end man is created.</margin>
Clerics, we have the testimony of **St. Augustine**, who speaks in this manner: *Priests are bound to know four things, otherwise the name of Priest hardly exists in them; and they are these:*
<margin>38. dist. ipsi.</margin>

<meta>catchword: Grama- (Grammar)</meta>

<summary>Cerone argues that music is a divine obligation for the clergy, citing the biblical examples of Saint John's visions and King David's devotion. He criticizes contemporary priests who neglect musical training, asserting that singing is an earthly imitation of angelic worship.</summary>
<keywords>liturgical music, King David, Saint John, ecclesiastical duty, plainsong, choir, Miguel Sanchez, Saint Augustine, clerical education</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ecclesiastical necessity of music, specifically addressing the legal and moral obligations of the clergy and friars to understand plainchant.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Which concerns Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.</header>

*Grammar, Canon Law, Computation, and Music. Grammar, for explaining the words of God; Canon Law, for discerning ecclesiastical rights; Computation <note>original: "Computus"; the calculation of the Church calendar</note>, for finding the moveable feasts; and Music, for singing the praises of God.* Thus, among the things an Ecclesiastic must necessarily know is Music, or at least simple Plainchant <term>Cantollano: the traditional unison chant of the Church, also known as Gregorian chant</term>. But to better understand who holds this obligation, let us look to the Holy Councils.

<margin>Distinction 92, final chapter. Clerics obligated to know how to sing.</margin>
Any (it says) Priest, Deacon, or any other Cleric deputed to the service of the church, in whatever place he may be where there is a church, if he does not come to said church to sing Matins and the other hours of the day; if, being punished for his fault, he does not wish to receive pardon from the Bishop with due satisfaction, let him be deposed or deprived of his Clerical status. I well know that because of that phrase, "deputed Cleric," some hold that this does not obligate any but those who hold a title in a specific church; but the opinion of Pope Innocent and the gloss <note>marginal or interlinear commentary on legal texts</note> says that it is understood to apply to any Cleric in holy orders.

<margin>Innocent III. On the celebration of the Mass, chapter "dolentes".</margin>
To the argument that says only those who are deputed are obligated, one can respond that once a person receives holy orders, he is already deputed to the service of the church. It is true that if he has a particular title to a church, he is obligated to go to it; but if he was ordained with the title of his own estate or patrimony, he is obligated to go to one of the churches he chooses, provided he is not legitimately hindered. This is limited according to custom—meaning common custom, not particular. Concluding this matter, I say that wherever I find in the Councils or decrees regarding the Divine Office <term>The official set of prayers and psalms marked for specific hours of the day</term> to which Clerics are obligated, it always commands that it be sung.

<margin>Psalm 118. Friars obligated to know how to sing. On the celebration of the Mass.</margin>
Regarding the friars, I say that by way of their vow (according to the prophet) they are obligated to say the Divine Office. Innocent III declares (concerning this) that it is in a man's hand to promise a thing to God, but once promised, he is obligated to keep it. The friars have the obligation of a solemnly sworn rule, therefore it binds them. Thus, although they do not have benefices or titles of patrimony, they are nonetheless obligated (by cause of their orders and rules) to say the Divine Office, either recited or sung, according to the solemnity of the day; unless they are reasonably hindered by obedience. And it is a certain thing that (not knowing how to sing) they will not be able to entirely satisfy the obligation they have.

<margin>Note.</margin>
Let us Ecclesiastics, then, strive to know how to sing at least Plainchant, and what we sing with our voice, let us contemplate with a simple heart; and let us see that from God our Lord we have received understanding so that we may learn the sciences and arts with which He may be served and glorified.

How fortunate are the Ecclesiastics, who hold the office of Angels: for they do on earth what the Angels exercise with great fervor in Heaven. Since men are the perpetual companions of the Angels and Saints, great must be their merit. Therefore, those who occupy themselves in this holy office of practicing Music to serve God should be highly esteemed and revered, and not despised or poorly treated.

---

# Concerning Virtue and Ignorance. Chapter X.

<margin>What virtue is.</margin>
**L**eaving aside the definition of virtue made by Aristotle in the second book of the *Ethics* regarding moral virtues, and in the *Politics*; and that which Saint Bernard makes in the treatise *To the Brothers of Mont-Dieu*; and that which Saint Augustine makes in the 15th book of *The City of God* and in that of *Free Will*, we shall state only two. The first shall be that which the aforementioned Doctor of the Church makes in the book *On the Quantity of the Soul*, where he says: *Virtue is an equality of life, which on all sides squares with reason.* And the second shall be from Giovanni Pontano in the second book of *On Prudence*, where he says: *Virtue is the good of man alone, and his alone is the possession and patrimony.*

<margin>The power of the virtues.</margin>
If the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle</note> said that virtues alone can make us content, blessed, and happy; if the Stoics wrote that virtues open the way for us to honors, fame, and great rewards; if the Platonists added that virtues alone lead us to true immortality and to the eternal rest of the Elysian Fields; how can we not seek every kind of virtue?

<summary>This page argues that all clergy and friars are legally and morally bound by church law to learn music (specifically plainchant) to perform the Divine Office. It concludes by beginning a new chapter on the philosophical definitions of virtue according to classical and Christian authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Plainchant, Canon Law, Divine Office, Clerical Education, Pope Innocent III, Virtue, Aristotle, Saint Augustine, Religious Obligation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse of Domenico Pietro Cerone’s musical treatise, shifting from the technicalities of music to a moral exhortation on the value of virtue and the dangers of ignorance.</meta>

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Praises of virtue.</margin>
That wise man said, *All things obey virtue* <note>original Latin: "Virtuti omnia parent"</note>. That other one said, *Riches are the handmaidens of virtue* <note>original Latin: "Virtutis pediffeque sunt diuitiæ"</note>. The third said, *Virtue is better than any art*. The fourth said, *Virtue alone can make us happy*. The fifth said, *Because of their virtues, men are called Gods*. The sixth said, *Virtue places mortals above the stars*. But the seventh said, *Nothing is more lovable than the highest good*. And in this manner we could go on following and saying a thousand other praises of the virtues. With all this, I cannot fail to include what Saint Augustine says of it in the book *The City of God*: *In this life (he says) one can possess nothing better than virtue*. Adding what Seneca wrote in Epistle 27, in which he says, *Virtue alone provides perpetual and secure joy*; and in the 93rd he says, *He cannot be miserable who is endowed with virtue*.

<margin>Aristotle, Ethics Book 3; Plato, Alcibiades and Sophist. What ignorance is.</margin>
Conversely, *Man becomes unjust through ignorance*; Plato says, *Ignorance is the cause of evils*. Whoever desires to know what thing ignorance may be, let him read in the definitions of the same Plato, and he will see that he says: *Ignorance is a certain dementia of the soul; which, while it strives toward the truth, itself prevaricates*.

<margin>How it is painted and its meaning.</margin>
Among the Greeks, ignorance was painted in the form of a naked boy, riding upon a donkey: he had his eyes bandaged, and a reed in his hand. With this painting they wished to signify that the ignorant person is of a simple and childlike wit; that he is naked of all good; governed by a spirit more coarse than that of a donkey; that he is totally blind of understanding, and empty in the brain; and finally that he is hollow like a reed. That all this is so, we can know from the answers that ignorant people sometimes give when asked about something.

<margin>Inappropriate answers made by the ignorant.</margin>
I say that very often they respond with terms similar to those of that other "clapper of a dried gourd" <note>A Spanish idiom for a fool or a loudmouth with an empty head.</note>, who, when a neighbor asked to borrow his sickle, replied that he had no hatchet in his house: whose inappropriate response Suidas and Zenodotus touched upon, saying; *I was asking for sickles, but they deny me hoes* <note>original Latin: "Falces petebam, at hi ligones denegant"</note>. Of such lapses Saint Jerome speaks in the *Epistle to Rufinus* where he says; *You are asked for a hand, and you stretch out a foot*; it means, one asks you for the hand, and you give him the foot. Also the proverb of Manutius speaks to one of these "pickled Ciceros" <note>A sarcastic term for someone who pretends to be eloquent or learned but is actually stagnant or foolish.</note>, and says to him; *I speak of garlic, and you answer about onions* <note>original Latin: "Ego de alijs loquor, tu respondes de capis"</note>.

<margin>Virgil, Georgics; Pliny, Book 18, Chapter 32.</margin>
The ancients, wishing to signify that one was idle and useless, and like land without fruit, said: *This one was born on the fifth moon*; he who serves for nothing else than to spend life in pleasures and always in feasts, and as the common refrain says; *He lives the life of a flute-player* <note>original Latin: "Tibicinis vitam viuit" - implying a life of wandering and performance rather than substance.</note>: and from here one comes to the knowledge that the sententious saying of Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> is more than true.

<margin>Preface to De Officiis.</margin>
*They are indeed men not in reality, but in name*. It is clearly seen that they do not know the difference that exists between virtuous men and the ignorant: nor do they know that these, in comparison to those, are worse than the dead. How many, many things there were to say about this, but to save words, and not spend many on this, I move forward in speaking of the memory that remains of them in the world after their lives: and this so that every one of noble mind may strive to flee such infamy, giving himself to the virtues.

<margin>Triumph of useless men after death. Plato in Phaedo.</margin>
Well, Plato, wishing to declare the triumphs that useless and ignorant men have after death, said that they were turned into so many donkeys. Hear how he says it; *Men who have given their hands to foul lusts, and given to the belly through sloth and lewdness, have led an inglorious and useless life, nor held any weight of shame, shall be cast down into donkeys after death*. Just as good scents make the clothes and garments in which they are kept also smell good; and bad scents make them smell bad: so every manner of living, and every life joined with virtue, is cheerful, delightful, and pleasant: and on the contrary, vice corrupts everything that seems resplendent, precious, and honest, and makes it bitter, and distasteful, and evil to those who possess it.

<margin>Fruits of Virtue.</margin>
If we wish to know the harms of ignorance, it is necessary to know the fruits of science <note>term: science/sciencia - in this period, this refers to broad "knowledge" or "learning" rather than just the physical sciences.</note>; because as many benefits as the one gives, so many harms the other gives. Since science gives honor, reputation, greatness, fame, nobility, wealth, and eminence or highness; it is to be considered that ignorance can give nothing more than affront, shame, dishonor, infamy, lowness, vileness, poverty, misery, and greater obscurities than the darkness of Egypt:

<margin>Harms of ignorance. Plato in Phaedo. Cimmerian darkness.</margin>
and what is worse, the destruction of cities. *Ignorance (says Plato) is most pernicious to cities*. The remedy and the theriac <note>term: theriac/tiryaca - a medical concoction used as an antidote to poison.</note> against this pestiferous poison is doctrine. *Against ignorance (says Plato) no remedy is found except doctrine*. Procure—

<meta>catchword: -ure (Spanish: "ren", likely from "procuren")</meta>

<summary>Cerone contrasts the nobility of virtue with the "donkey-like" nature of ignorance, using classical and patristic authorities to argue that learning is the only antidote to moral and social decay.</summary>

# Of Adornments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>No one is born with learning.</margin>
One must, therefore, place themselves under the banner of virtue, fleeing from that of ignorance. Here it is appropriate to say that no one in the world—no matter how wise, prudent, virtuous, wealthy, or powerful their father may be—is born with learning. Even the wisest and most virtuous men who have lived in the world could not leave their virtues and sciences to their children, friends, or heirs upon their death. Thus, it is necessary that whoever desires to learn any virtue must achieve it through their own sweat and purchase it with great labor. <margin>In Plato's *Protagoras*.</margin> Let them hold this as a maxim: *Virtue is neither born within us, nor does it happen by chance, but is acquired through teaching and study* <note>original: "Virtus neque nascitur, neq; casu contingit, sed doctrina ac studio cõparatur"</note>; and that *Learning naturally proceeds from man to man, according to the actions the Teacher performs toward the disciple.* <note>original: "Doctrina naturaliter procedit de homine ad hominẽ, secundũ operaciones quas habet Doctor ad discipulum."</note>

<margin>The Fable of Jason.</margin>
Those who say that while Jason was going for the Golden Fleece, a shoe fell from his foot into the sea, wish to signify to us that those who follow virtue and achieve the immortality of their name must strip themselves of corrupt affections and remain with moderate ones. <margin>Dido.</margin> Virgil meant the same when he said that Queen Dido, wishing to kill herself because of the disloyalty she felt she had committed against her husband Sychaeus, uncovered one foot; because she cast away the affection for an infamous life with the desire for death, which she held to be honorable. I say that the Poet, to praise that Queen, says she remained with one foot shod and the other bare to teach us that there are good and bad affections, and that the office of temperance is to moderate and suppress them. <margin>Achilles.</margin> Likewise, know that when the ancients feigned that Achilles could not be wounded except in the feet, because he had not washed them in the Stygian waters, and that he was wounded in the feet in such a way that they killed him: they wished to give us to understand that that valorous Captain was courageous and fortunate in matters of war; but that he allowed himself to be conquered by greed and other harmful affections, signified by the feet. I say the fiction was to warn us that no matter how courageous a man might be, he would not live in tranquility if he did not repress his evil affections and subject them to the empire of reason; which affections they understood to be represented by the feet.

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
Just as coins are worth nothing without stamps and crosses: so the scientific virtues <note>The "intellectual" or "demonstrable" virtues.</note> are worth nothing without the moral virtues, nor are these worth anything without the theological virtues, when they are to be true goods and meritorious of eternal blessings. <margin>What true virtue is. In the 3rd book of Ethics. From the Faculty of Theology 3. d. 23.</margin> But let us be careful not to confuse ourselves, thinking that by "virtues" one only understands possessing Grammar, knowing Music, etc. Rather, we must know (which is what is most relevant) that *Every duty of virtue* (as Lactantius and Boethius testify) *is not to sin* <note>original: "Omne virtutis officium... est non peccare"</note>; and this conforms to the saying of Aristotle: *It is better to die than to act against the good of virtue.* <note>original: "Melius est mori quã facere cõtra bonũ virtutis."</note> Furthermore, we must know here that the virtues are all tied together as one: and thus, he who has one, has them all; and consequently, he who lacks one, lacks them all; as it is written: *Whoever offends in one point is guilty of all* <note>original: "Qui in vno offendit, factus est omniũ reus"</note>; and St. Augustine says: *Virtues are infused together, and lost together.* <note>original: "Virtutes simul infunduntur, & simul admittuntur."</note> If one virtue is lost, all the others are lost; at the very least they remain unformed, neither meritorious nor satisfying.

<margin>What is the best thing in the world. Psalm 23. Chapter 3.</margin>
There is nothing better in the world than to do good and possess virtues; because such things come from God, who is called the LORD OF VIRTUES <note>Or "Lord of Hosts/Powers."</note>. Solomon exclaims in the 3rd chapter of Ecclesiastes: *And I knew that there is nothing better than to rejoice and do good in one's life* <note>original: "Et cognoui quod nõ est melius, nisi lætari, & facere bene in vita sua"</note>; and Saint John the Evangelist adds the best part, saying: *He who does good is of God.* <note>original: "Qui bene facit, ex Deo est."</note> If the nobleman wishes to perpetuate his fame and achieve the celebrity of his name, and leave behind an immortal memory of himself so that the succession of mortals always holds it alive before their eyes, let him strive to be virtuous, and in this way he will endure. *Virtue lives after death* <note>original: "Viuit post funera virtus"</note>; or as Ennius the poet said: *One lives through the intellect; all else belongs to death.* <note>original: "Viuitur ingenio cætera mortis erunt"</note> And Plutarch left written: <margin>In the book on the education of children. In what way man can endure. Wisdom chapter 2. Lactantius Firmianus on divine practice. Pliny chapter 7. Comparison. Only virtue is preserved by time.</margin> *Learning is the only thing among all the goods we possess that is immortal and divine.* <note>original: "Doctrina omnium, qua nobis adsunt bonorum, sola immortalis est ac diuina."</note> Otherwise, no matter how rich or noble he may be, he will not achieve it, for it is said: *We are born from nothing, and after this we shall be as though we had never been. Our name is forgotten through time, and no one will have a memory of our works.* <note>original: "Ex nihilo nati sumus... Nomen nostrum obliuionẽ accepit per tẽpus..."</note> But virtue (as has been said) never dies, but lives forever. *Virtue is perpetual without intermission, nor can it depart from him who has once received it.* <note>original: "Virtus sine intermissione est perpetua..."</note> And know that just as the lightning of fire strikes everything it finds on the earth except the laurel: so calamity overthrows everything except virtue. And even though everything comes to an end with time (which is why the ancients painted Saturn eating his children, to let us know that time, signified by Saturn, consumes everything it makes), virtue does not end because of this. For granted that time wears out everything that can be worn out by use, and goes on inventing other new things, yet the memory of notable and honorable works is so far from being worn out that time instead guards and preserves them. Whence Archimedes the Syracusan came to call time the inventor of new things,

D

<summary>This page argues that virtue and learning are not inherited but must be earned through individual effort. It uses classical allegories like Jason's shoe and Achilles' heel to illustrate the need to master one's passions to achieve immortal fame.</summary>

<margin>
Time is the inventor of the new and the register of the old.
Goddess of perpetuity.
</margin>
...new things, and a register of the old. And the Poets called Fame the daughter of the earth, and the goddess of perpetuity; for no other reason than because she walks over earthly things and makes them perpetual, delivering them to immortal memory: so that the Fame which lasts represents the life that passes. Concluding then this entire chapter (leaving the conclusion of the present matter to the three following chapters), I say with Apuleius: <note>original: "Ex bis quæ ſunt in nobis, primum bonum atque laudabile eſt virtus"</note> *Of those things which are within us, the first good and praiseworthy thing is virtue;* and thus by all it ought to be embraced, honored, and revered.

## How delight, laziness, pleasure, and riches are great enemies to virtue. Chap. XI.

<margin>
Image of madness, and president of foolishness.
In the 1st of his works.
Plautus in the Merchant.
In Anacreon.
</margin>
Delight (called by Chilon <note>One of the Seven Sages of Greece</note> the true image of madness; and by Euripides, the president of foolishness) is the cause that man remains ignorant, and it hinders him so that he knows nothing, nor has any good thing in himself: for this reason Philo the Jew writes: <note>original: "Duo alia nõ ſũt in rebus ita oppoſita, quã sũt voluptas & ſcientia"</note> *Two other things are not so opposed in matters as are pleasure and knowledge.* Marcus Tullius Cicero in the Oration for Marcus Caelius says: <note>original: "Fieri nullo modo põt vt animus libidini deditus... literis operã dare poſſit"</note> *It can in no way happen that a mind given over to lust—never unhindered by love, longing, desire, abundance, or even want—can devote effort to literature;* and in the book *On Old Age* he says: <note>original: "Diuinus Plato, eſcã malorũ voluptatem appellauit; quod ea videlicet homines capiantur, vt hamo piſces"</note> *The divine Plato called pleasure the bait of evils; because by it, clearly, men are caught as fish by a hook.* This then is one of the causes among many others why some miss the path to the habitation of virtue, as happened to Corybrantes, who drinking from the poisonous cup of pleasure before the door of Jupiter, could never find the cell of Mercury, his preceptor and Master.

<margin>
How Laziness was figured by the Egyptians.
</margin>
The ancient Egyptians, wishing to signify laziness, painted two hands hidden in the breast: because the lazy man does not want to set himself to work at anything; and perhaps they wished to figure it thus because of what Anaxagoras said; namely, that man appeared the wisest of all animals solely for having and possessing hands.

<margin>
Proverbs ch. 20.
Sensuality binds man with flatteries.
</margin>
Speaking of the lazy man, Solomon says: <note>original: "Propter frigus piger arare noluit; mendicabit ergo eſtate, & non dabitur illi"</note> *Because of the cold the sluggard would not plow; he shall therefore beg in summer, and it shall not be given to him.* Likewise he says: <note>original: "Abſcondit manũ ſuã ſub aſcella ſua & laborat, ſi ad os ſuum eam conuerterit"</note> *He hides his hand under his armpit and it wearies him if he should turn it to his mouth.* Of all this, laziness and the sensuality of the body are the cause, which with sweet flatteries holds man bound in such a way that he cannot occupy himself in studies to emerge with any value; putting him in fear of the fatigues and sweats that it is necessary to suffer to reach the virtues: as is said in the chapter that follows.

<margin>
Book of Offices.
</margin>
In such a way that, flattered and caressed, the ignorant man gives in to such tenderness, refuses to give himself to the reading of good books, and gives himself over to the power of idleness and pleasures; despising the sciences in the same way that the donkey scorns the sound of the harp or guitar. From here Marcus Tullius took occasion to say: <note>original: "Impedit conſilium voluptas rationi inimica... nec vllum habet cũ virtute commertiũ"</note> *Pleasure, the enemy of reason, hinders counsel and (so to speak) blinds the eyes of the mind, nor has it any commerce with virtue.* Finally, one can exclaim against them with that verse of Homer: <note>original: "Ab miſeri, quorum caput ignorantia texit"</note> *Ah, miserable ones, whose heads ignorance has covered.*

<margin>
Where there is plenty, there is a swelling; where there is honey, there is gall. (Proverb)
Proverbs ch. 14.
In the comedy of Amphitryon.
In the Phaedo.
There is no pleasure without pain.
</margin>
But let them enjoy themselves as much as they wish in the beginnings of this book of the will, for in the end they will turn the page, in which they will find no more written of pleasure, laughter, and joy; but rather tears, sighs, and sorrows: for misery and unhappiness are the end of all pleasures. <note>original: "Riſus dolore miſcebitur"</note> *Laughter shall be mingled with sorrow,* says Solomon: and the maxim of the Greeks said; <note>original: "Voluptatis ſoror eſt triſtitia"</note> *Sadness is the sister of pleasure.* Which was usurped by Plautus with these words: <note>original: "Ita dijs placitũ eſt, vt voluptati mæror ſubſequatur"</note> *Thus it has pleased the gods, that mourning should follow pleasure.* The same was approved by Plato, saying: <note>original: "Dolor voluptaſq; ſimul ex eodem capite connexa eſſe videntur..."</note> *Pain and pleasure seem to be connected together from the same head, and for that reason it happens that when one is present to someone, the other follows afterward.* And in another place (which is in the *Philebus*) he adds more and says: <note>original: "Ratio nobis dicat... dolores voluptatibus immiſceri"</note> *Reason tells us that in the tragedy and comedy of our whole life, as it were, sorrows are mingled with pleasures.*

<margin>
Read at the end of the chapter.
Virtue is the target of fortune.
</margin>
❦ ❦ ❦ Perhaps it may well be that such people do not want to learn, nor do they care for the sciences and virtues, because they see many learned men reduced to a bad end because of study, such as weakness of the brain; chest ailments; evident madness; the disease of consumption; and a thousand other labors of soul and body: because (as Seneca says) *Virtue is the mark and the target of all the blows of fortune:* nor was a truly virtuous man ever seen who did not suffer the tempests of this sad and disordered world. Just as when there are two walnut trees together by the road, one fruitful and the other sterile; the fruitful one is stoned by those who pass by, and the one that never gave fruit is touched by no one; rather it is pampered and very undisturbed: so in the journey of this life, the just and virtuous, burdened in their time with the fruits of good works, are often insulted and persecuted; and those sterile of virtues live contentedly. This is a thing so well known that...

<summary>Cerone discusses how sensory pleasures and laziness act as barriers to the pursuit of virtue and knowledge, citing numerous classical and biblical authorities. He concludes by noting that the virtuous often suffer more hardship than the ignorant, comparing them to a fruit-bearing tree that is pelted with stones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the moral value of labor and study versus a life of idle pleasure, transitioning into a meditation on the brevity of human existence.</meta>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

...verbally: *A bad weed does not perish,* <note>original: "Malam herbam non perire"</note> or in this other way; *A bad vessel is not broken:* <note>original: "Malū vas non frangitur"</note> used to say that a man who is gloomy and of no benefit to the republic lives on without hardships.

<margin>Considerations for those who abandon virtue out of fear.</margin>
I would rather that, just as they are frightened by what has been said, they might come to their senses through what follows, regaining their courage. Let them set their eyes upon many others who, by studying moderately, kept themselves healthy, cheerful, content, and of a most perfect complexion. Let them notice that when wise men are molested by men of the world, they make a noble resentment, mounting a horse against those who wish to rise up against them, giving them the blows of a blind man and nine-year lashings; finally throwing them (so to speak) into a filthy and stinking dungheap. To these second considerations, and not the first, those who are enemies of virtues and studies should open their eyes.

<margin>The brief life of man.</margin>
But even supposing that he who gives himself to pleasures and a lazy life lives healthier and for more years, what of it? Does he think at least to live an infinity of years more? If he believes this, he travels much deceived: he can preserve himself as much as he wants, but it will all be nothing.

<margin>On the Education of Children.</margin>
For if he pays attention to what Plutarch says, he will realize that: *The life of each person is but a point in time.* <note>original: "Vniuſcuiuſque vita eſt temporis punctum."</note> Homer compares the life of man to the falling leaves of trees: and Pindar to the dream of a shadow; not being content to call it a shadow, but a dream of a shadow.

<margin>Manilius.</margin>
In being born we die, and the end depends upon the beginning. *As soon as we are born, we immediately cease to be.* <note>original: "Nos nati, continuo deſuiuimus eſſe."</note> We, in beginning to be born, begin to die: so that our life is a prolonged death; which we do not call death, except at the end of life; but it begins (as has been said) when life begins.

<margin>Wisdom 5.</margin>
Let them notice then, that all the time one lives is taken away from the span of life; and each time less remains of what is left: so that the time of life is nothing but a race toward death.

<margin>St. Augustine.</margin>
We run then, and without knowing what we do, we go until we strike against the limits of death: and the more we grow, the more life diminishes: the more we approach being, the more we cease to be: and the more we live, the more we stop living. From which it is gathered that to live is to lose life; and to lose it is to die: and to die is to cease to be. I advise the curious reader here that Adam is not a proper name, as is commonly said, but an appellative; which derives from *Adamath*, a Hebrew word that means earth: just as *hombre* [man] is derived from *humo* [humus], a Latin word that means the same: so Adam, and *homo*, and *hombre*, are all one; and it means earth. Consider then that before you were a man, you were earth; and before you were earth, you were nothing: therefore between you and nothing, there is placed only a little earth. We are all from earth, and earth from nothing: therefore we are children of the earth, and grandchildren of nothing.

<margin>Note.</margin>
And so, to show the fragility and vileness of man and his short duration, the ancients likened him to those bubbles and swellings full of wind that waters usually make; which suddenly appear, and suddenly disappear: from here, regarding Varro, the proverb was put into practice: *Man is a bubble.* <note>original: "Homo bulla."</note> *Our life passes* (says Wisdom) *like the trace of a cloud.* <note>original: "Tranſit vita noſtra tanquam vestigium nubis."</note>

<margin>Book on Rural Affairs.</margin>
<margin>Wisdom ch. 2. James ch. 3.</margin>
And Saint James asks: *What is our life?* He answers and says: *It is a vapor; appearing for a little while and then it shall be vanished.* <note>original: "Vapor est; admodicum parens & deinceps exterminabitur."</note> Our life passes like a sign of a cloud. What is our life but a vapor, which in appearing disappears? And if by chance we want to grant it more time, we shall say that:

<margin>Eccl. ch. 14. Isa. 40. Pet. 1. Jas. 1.</margin>
*All flesh is grass:* <note>original: "Omnis caro fenum"</note> which has its being from morning until evening, and no more: being in the morning flowery, and in the evening rotten, and at all hours combated, it being that:

<margin>Job ch. 7. To the brothers in the desert.</margin>
*The life of man upon earth is a warfare.* <note>original: "Militia eſt vita hominis ſuper terram."</note> And as Saint Augustine says: *In mourning we are born, in labor we live, and in pain we die.* <note>original: "In luctu naſcimur, in labore viuimus, & in dolore morimur."</note> Now if we want to say that this life of ours is brief in respect to eternity (which is time without end) rather than in respect to temporal and limited things, it is a great deal to live fifty, sixty, and eighty years: and that in this it is a great loss to live ten or twelve years less because of the labors and fatigues there are in acquiring virtues, I will say with Plutarch:

<margin>Consolation to Apollonius. Marsilio Ficino lib. 1. epistle 9. Ethics.</margin>
*Everyone ought to think to themselves that the longest life is not the best, but that which is led with virtue:* <note>original: "Quiſque ſecum cogitare debet, non vitam longiſſimam eſſe optimā, ſed quæ cum virtute ducitur"</note> so that, *He who has not yet sufficiently perfected himself in character and virtue, though he may have lived a hundred years, has nonetheless lived little;* and the contrary; *He lives much and happily who lives for the sake of attaining eternal happiness.* <note>original: "Ille multum, ac feliciter viuit, qui æternæ felicitatis comparandæ gratia viuit."</note> Taking as a conclusion to this the saying of the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>, who wants us to give ourselves to a brief but honorable life, and not to a long one without honor, I say: *A brief life joined with honor is rather to be chosen than a long one with disgrace.* <note>original: "Vita breuis & cũ honore coniuncta potius est eligenda, quam diuturna & cum dedecore."</note>

<margin>Bede on the 1st book of Ezra.</margin>
Menander says that riches are blind, and that they blind others. It is familiar to the human heart to become dissolute...

<summary>This page explores the theme of human fragility and the brevity of life, arguing that a short life lived with virtue and study is superior to a long life of idle pleasure. It utilizes various classical and biblical authorities to illustrate that man is essentially "earth" and his life a mere "bubble" or "vapor."</summary>

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>St. Gregory on Ezekiel.</margin>
<margin>St. John Chrysostom on St. Matthew.</margin>
<margin>Riches are enemies of virtue.</margin>
<margin>Crates threw a quantity of gold and silver into the sea.</margin>
with wealth and liberty. The abundance of earthly goods is a neighbor to pride; riches are the authors of many dangers and are a school of malice. I warn, then, that beyond pleasures and delights, riches are also the capital enemies of virtues. *So much do riches and virtues differ from one another* <note>original: "Ita diuitiæ & virtutes inter se discrepant"</note> (says Plato in the 8th book of the Republic) *that, as if both were placed on the scales of a balance, they always tip in opposite directions.* That it is true that riches divert and separate man from virtue is shown to us by the example of Crates the Theban, who, throwing a great quantity of silver and a great weight of gold into the sea, said: *I would rather drown you than be drowned by you.* <note>original: "Ego vos potius mergam, quam mergar à vobis"</note> Because, in a certain way, he considered himself lost if he did not voluntarily cast away from himself that evident occasion for his ruin. And in this respect, the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Plato</note> commands in the *Timaeus* that gold be thrown into the sea; from this also Horace came to say:

->*Either into the nearest sea*<-
->*Let us send gems and stones, and useless gold,*<-
->*The material of the highest evil,*<-
->*If we truly repent of our crimes.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Vel nox in mare proximum / Gemmas & lapides, aurum & inutile, / Summi materiam mali / Mittamus, scelerum si bene pœnitet."</note>

<margin>Anaxagoras rejoices in the loss of his goods.</margin>
<margin>Lactantius Firmianus, On False Wisdom.</margin>
<margin>St. Augustine, Book 18 of The City of God, & in Book 1 against the Academics.</margin>
<margin>Lowly people hold riches in high regard.</margin>
The philosopher Anaxagoras also concurs in this same opinion, because upon hearing of the shipwreck of his goods, he said: *I would not be safe unless these things had perished.* <note>original: "Non essem ego saluus, nisi ista perijssent"</note> And it is because he considered that: *No one is rich except he who abounds in virtues.* <note>original: "Nemo diues, nisi qui virtutibus abundat"</note> He knew that: *Riches without virtues harm the wealthy.* <note>original: "Diuitiæ sine virtutibus nocent diuitibus"</note> He came to the knowledge that: *Riches lay snares for virtues.* <note>original: "Diuitiæ virtutibus insidias parant"</note> And he wished to confirm that: *Riches do not always fall to the best people.* <note>original: "Diuitiæ non semper optimis contingunt"</note> That riches are the cause of diverse and infinite evils, Ovid warns us in the first book of his *Metamorphoses* while he writes: *Wealth is dug up, the incentive for evils.* <note>original: "Effodiuntur opes irritamenta malorum"</note> Seneca does the same in the book *On the Division of Sciences*, with that sorrowful exclamation: *O happy age, which produced so many wise men, who like shining stars of the world, irradiated the world's darkness. But alas, alas, because now all serve earthly cares, all burn with the ambition for riches: Therefore it is no wonder if we see vices increase.* <note>original Latin: "O felix ætas... Quare mirum non est, si vitia crebescere videmus."</note> So it is only among persons of lowly spirit, given to pleasures and pastimes, that:

->*Joyful is the passion*<-
->*For goods and wealth,*<-
->*It holds everyone's esteem:*<-
->*And wretched poverty*<-
->*Lacks any regard,*<-

<margin>Psalm 75.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes, chapter 5.</margin>
however prudent, wise, and learned it may be. These wretched people do not consider that a virtuous man is always rich with eternal riches, as was said in the preceding chapter; but another who is not, no matter how many treasures he has, they end for him upon closing his eyes. *All the men of riches found nothing in their hands.* <note>original: "Nihil inuenerunt omnes viri diuitiarum in manibus suis"</note> The reason is because: *As a man came forth naked from his mother's womb, so he returns, and takes nothing with him from his labor.* <note>original: "Sicut homo egressus est nudus..."</note> So his name and fame last only while he lives, which in an instant vanishes like smoke and melts like snow. Speaking of such people, Saint Job said:

<margin>Chapter 21.</margin>
<margin>See ahead in chapter 59.</margin>
<margin>On True Worship.</margin>
<margin>On Tranquility.</margin>
<margin>Seneca, epistle 93.</margin>
<margin>Fortune can take back what she gives, and no more.</margin>
> *They take up the timbrel and the harp, and rejoice at the sound of the organ. They spend their days in wealth, and in a moment they go down to hell.* <note>original: "Tenent tympanum & cytharam..."</note> Besides the great difference there is in value, there is also a difference in their duration; because the riches and money of the world are in the power of Fortune: and truly so, since those who are Lords in the morning are servants in the evening: as we shall say in the chapter on Nobility. But over virtue, she has no power at all; neither she, nor death, nor man, nor anything else. Hear Lactantius Firmianus: *The principle of virtue consists chiefly in acquiring those things which neither any man nor death itself can take from us.* <note>original: "Virtutis ratio præcipue consistit..."</note> Regarding Fortune, Seneca says: *Fortune snatches away nothing except what she gave; but she did not give virtue, therefore she cannot snatch it away.* <note>original: "Nihil eripit fortuna..."</note> And the harm she can do to it is only as much as the darkness can do to the Sun: Hear the aforementioned in epistle 93: *Calamity, loss, and injury can do against virtue only what a mist can do against the Sun.* <note>original: "Calamitas, damna & iniuria..."</note> Hold it as a certain thing that ordinarily riches are lost because of our sins and not because fortune so willed it, as men of the world are ordinarily accustomed to attest. This truth was known by the wise Chrysippus, who says: *How falsely they accuse...*

<summary>This page argues that material wealth is an obstacle to virtue, citing classical philosophers like Plato, Crates, and Seneca alongside biblical figures to emphasize that true riches are eternal and immune to the whims of Fortune.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cerone’s moralizing discourse on the value of education and virtue, specifically contrasting intellectual pursuit with the vanity of the wealthy. It follows a discussion on the symbolic "golden apples" of knowledge.</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Softness.</margin>
...to prevent anyone from gathering them; and this is the temptation of deceitful softness and pestiferous vanity, with which the devil works to deceive us to impede our reaching these three things, signified by these three golden apples. To attain them, it is necessary to conquer this serpent and crush it underfoot, triumphing over it with great firmness and constancy.

<margin>Who is a "golden sheep."</margin>
Bad habits have advanced so far that nowadays rich men do not want their children to break their heads learning sciences or liberal arts; they are almost ashamed if they know how to read. Because of this, one finds more than ten of these "golden sheep"—I mean those rich in property but poor in science—who at every moment enter the shops of merchants and apothecaries and resort to the favor of young servant boys to have their letters written to send to their friends, discovering <note>In this context, "discovering" means revealing or exposing.</note> at one and the same time their secrets and their ignorance.

<margin>Book 3.</margin>
What greater ignorance can there be in the world than the example of that man of whom Stefano Guazzo writes in his *Civil Conversation*? <note>A famous 16th-century conduct book on social manners.</note>

<margin>Example of ignorance.</margin>
He says, then, that being one day in the study of a lawyer, he saw a clerk who, after having closed a letter he had written in the name of a rich man present there, asked him for the name of the person to whom it should be sent to write the inscription. The gentleman replied that it was not necessary to write anything more than: **To my kinsman in Cremona.** But when the secretary repeated that it was necessarily required to specify both the proper name and the surname so that this kinsman could be found, he replied again that he should only write **To my kinsman in Cremona**, and that writing so many words was superfluous, since all the people of Cremona knew him. But alas, not only this art of which we treat <note>Music and letters.</note>, but all the other liberal arts are reviled in the houses of the rich and lords, and held as infamous beasts.

<margin>Virtue reviled by Lords and rich people.</margin>
And when the Poets feigned that Hercules, wearing the **CROCOTON** <note>original: "crocoton" — a saffron-colored, costly garment associated with luxury or femininity in antiquity.</note> (which was a rich and pompous dress), served by spinning wool in the chamber of the Queen of the Lydians, they secretly wished to deplore (which is to weep much with lamentation) the misery of virtue, signified by Hercules. Virtue, instead of being honored and cherished in the houses of the great, is there often forced to serve with dishonor and do things unworthy of her, occupying herself in low and vile exercises.

<margin>Book 5, Politics.</margin>
<margin>Virtue and nobility are found in few.</margin>
This is not said because all nobles and rich people generally abhor and revile it, but to show that it is mistreated by the majority; for there are always some virtuous nobles and rich men, but very few; for as the prince of philosophers <note>Aristotle.</note> says: *Nobility and virtue are found in few.* <note>original: "Nobilitas & virtus in paucis inueniuntur."</note>

<margin>Battista Egnazio in the life of this Emperor.</margin>
<margin>A species of virtue is to love the virtuous.</margin>
And others honored the virtuous and virtue itself very much, as we read of the Emperor Sigismund; who, being rebuked by his courtiers because he honored learned men too much, replied: *I love those whom I see excel others in virtues and learning, by which I measure nobility.* <note>original: "Ego eos amo, quos virtutibus & doctrina, ex quibus nobilitatem metior, cæteros antecellere video."</note> With which answer he showed himself a virtuous Prince, since loving the virtuous is a species of virtue.

<margin>Antonio Panormitano in his chronicle.</margin>
Don Alfonso, King of Naples, gave clear signs of knowing nobility and its value like a true Prince; for being asked what thing there was in the world that could impoverish him, he replied (according to Antonio Panormitano): *Knowledge, if it were for sale*; because he would give for it everything he possessed.

<margin>Value of the virtues.</margin>
With this answer he affirmed the saying of Plato, who in the 5th book of *Laws* writes: *All the gold that is above the earth or under the earth cannot rightly be compared to virtue.* <note>original: "Totum aurum quod est super terram vel sub terra, virtuti iure comparare non potest."</note> All the gold (he says) that is on the earth or under the earth is not to be compared with virtue.

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
And know that just as a gold coin on the ground is worth more than a plate of copper placed upon the head, so a poor and dejected virtuous man is worth more than a rich and exalted ignorant one.

<margin>Virtue is the nail to hold the wheel of fortune steady.</margin>
Whether riches are to be preferred to virtues or not is clearly shown to us by the answer of Lagydes the Pythagorean, who, being asked this, replied that riches were at the top of the wheel of Fortune, but that virtue was the nail to hold it firm so it does not turn downward; and for this reason, honor belonged to virtue; and as the other said: *Riches are the handmaidens of virtue.* <note>original: "Virtus pedisseque sunt diuitiæ."</note>

<margin>Notable saying of Pius II.</margin>
It will not be out of reason to put here for this purpose the saying of Aeneas Sylvius (who was Pope Pius II). This great Pontiff used to say that common men should hold letters as silver, nobles as gold, and Princes as precious stones. And for this, it is necessary they strive to banish from themselves the de—

<summary>Cerone argues that true nobility is found in virtue and learning rather than wealth, using historical anecdotes of kings and philosophers to shame the "ignorant rich" who despise the liberal arts.</summary>
<keywords>virtue vs wealth, liberal arts, education, nobility, Hercules, Emperor Sigismund, King Alfonso of Naples, Plato, Pope Pius II, social status</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on moral philosophy and the vanity of worldly pleasures, transitioning into a historical-mythological genealogy of Hercules and other figures to illustrate how names and deeds are often conflated over time.</meta>

# Concerning Adornments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>Conclusion of Epistle 52. Giovanni Pontano on Obedience, Book 1. Lactantius Firmianus in the book on Divine Institutes & in the book on True Worship.</margin>
...excessive delights and pleasures, and that life so lazy and idle; like so many infernal monsters; and as our particular enemies, and enemies of the virtues together. **Pleasures especially (says Seneca) must be torn out, and held in hatred like thieves: for they embrace us only to strangle us.** <note>original: "Voluptates præcipue... euellere oportet, & tanquã latrones odio habere:nos enim amplectuntur ut strangulent."</note> Remembering that: **Nothing is so slavish and abject as to be devoted to pleasures**: <note>original: "Nihil est tam seruile & obiectum, quam voluptatibus deditum esse"</note> considering that, **An excessive craving for pleasure brings both danger and generates madness**: <note>original: "Nimia voluptatis appetentia, & periculum parit & insaniam generat"</note> finally keeping forever in the memory that; **Just as eternal life is found in virtue, so death is found in pleasure.** <note>original: "Sicut vita perpetua est in virtute, ita mors est in voluptate."</note>

<margin>Epistle of Girona, Book 2. Hercules is a title and not a proper name. How many Hercules there have been. Book 5. Aeneid 3. The glory of all 43 Hercules was attributed to one alone. Libyan Hercules was King of Spain. Hispalus built Seville.</margin>
For the greater fulfillment of what is being written (since mention was made of Hercules above), it is worth knowing that this name of Hercules (as the Bishop of Girona <note>referring to Joan Margarit i Pau</note> points out) is not a proper name as some think, but a title that means "Strong Man"; the same is said by Marcus Varro and Eusebius in the *Chronicon* <note>literally "book of times"</note>; and they indicate that there were 43 who held such a name. And because of the Poets—it being a custom used among them, when there are two, three, or more of one name, to attribute to one alone what they all did—thus the deeds of all the Hercules are attributed to one. From this it follows that the Greek writers (as Sabellicus points out), being excessively inclined to praise their own nation, wish to accumulate the glory of the deeds of all the Hercules into that one alone who, by being born in Corinth and raised in Thebes, was called the Theban. And so they not only attribute to him the deeds of the succeeding Hercules, but even of the first Hercules, called the Libyan: a title derived from his proper name, which is Lybius; who was King of Spain and father of Hispalus, who built the so celebrated and illustrious city of Seville. He was also the father of Tuscus, from whom the province of Tuscany in Italy was named; and who finally ended his life on the island of Cadiz, whose inhabitants built a magnificent temple in his honor; and having placed his ashes in it (according to what Berosus and Alexander Scultetus say), they gave him divine honors. This Libyan Hercules and King of Spain preceded the Theban by more than two hundred years; for the latter was present at the destruction of Troy the first time the Greeks destroyed it, and the former lived 195 years before Troy was even founded. And so that his antiquity may be understood, know that he was the son of Osiris, also called Jupiter, who was the son of Ham, that wicked son of Noah: from which it is seen that the Libyan Hercules was the great-grandson of Noah: and Moses makes mention of him in chapter 10 <note>of Genesis</note>, calling him Lehabim.

<margin>Josephus the Hebrew, Book 1 of Antiquities, chapter 12. Libyan Hercules is Lehabim, great-grandson of Noah. How many Saturns and Jupiters there have been.</margin>
The same is true of the Saturns, whose works are recounted as those of a single man; notwithstanding that those named by this name were many and in diverse times. Also, a single Jupiter is honored for the deeds of many who held this name, who reached the number of 30, as Tertullian says in the *Apology*, and he cites Marcus Varro for this, who affirms it.

---

<margin>Note.</margin>
❧ ❧ And so we read that the Gentiles in the temple of the goddess Volupia, goddess of delight, to whom they attributed joy, kept the statue of Angerona, goddess of agony, to whom they attributed sadness; who had her mouth closed with a padlock and sealed with a seal. That having the seal and the padlock on the mouth is to signify that he who hides his pain and his passion, and places silence upon the injuries received, will come to have contentment through the benefit of patience, and sorrow will be converted into pleasure for him. But applying the meaning to our purpose, it can be said that the reason why the statue of Angerona was in the temple of the goddess Volupia where her feast was held was to signify to those Gentile philosophers that sadness (as has been said) goes mixed with joy; and joy with sadness. In such a way that there is no Volupia without Angerona, that is, there is no pleasure without sorrow; they go entwined one with another: joy with sadness, labor with rest, tranquility with restlessness, poverty with wealth, peace with discord, ignominy with honor, sweet with sour, gall with honey, evil with a little bit of good. Thus the whole world goes sorted and mixed, without there being in it pure contentment; and with this I say again: **Reason tells us that, in the tragedy and comedy of all life, as it were, pains are mingled with pleasures**: <note>original: "Ratio nobis dicat, in totius vitæ quadam veluti tragedia atque comedia, dolores voluptatibus immisceri"</note> and I conclude with the proverb; **Where there is a teat, there is a swelling; where there is honey, there is gall.** <note>original: "Vbi uber, ibi tuber; ubi mel, ibi fel." A Latin proverb meaning that every advantage has its disadvantage.</note> above ❧ ❧ ❧

<summary>This page discusses the dangers of worldly pleasure and provides a genealogical history of Hercules, arguing that the legendary figure was actually a composite of many men, including a great-grandson of Noah who ruled Spain. It concludes with a philosophical reflection on the inseparable nature of joy and sorrow, using the Roman goddesses Volupia and Angerona as metaphors.</summary>

<header>From the Music of Cerone, Book I.</header>

### To the faint-hearted and those of little spirit. Chapter XII.

<margin>Goes to Genesis <note>Likely a biblical cross-reference to the nature of labor.</note></margin>
<margin>What causes fear at the beginning.</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 26.</margin>
<margin>More timid than Pisander <note>A Greek proverbial figure known for cowardice.</note></margin>
<margin>To the bold and deliberate, everything becomes easy.</margin>
**S**o true is the sentence of Euripides, who said that labor is the father of good fame (just as, according to Saint John Chrysostom in the 14th Homily, idleness is the teacher of all malice) that to wish to contradict it would be like wishing to blind the Sun. The fear that a youth has at the beginning of learning Music or any other science is no small impediment, when he hears it said: that he must be subject to his Master for many years; that he must frequently cut short the thread of his sweetest sleep; that he must receive blows <note>original: "mocecones," referring to physical discipline common in historical apprenticeships</note> and hear reprimands in the presence of those who know him, and sometimes without deserving it; that he must obey his Master in the same manner as if he were his servant and subject; and finally, that he must deprive himself of many contents, pleasures, and many recreations, only to set himself to suffer labors, sweats, and sleepless nights, besides other vexations. None of these seem like a small grain to him, but rather a very large mountain, higher than Mount Olympus, and much more dangerous to cross than the forest of the Sierra Morena. For he says: *The lion is in the way, and the lioness is in the paths* <note>original Latin: "Leo est in via, & leana in itineribus;" a reference to Proverbs 26:13</note>; and for this reason he pulls back his hands and stays still, and wishes to do nothing. The whole path seems to him to be full of lions and tigers, and he thinks it is impossible for him to pass through there. Frightened by these considerations, the faint-hearted man not only harms himself, but even scares many others with his vain and foolish fears. And when one fears any small sign, we can say: *He fears even the flies flying by* <note>original Latin: "Muscas metuit praeter volitantes"</note>; or perhaps more to the point would be to say: *He fears his own shadow* <note>original Latin: "Umbram suam metuit"</note>. And because the faint-hearted and those of base spirit do nothing that partakes of the difficult or the dangerous—instead fleeing dangers and difficulties—they are content to remain always in the same state, and not rise to a higher degree. From this comes the ancient saying: *The timid never erected a trophy* <note>original Latin: "Timidi nunquam statuerunt tropheum"</note>. O foolish youth of little understanding, those who out of faint-hearted fears do not learn the sciences and liberal arts! They are truly like children who are frightened by the noise of a piece of paper, and flee and hide under their mothers' cloaks. Let them know that the labors to be endured are not as many as they imagine them to be, nor are they insufferable to one who wishes to receive them in his breast, because: *Nothing is difficult to the willing* <note>original Latin: "Nihil difficile volenti"</note>; to one who has the will, nothing is difficult.

<margin>There is more glory where there is more labor.</margin>
We well know that the virtues are placed upon the highest summit, and that to have them it is necessary to use great diligence and much fatigue; for this they say: *In the path of virtue there is no [easy] way* <note>original Latin: "In via virtuti nulla est via"</note>. But who can deny that they are of greater merit, greater glory, and greater honor, the more they are achieved with labors and with sweats? And so one wrote learnedly:

> ->*The Gods have decreed that virtue must be won by sweat.*<-
> <note>original Latin: "Virtutem posuere Dij sudore parandam"</note>

The other also, with much perfection and great judgment, added the merit, saying it all together:

> ->*It is difficult, I confess, for virtue tends toward arduous things:*<-
> ->*But the grace of such merit will be greater.*<-
> <note>original Latin: "Difficilis est fateor, nam tendit in ardua virtus: Sed talis meriti gratia maior erit."</note>

<margin>Ethics, Book 9.</margin>
Aristotle says: *That which is acquired with greater labor is more dearly loved.* <note>original Latin: "Quod maiori labore acquiritur, magis diligitur."</note>

<margin>The reason why virtue is attained with labor and sweat.</margin>
<margin>Comparison from "On Curing the Affections of the Soul." <note>A work by Galen.</note></margin>
<margin>In Against [the Vices]. Vices are reached with ease, and virtues with difficulty.</margin>
It is true that study ordinarily brings with it a few setbacks; and this is so that he who rises through it to dignities and to gain fame may be deservedly honored. A Doctor says that God disposed us in such a way that without labor we cannot be wise: and note that just as those who go to war, not all who fight conquer, but no one can conquer without fighting; so not all who labor are virtuous, but no one is virtuous without labor, and very much labor. For as Galen says: *The road that leads to virtue is laborious.* And Giovanni Pontano says: *Men are born to acquire virtue and to cultivate their minds; but no one can achieve this without many labors.* Hesiod the poet shows that vices are very easy to reach, saying:

> ->*It is altogether easy for anyone to reach vice:*<-
> ->*The path to it is short, and it dwells in a nearby seat.*<-
> <note>original Latin: "Omnino vitium facile est contingere cuiuis: Est via ad id brevis, & vicina in sede moratur."</note>

And virtues are very difficult. On the contrary, the divine ones have hedged virtue all around with sweats, and the path to it is long and through arduous places.

<summary>Cerone encourages young music students to overcome their fears of the rigorous discipline and hard work required to master the art. He uses classical and biblical authorities to argue that true virtue and skill can only be attained through significant effort and perseverance.</summary>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>Proverb. Virtue as she was depicted. Atlas was a King of Macedonia, who is said to have discovered Astronomy, and for this reason the poets feign him to be a great giant who sustains the heavens upon his shoulders. Virtues cannot be achieved without sweat and labor. Comparison. Proverb.</margin>

> It is harsh at first: but when as victor you have touched
> the high summits, it becomes easy, though it was arduous before.
<note>original Latin: "Asper & est primum: sed ubi alta cacumina victor / Contigeris, iam fit facilis, licet ardua dudum."</note>

From this, the wise came to say: *Difficult things are beautiful* <note>original Latin: "Difficilia quæ pulchra."</note>. And for this reason, the ancient Egyptians depicted Virtue with eyes and hands, to give us to understand that she is conquered through study and through works. Furthermore, they represented her with clothes covered in dust and with a flushed face, to signify the fatigues and the sweats by means of which she guides her followers to the possession of honor; these followers hold everything as nothing when they think they are to be rewarded with immortal gifts. Just as the daring Atlas, solely to gain glory, sustained the heavens upon his shoulders without feeling the insupportable weight: so the youth who is inclined toward knowledge, solely to gain honor, sustains every kind of labor with joy. One can do no less than pass through the path of sweats and vigils if one wishes to enter the palace of public Fame, to light one's torch at the flame of the immortal fire of the virtues. For just as those who wish to light a fire with green wood must have patience and wait until the wood gradually dries and parches—and with all this, it is necessary to be there blowing and poking, and even shedding many tears from the smoke, if one wishes to enjoy the desired flame—so too it is fitting that the new disciple labor and persevere at the beginning of his study, if he wishes in the end to enjoy the shining and clear flame of worldly immortality. Whoever does this will trade with great profit; for he will give a thing of little value, which is labor, and will receive another of great value, which is virtue; and in truth it could be said, *This is the exchange of the arms of Glaucus with the arms of Diomedes* <note>A classical reference to an unequal trade; in the Iliad, Glaucus traded his gold armor for Diomedes' bronze armor.</note>.

<margin>Where there is no difficulty, there is no virtue. Aristotle, Book 2 of the Ethics.</margin>

By this, those who have a good desire to learn this and the other arts and exercises will understand that good desires alone are not enough to reach the virtues unless they are accompanied by a great fortitude of spirit to overcome the difficulties that present themselves in the execution of those good desires. Because it is a known thing that *Art and virtue are always occupied with difficult things* <note>original Latin: "Ars & virtus semper in difficilibus versatur"</note>: all virtues are surrounded and accompanied by difficulty; for where there is no difficulty, there is no virtue; and where there is more difficulty and more labor, there is more honor and more glory, as has been said. It is necessary, then, that the youth who began to study in this virtuous exercise of <term>Music</term> <note>The author treats music not just as sound, but as a rigorous mathematical and moral discipline.</note> (or in any other) persevere with patience through some labor: and when it is for no other better end, let it be at least to avoid being in idleness: the root of all evils, and the gate to every vice.

---

## Concerning Idleness. Chapter XIII.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus chapter 33. Homily 14. Genesis. In the book "Remedies for Love." Idleness has taught much malice.</margin>

**B**ecause at the conclusion of the previous chapter, mention was made of idleness, saying in general that it is the root of all vices and evils; it is well that now, with this occasion that offers itself to us, we speak in particular of the damages that occur because of it; and this to the end that everyone may give themselves to virtue with greater daring and greater strength. To begin this, we shall say that idleness (according to Ecclesiasticus) teaches much malice: according to Saint John Chrysostom, it is the teacher of all evils. Saint Jerome called it the rust of the intellect: and Saint Bernard, the sinkhole of all evils. How much idleness is the teacher of all evil and the fountain of all abomination is shown by Ovid, while he recounts the occasion by which Aegisthus became an adulterer, saying that the principal cause was because *He was slothful* <note>original Latin: "Desidiosus erat"</note>: where to this purpose he says,

> If you take away idleness, Cupid's bows perish,
> And his torches lie despised and without light.
<note>original Latin: "Otia si tollas, periere Cupidinis arcus, / Contemptæque iacent, & sine luce faces."</note>

From this, Lucian in one of his dialogues came to feign with much grace that the goddess Venus cruelly reprimands her son Cupid, because with his arrow or bolt of love he has not wounded Pallas (called by another name Minerva), feigned by the poets as the goddess of wisdom. To whom he responded in his defense that he never found her in idleness, but always occupied in some virtuous action: wanting to infer that idleness (as has been said) by itself causes all evils. Plato says that *Idle men, in whatever city they may be, [destroy] it...*
