ness (he says) is the death and burial of a living man. Cato the Censor used to say: *That he was never less idle than when he was idle: nor less alone than when alone.* <note>original: "Nunquam se minus otiosum esse, quam cum otiosus: nec minus solum, quam solus."</note> A reasoning worthy of a man as singular as he was, with which he declares that in leisure he also thought of business; and in solitude, he was used to speaking with himself. they seek things to pass the time: in short, they do not pass it, but it passes through them. This

flies with a bodkin: and he preferred to make the flies perish in labor than to let his wit perish in idleness: and if by chance the Emperor deserved some rebuke in this, he did not deserve it so much for the exercise he did, as because he withdrew from business and from things suitable to his greatness and to the Imperial Majesty.

exercises: for just as good occupation humbles and moderates disturbances, and awakens the soul to high enterprises, so idleness allows the appetites to be taught and is the awakener of disordered desires; for according to the proverb: *Study generates study, laziness generates laziness* <note>original: "Studium generat ſtudium, ignavia ignaviam"</note>. Let the young man work to acquire virtues, and let him not be terrified by the little fatigue there is in reaching them; for fearing the little, he will by necessity come to feel the much: because it is an experienced thing that, *He who fears the frost, the snow shall fall upon him* <note>original: "Qui timet pruinam, irruet ſuper eum nix."</note>. And it is that he who sows in youth by working, reaps it afterward in the course of his life, resting and enjoying the virtues he possesses: but when one stands with his hand on his belt <note>An idiom for being idle or doing nothing.</note>, in a manner it can be said, *He sits by the Eurotas; he sits with folded hands; he always has his hand under his cloak* <note>original: "Sedet ad Eurotam; Compreſsis manibus ſedet; ſemper manum habet ſub palio." These are classical metaphors for extreme laziness.</note>: such a one, I say, gathers the fruits of idleness, which are a thousand shames and misfortunes. So that, *He who gathers in the harvest is a wise son; but he who sleeps in summer is a son of confusion: and he who sows iniquity shall reap evils* <note>original: "Qui congregat in meſſe, filius ſapiens eſt; qui autem ſtertit aſtate, filius confuſsionis: & qui ſeminat iniquitatem, metet mala."</note>. Let us conclude then this matter and say, that no disciple who wishes to sit at the school of virtues, particularly at Music, should lose heart, nor fail, nor have fear, nor be frightened by the labors found therein, because they are not as many as he goes imagining; and also because the few that there are, later turn into rest and pleasures. For just as those who learn letters and painting at first have much pain, labor, and difficulty; however, moving forward little by little they come to know it more, and becoming accustomed to it, everything becomes pleasant and easy for them, and good to say and do: so also Music and the knowledge of the other arts and sciences, through negligence and carelessness, have an unaccustomed heaviness at the beginning in works and words: but it is not fitting for that reason to go outside and leave it at the beginning through fear, dread, and cowardice; but rather to try and experience everything, and persevere in it with the greed to always move further ahead; for this is what makes sweet and pleasant everything that is good and honest, and that we accustom ourselves to it. Not without cause did Solomon say: *It is bad, it is bad, says every buyer; and when he has departed, then he shall boast* <note>original: "Malum eſt, malum eſt dicit omnis emptor; & cum receſſerit, tunc gloriabitur." From Proverbs 20:14.</note>. It is expensive, it is expensive, says the buyer; but after he has the merchandise in his hands, he goes about boasting. Well, so it happens every day to young men in this exercise, that as at the beginning they do not know the quality of this merchandise because they are without discretion and without judgment, and they feel what is asked of them for it: being lazy, what is asked of them seems very expensive for what they are given.

But after they begin to taste how sweet (so to speak) Music is, then they boast in their merchandise, and know that for no price is such an honorable exercise expensive: with which they come to verify the proverb that says, *Past labors are pleasant* <note>original: "Acti labores iucundi ſunt."</note>. And even though study may be very harmful to many, and be a silent file that goes gnawing away their complexion <note>In early modern medicine, "complexion" refers to the balance of bodily humors and overall health.</note> little by little without being felt, with all this I take it as understood, that studies taken with temperance and pleasure, and in the manner we shall say in chapter 19, where licit and honest leisure is treated, are not laborious nor harmful: and that those who go damaging their complexion with study do not enjoy it; and if they do enjoy it, they do not know how to share it as is fitting, which is with order and measure. Because for those who take it with temperance, it is that which rests and strengthens them, being that, *He is affected with joy, who in learning and contemplating is delighted by intelligence itself* <note>original: "Gaudio afficitur ille, qui diſcendo & contemplando ipſa intelligentia delectatur"</note>, says Plato: and Aristotle says, *To learn something is delightful* <note>original: "Addiſcere aliquid delectabile eſt."</note>. Rather, according to the opinion of Marsilio Ficino, one cannot make profit in a science if he does not have pleasure in studying it. *No one (he says) can ever either invent or learn any art, unless the investigations are excited by delight and the desire for discovery* <note>original: "Nemo vnquam aliquam artem aut inuenire, aut diſcere poteſt, niſi inueſtigationes oblectatione, & inueniendi deſiderio excitentur"</note>; for according to the Philosopher <note>A common historical shorthand for Aristotle.</note>, *Delight perfects the operation* <note>original: "Delectatio perficit operationem."</note>. Knowledge is pleasant, the path is laborious: whoever wants the one, let him not refuse the other; although in truth voluntary labors are not labors: and this has been confirmed by Democritus, who said that fatigues voluntarily taken make them not be fatigues.

### *Regarding those who lack confidence, and those who do not persevere in their studies. Chapter XIIII.*

<margin>Lack of confidence and presumption usually attack whoever begins to study.</margin>
**T**wo opinions, contrary to one another, often attack the person who desires to know this art, or any other. The first is lack of confidence, and the other is presumption. Lack of confidence usually causes many students to lose heart, making them believe that it is impossible to reach a level that would bring them honor or profit. These students, cooled by this lack of confidence, abandon the study they have begun, and completely banish from themselves that art which they previously held in high regard and loved excessively. Therefore, so that no one loses heart, I wish in this present chapter to provide some examples, sayings, and maxims, which will serve as spurs to quicken the pace and move further ahead. For this, it is very important to note what Plato says: that art, nature, and continuous study are necessary to make one distinguished in their profession. This <margin>This was treated in chapter 8 of the present book.</margin> was later approved by Theognis <note>original: "Theoñides"</note> saying *Nature makes one capable, art makes it easy, and use makes one powerful.* <note>original: "Natura facit habilem, ars vero facilem, ususq; potentem."</note> Nature itself gives ability to man, but art gives ease, and use gives power—that is to say, practice and speed. Do you wish, then, to entirely overcome lack of confidence? Consider that the great Babylon was not built all at once (as the saying goes), but at many different times and over a long period. You too, with diverse and continuous lessons and over a long time, will come to know something more than what you are currently imagining; <margin>One learns little by little and not all at once.</margin> because by walking continuously forward, without making any stop in the middle—not by impulse or jumping, but by going gently and little by little—you will without fail recognize the fruit that you have produced in Music after some months, as that other poet says:

<margin>Hesiod.</margin>
->*Do this very often,*<-
->*Put a little upon another,*<-
->*And you will make a great heap.*<-

This was said not only for the increasing of riches, but also for other things: and we can principally apply it to this, our purpose. Although the youth might feel himself unrefined and of little wit, he must nevertheless force himself to learn what he can; trusting <margin>Exercise strengthens natural abundance and supplies what is lacking.</margin> always that time and continuous study will make him perfect and very expert in the profession to which he applies himself: and always considering that with exercise, not only is the abundance of one's natural talent confirmed, but also faults and defects are supplied. How profitable a thing, and of what efficacy continuous labor and diligence may be, they will learn from many things, if they care to look into it. Hear the example of Lucretius:

> Do you not see even drops of moisture falling upon rocks,
> In a long space of time, bore through the stones?
> <note>original: "Nonne vides etiam guttas in saxa cadentes / Humoris, longo in spatio pertundere saxa?"</note>

<margin>The dripping water hollows the stones. proverb.</margin>
From here the Latins took their proverb: *The drop hollows the stone, not twice, but by falling often:* <note>original: "Gutta cauat lapidem, non bis, sed sape cadendo"</note> The drop hollows very hard stones, not once or twice, but by always falling. It seems to me that this proverb gives much spirit and daring to those of little wit, and gives them heart by saying that a thing as small and soft as a little drop <margin>Hands regularly handling an iron object consume it.</margin> of water hollows and leaves a mark on material as hard as stone. They should also know that iron and metal are worn down by being handled. The wheels of carts that have been bent by force and labor can in no way return to the straightness and rectitude they had at the beginning. The staffs, crooks, and twisted rods of performers are impossible to straighten: because the labor taken against nature overcomes the power of nature. Where there is perseverance, all things in the <margin>What perseverance is.</margin> long run reach their desired end, for according to Plato: *Perseverance is an affection (that is, endurance against labors) which perfects whatever it has chosen.* <note>original: "Perseuerantia est affectio (id est tolerantia aduersus labores) quæ quodcunque elegerit perficit."</note> Thus the prudent <margin>Ant.</margin> ant fills its granary in the summer time, carrying its provision grain by grain. Thus <margin>Spider.</margin> the spider finishes its web which it makes to catch flies, adding one thread to another. Thus the swallow builds its nest; for (when it happens to build it in a land where there is <margin>Swallow.</margin> no mud or mire at all) it wets its wings in the water and rolls in the dust, and in this manner makes mud; and with many such trips, it comes little by little to give an end to its work. Thus <margin>Bee.</margin> the bee fills its hive with wax and honey, going from flower to flower gathering the one and the...

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>A little bird.</margin>
...and the other. In this way, the little bird weaves its nest in the secret parts of the tree, joining one small straw to another; so that, although the materials are small, continuation and perseverance ensure that the work is brought to completion. Not only in these examples I have mentioned is the force of diligence and its power clearly shown, but also in a thousand other things. We know that land which is of a good nature, if it is not worked or cared for, becomes sterile and wild; and the better and more fertile it was by nature, the more it is lost through carelessness and negligence; whereas that which is sterile and very harsh, if it is worked, soon gives very good fruit. <margin>Earth.</margin> Tell me, what trees are there that do not soon twist and go astray, becoming sterile if they are not cultivated and worked? And on the contrary, if they have good care and diligence, they become fruitful and very fertile. <margin>Tree.</margin> <margin>Body.</margin> What strength and fortitude of body is there that does not grow dull and is not lost through negligence, pleasures, and poor disposition? What nature is so thin and weak that it does not gain strength through exercises and struggles? Study and perseverance make <margin>Epistle 14.</margin> <margin>The force of perseverance.</margin> <margin>Usage overcomes difficulty.</margin> difficult and harsh things easy: and Saint Bernard clearly tells us this in one of his epistles: *To one proceeding suddenly from shadow to Sun, from leisure to labor, everything he begins seems heavy: but after he begins to pour out from the former and accustom himself a little to the latter, usage removes the difficulty, and he finds that to be easy which he previously thought impossible.* <note>original: "Subito procedenti de umbra ad Solem..."</note> <margin>Where there is perseverance there can be no imperfection.</margin> Trust that where there is perseverance, there can be no imperfection, for it leaves nothing imperfect; as Seneca says: *I do not despair of the hardened man: there is nothing that persistent work and intent, diligent care cannot overcome.* <note>original: "Non despero indurato..."</note> <margin>Comparison.</margin> For just as negligence is the stepmother of the virtues, so diligence is the true mother of them all. If anyone thinks <margin>No one can learn if they are not disciplined.</margin> that those who have a good natural disposition will necessarily be virtuous without being disciplined—even if they sit all day with their hands folded—or that those who do not have a good natural disposition will be unable to supply the defects of nature even if they are disciplined and exercised in virtue: such a person should know that they err greatly, or better said, entirely <margin>Plutarch, On the Education of Children.</margin> (it being more than true that, *Nature without discipline is blind; and discipline without nature is imperfect*) <note>original: "Natura absque disciplina est cæca..."</note> because laziness and slackness corrupt natural strength and virtue, while doctrine corrects and amends vice and sluggishness. That which is easy flees from the negligent and lazy; and the very arduous and difficult is attained with care and diligence. Of this we have the example of Saint Jerome (among many others) who, <margin>The answer of St. Jerome.</margin> being asked from whom he had learned the science he knew, responded: from toil and from experience. For confirmation of this truth, Lactantius Firmianus says in the third book of *The False Religion*: *Knowledge cannot come from innate talent, nor be comprehended by thought: because to have proper knowledge within oneself is not the part of man, but of God.* <note>original: "Scientia ab ingenio venire non potest..."</note> The wise, to teach us the value of toil and to warn us that through labor anything can be achieved no matter how difficult, left us various maxims in writing: among whom is Virgil in <margin>Virgil.</margin> the *Georgics*, who says: *Labor conquers all.* <note>original: "Labor omnia vincit."</note> Among the Greeks there is this proverbial maxim: *The gods sell good things for labors:* to which Horace alludes, saying: <margin>Horace.</margin>

->Herculean labor breaks through Acheron.<-
<note>Acheron is a river in the underworld; the quote refers to Hercules's strength and effort allowing him to achieve the impossible.</note>

<margin>Sophocles.</margin> Electra says: *Without labor nothing is well.* <margin>Simplicius.</margin> Simplicius Antiphanes <margin>Seneca.</margin> says: *Diligence tames and subdues all things.* And Seneca, in the second book of *On Anger*, says: *And there is nothing so difficult and arduous that the human mind cannot conquer it, and bring it into familiarity through assiduous meditation:* from here the proverb came into use: <margin>Proverb.</margin> *Practice can do all things.* Here (I say again) it is necessary to notice that the more one suffers and works to attain a science, art, or whatever it may be, the more glory one has from it, and the more one prizes it and holds it dear: <margin>Aristotle, Ethics Book 9.</margin> *That which is acquired with greater labor is more greatly loved:* and also one has all the more joy when one occasionally remembers it.
To confirm this, the wise put this saying into use: *Labors accomplished are pleasant:* <margin>M. T. Cicero.</margin> among whom is Cicero, who in the second book of *On the Ends of Good and Evil*, says: <margin>Proverb.</margin> *What if even the memory of past evils is pleasant, so that some proverbs are truer than your dogmas? For it is commonly said: Labors accomplished are pleasant.* <margin>Horace.</margin> Horace says:

<margin>Book 15, Odyssey.</margin> ->They rejoice when, with shaved heads,<-
->The safe sailors chatter about their dangers.<- <margin>Homer.</margin> Homer said before
all of them: But we, eating and drinking on our benches,
->Let us recreate our hearts among ourselves with sad deeds,<-
<note>Referring to the pleasure of recounting past hardships once safety is reached.</note>

<summary>This page argues that diligence and perseverance are essential for overcoming natural weaknesses and achieving mastery in any field. Using classical and theological authorities, the author emphasizes that labor makes difficult tasks easy and that the memory of past struggles brings the greatest joy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cerone’s discourse on the dedication required for musical study, moving from the necessity of labor to the importance of daily consistency and the psychological pitfalls of intermittent practice.</meta>

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

*Commemorating. For indeed it happens that one later rejoices in deeds,*
*Whoever has endured many hardships, having wandered through many things.*
<note>original: "Commemorando. Etenim fit, vt post gaudeat actis, / Aspera quisquis multa tulit per multa vagatus." This is a Latin paraphrase of a sentiment found in Homer's Odyssey.</note>
And **Virgil**, as a competitor of **Homer**, in the first book of the *Aeneid*, says:
->*Perhaps one day it will be pleasing to remember even these things.*<-
<note>original: "Forsan & hæc olim meminisse iuuabit." A famous line of encouragement to those suffering through trials.</note>

<margin>Virgil. Seneca. The Greeks. Euripides.</margin>
Seneca wrote the same in his *Tragedies*, saying: *What was hard to suffer, is sweet to remember*. Among the Greeks it is generally said: *The memory of a past evil is now sweet*. More particularly, Euripides spoke in this manner: *Sweet is the memory of past labors*. One poet expressed the same sentiment, but with different words, in this way: *It is sweet for the man who is saved to remember his labors*; and another said:
->*Greatly pleasant to a man is the renewed mention of sorrows,*<-
->*Who has endured much, and has accomplished much.*<-

<margin>Plutarch, book 1 on the dignity of the pupil. Penelope’s Web.</margin>
*Assiduous exercise brings together experience and the knowledge of science*. But here it is very much to be noted that for this work to be profitable, it is necessary that (besides perseverance) there be great continuity. Because there are some who never carry out a thing in a followed or continued manner, but rather it seems (using the proverb, *To weave Penelope’s web*) that they are always weaving and unweaving the cloth, as they say of Penelope <note>In Greek mythology, Penelope delayed suitors by weaving a shroud by day and unravelling it by night.</note>: and it is that they take this exercise to heart for two or three months, and then they slacken and neglect it in such a way that when they return to what they started, they are already as cold and as remote from it as if they had never begun, nor ever knew what a thing Music is. And so they return to propose anew, and to sketch their exercises once again; and after they have arrived somewhat at a good point, either because of the fatigue of the climb, or because it seems to them that they were already well on their way, they return to neglecting the work, and so they return to beginning as at first. And in this, their time goes by building, destroying, and overturning (according to the proverb, *You carry the Sisyphian stone*) the stone of Sisyphus, which when he had it halfway up the mountain, it would then roll back down on him; and so he began anew to labor to turn it back up; which fable Ovid touches upon:
->*Either you seek, or you push, Sisyphus, the stone that will return.*<-

<margin>Stone of Sisyphus. Metamorphoses book 12. In Hercules Furens. Book 2, lyric poem, ode 14.</margin>
Seneca also makes mention of it, saying thus:
->*Upon the Sisyphian neck a great stone lies.*<-
But **Horace** sings in this manner:
->*And Sisyphus, son of Aeolus, condemned to long labor.*<-

<margin>Leaving study for two or three days is the cause of leaving it entirely.</margin>
These are those who for very small occasions leave their exercise, to whom it often happens that, thinking to leave study for two or three days, they leave it for as many months: and may it please God they do not leave it forever: because when they want to return to it, they cannot find the door, and so they end up staying outside entirely. For a remedy to this, it is convenient in every case to use this diligence, which is not to permit (according to the proverb, *Let no day be without a line*) <note>original: "Nulla dies sit sine linea." A phrase attributed to the painter Apelles, suggesting daily practice is essential for mastery.</note> a day to pass in which a brief lesson is not taken; be it now from Masters, be it from books, or be it from another person of the profession, for all will be good. And whenever you do this, let it be without force, but with gentleness: for just as a city is not built in one day, so do not think you will learn this science in one day.

<margin>Proverb taken from Apelles.</margin>
It is necessary to have much patience, and never to despair, but rather holding for certain that continuous use (as has been said) overcomes all difficulties: and that from a good disciple, with time, a perfect Master is made. Notice, however, that sometimes this exercise works so secretly that a man can hardly perceive it, because virtue commonly works (like nature) little by little, as is seen in a plant, which, not seeing when it grows, we see later that it has grown.

<margin>It is easier to know when a student loses ground than when he gains it. Comparison.</margin>
Therefore, the dull disciple of crude wit should not despair if he does not clearly recognize every day the fruit he produces; for after some days he will see it, and will know that he did not entirely lose his time. A man sees more clearly when he goes backward than when he moves forward: just as a stone would be seen more clearly coming rolling with impetus down a hill than one that goes upward: because (commonly speaking) growing is difficult, and decreasing is easy, and thus it is clearer to see. Furthermore, it is usually said that it is easier to tear down than to build. Let him attend to always working to improve in Music, adding hour by hour desire to desire, love to love, and knowledge to knowledge; so that with these continuous additions, that which he desires may always be on the increase.

<margin>Note, pay attention.</margin>
And let him be warned that he must not trust so much in himself as to be presumptuous, haughty, and reckless;

<summary>Cerone emphasizes the necessity of daily, consistent practice in music, warning that intermittent study is as futile as the labor of Sisyphus or Penelope. He encourages the student to persevere through "crude wit," noting that progress is often invisible until viewed in retrospect.</summary>
<keywords>musical education, perseverance, Sisyphus, Penelope, Apelles, daily practice, pedagogy, Seneca, Virgil</keywords>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.

<margin>St. Bernard, Epistle 7.</margin>
...nor should one despair so much as to remain lowly, cowardly, and dejected. To conclude all this, it is necessary for you to know that it is not enough to begin a work, but to finish it; because perseverance is what attains the reward <note>original: "galardon"</note>: and it is for perseverance, and not for the beginning, that the prize and the crown are given: *Not he who begins, but he who perseveres* <note>original Latin: "Non qui incipiet sed qui perseuerauerit"</note>. And Saint Bernard says: *Strive for perseverance, by which one is crowned* <note>original Latin: "Studete perseuerantiæ qua coronatur"</note>. When one does not have a firm determination to persevere in study for their own honor and reputation, it is better that
<margin>In Antigone.</margin>
they do not begin; for the teaching of the wise admonishes us, saying: *That which cannot be done, should not even be begun* <note>original Latin: "Quod fieri non potest, nec incipiendum quidem est"</note>. And Sophocles also says, *What you cannot accomplish, it is not fitting even to undertake* <note>original Latin: "Quæ efficere nequeas, nec vel ordiri decet"</note>. I give this warning because we know that, *To begin is for many, to finish is for few* <note>original Latin: "Incipere multorũ est, finire paucorum"</note>. He who desists from the work begun and does not continue until the end, sometimes
<margin>Fable.</margin>
tends to curse the one who advised him to set his hand to it; as did the Ape <note>original: "Ximio"</note>, who after having carried a ladder with much sweat and labor to a walnut tree to gather its fruit (for he was told that tree produced very tasty nuts with a rich kernel), climbed up, and taking a nut bit it whole with its green husk; and as he felt the bitterness of the husk, he threw it away: and trying two or three others, finding them of no better flavor but bitter like the first, and not caring to proceed and seek with patience the tasty kernel they had inside, he cursed the one who showed him and praised the nuts: and the one who gave him favor, help, and advice so that he might eat of them; saying with great anger, that the sweetness of the fruit so highly praised had turned into bitterness.
<margin>Exhortation to the new disciple.</margin>
See, reader, that you do not do the same: do not doubt, however unrefined or of little wit you may be, that you are wasting time in vain and without profit. Have patience and do not leave the work begun, however laborious it may be: but trust always that from continuous labor the treasure is drawn. Consider the end; which is the reward of
<margin>He who keeps the reward before his eyes suffers any labor.</margin>
labor, which if you look upon diligently, you will finish your work without great pain; *If you wish to endure the labor*, (says Saint Augustine) *look to the reward* <note>original Latin: "Si vis sustinere laborem... attende mercedem"</note>: for as Aristotle writes, *We love all things, but rewards more* <note>original Latin: "Omnia amamus, sed præmia magis"</note>: and hold it for certain, that he does not deserve
<margin>In Psalm 36. Book 7 of Politics. On Scholastic Disciples, ch. 4. Latin Proverb.</margin>
sweet things, who does not taste the bitterness. Hear Boethius; *He is not worthy of the height of sweetness, who knows not how to be entangled in the weight of bitterness* <note>original Latin: "Non es dignus dulcoris acumine, qui amaritudinis nescit inuiscari grauamine"</note>. He who wants no sting, shall have no honey; first we taste the bitter and then the sweet; and he who does not want the first, shall not have the second; *Neither honey, nor bees* <note>original Latin: "Neque mel, neque apes"</note>; says the Latin proverb.
<margin>He who does not taste bitterness does not deserve sweet things.</margin>
Let those who are naturally of good wit take warning not to mock those who are not so: because there is no reason that asks, nor breeding that commands, that we should throw natural faults in the faces of those who have them; and afflict the afflicted: for natural law says; *Affliction is not to be given to the afflicted* <note>original Latin: "Afflictis non est danda afflictio"</note>; to the afflicted one must not give more
<margin>Warning to those of good wit. It is bad breeding to give affliction to the afflicted.</margin>
affliction: and rather he who has them is worthy of compassion than of derision, for natural faults are more a punishment than a fault. To whom wit and nature give some help, let him give many thanks to God, the dispenser of all graces: for without Him there is not, nor would there ever be, any good thing; and let him not become proud, nor glory in this. For many times that which is praised today with love, is despised tomorrow with hate: and that flower which
<margin>Ignorant humility is more acceptable than proud doctrine.</margin>
just now is very beautiful on its stem, in a very short while will be on the ground, discolored and trampled. It is seen that the world has no honored memory of the proud, even though they may have been more than learned: and among all, simple and ignorant humility was always held in greater value than proud and ambitious doctrine.

### To those of the second opinion, which is that of confidence; and to those who presume much. Chapter XV.

<margin>Which ones are they who profit and go furthest in a science.</margin>
**T**he other opinion contrary to the last is presumption, which makes many believe that they have already reached the end, or at least that they have profited somewhat in the practice of Music. These, deceived by this false confidence, do not work any more to move forward: nor do they see that on this path, only he goes furthest who sees himself to be most lacking; and that the more he approaches perfection, the further away he seems to be from it. For these confident ones, a remedy is found with difficulty: because he who does not recognize himself as sick...

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<page-num>40</page-num>

firm, he does not seek medicine, and thus becomes entirely incurable. In this category
<margin>*Young men who remain ignorant forever.*</margin>
fall all those young men who know little and presume much: those who enjoy the title of Masters without having been disciples. Being blind, they presume to guide others and teach them the path they themselves do not know how to walk; they never confess that there are others of their class with better ability than them, for they never yield to anyone. To this consideration I have been brought by the history of those fishermen who, having sold a cast of their net to certain merchants, caught in it a very rich table of gold. This caused a great dispute between the fishermen and the merchants; while the latter claimed everything caught while fishing belonged to them, the former said they had only agreed upon the fish. Regarding this dispute, the Oracle later declared that the said table should be given to a wise man. Whereupon the fishermen, with the consent of the merchants, sent it immediately to Thales <note>One of the Seven Sages of Greece</note>, as he was a wise man and one of the most celebrated. But Thales, knowing himself unworthy of such honor, refused it; and he himself sent it to another wise man named Bias. This second one also, for the same reason as the first, refused
<margin>*Examples of humility.*</margin>
it and sent it to another wise man; and he to Solon, and Solon finally told them to offer it to Delphic Apollo. Here it is seen that all these wise men, stripping themselves of self-love and knowing themselves and their own unworthiness, refused the rich table of gold and successively passed it on to those wiser than them. It may well be that today other Thaleses, other Biases, and other Solons might be found; but for all that, I will not be so easily persuaded—and even less so that such a manner is used among Musicians today. For I am of the opinion that there is no sacristan nor choir boy who does not presume to be a second **Palestrina** <note>original: "Prenestina", referring to Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina</note>, and a second **Morales** <note>Cristóbal de Morales, a leading Spanish Renaissance composer</note>; and who does not boast of knowing more than **Guerrero** and more than **Master Victoria**. This presumption, then, is one of the principal causes that makes men ignorant. *He who thinks he knows, when he is ignorant (says Marsilio Ficino) renders his mind unteachable.* <note>original Latin: "Qui se scire putat, cum ignoret... reddit animum suum indocilem."</note> And I can very well say
<margin>In the book on Tranquility of the Soul.</margin>
with Seneca: *I think many could have attained wisdom, had they not thought they had already attained it.* <note>original Latin: "Puto multos ad sapientiam potuisse peruenire, nisi putarent se peruenisse."</note> For my part, I hold that many would reach wisdom if they did not understand (or better said, did not dream) that they had already reached it. Often when a person believes they know something, they know nothing; and when they think they deserve the name of Master and to be placed among the number of good Musicians because they understand what they study in a coarse and <term>clumsy way</term> <note>original: "à la babalana", meaning haphazardly or without depth</note>, they are placed among the number of dull disciples for not understanding
<margin>In Seneca.</margin>
the principal part of what is intended. As we read of that "arch-master" who (after having read Virgil in his school for fifteen years) being asked how well he understood
<margin>*Ridiculous answer from a schoolmaster.*</margin>
Virgil's *Aeneid*, answered very well; but for all that, "I am still not entirely clear if Aeneas was male or female." Here the words of Lactantius Firmianus harmonize very appropriately: *Let those who teach first learn: and before they correct the manners of others, they ought to correct their own.* <note>original Latin: "Prius discant qui docent..."</note> Boethius says the same: *It is necessary to learn before teaching.* <note>original Latin: "Oportet prius discere, quam docere."</note>
<margin>Lactantius on True Wisdom.</margin>
<margin>Boethius, Discipline of Scholars, ch. 6.</margin>
Plato says: *Do you not know that it is necessary for those who are to say something to understand it before they teach?* <note>original Latin: "Nonne scis, oportere eos..."</note> The same Boethius in another place says: *It is a miserable thing to be a master who never knew himself to be a disciple;* <note>original Latin: "Miserum est esse magistrum..."</note> he says further: *He who has not known how to submit himself will never know how to be a master.* Which can be concluded with the words of Gambaros, saying: *Let the young man carefully beware lest he wish to be a Master before he has known how to be a disciple; lest if the blind lead the blind, both fall into the pit.* <note>original Latin: "ne si cacus cacum duxerit ambo in foueam cadant." A reference to Matthew 15:14.</note> Let the young man not think that because he has been disciplined for five or six years, he therefore comes out a master; it is necessary afterward that he study by himself, in the manner we shall describe further on; which study must not be for one or two years, but forever. Hear what Ecclesiasticus says: *From your youth receive instruction, and even to your gray hairs you shall find wisdom.* <note>original Latin: "A iuuentute tua excipe doctrinam..."</note> More to our purpose is the saying of Plato: *One must learn as long as one lives;* <note>original Latin: "Tamdiu discendum est, quamdiu uiuitur."</note> and even more complete is this other from Seneca: *One must learn as long as one is ignorant, or rather, as long as one lives.* <note>original Latin: "Tamdiu discendum est, quamdiu nescias..."</note> The cause is that many years are what make a man wise and learned, for time goes on increasing knowledge every day; so that where there are not many years of study, there cannot be much learning. *While all other things are diminished by time (says Plutarch), knowledge is increased by old age.* <note>original Latin: "Cum reliqua omnia à tempore diminuantur... scientia senectute augetur."</note> It is no less damaging to think that you have already reached the end than to think you will never
<margin>Plato on the Nature of Man, book 5. Where cited above, ch. 2.</margin>
<margin>Matthew ch. 15.</margin>
<margin>Chapter 6.</margin>
<margin>Lactantius.</margin>
<margin>Epistle 77.</margin>
<margin>On the Education of Children.</margin>

<summary>Cerone argues that true mastery in music requires lifelong study and humility, using the classical anecdote of the Golden Tripod and various philosophical authorities to warn against the arrogance of young musicians who claim the title of 'Master' too early.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's moral and philosophical reflections on the study of music, transitioning from the internal disposition of the student to the external pitfalls of seeking education abroad.</meta>

# Of Ornaments and Moral Consonances.

<margin>Reasoning for one who desires to profit in virtue.</margin>
...to be able to arrive, as I said another time. You can very well make a theriac <note>original: "triaca"; an ancient herbal jam used as an antidote to poison</note> out of this same poison against it, holding it as a very certain thing that there is no clearer sign of being very far from perfection than believing that you have reached the height of it. Because in this exercise (as in others), those who go about discovering more secrets are those who hurry most to see what is lacking; and with the taste of what they have investigated, their desire for what remains to be investigated always grows. Therefore, they never take account of what they know in comparison to what they hope to know: for it is a natural thing for the prudent not to look so much at what they know as at what they lack in knowing.

<margin>There are two steps that reveal one who knows little.</margin>
But whoever believes that they already know everything, it is a very clear argument that everything they know is a small thing and of no value: because the first step of knowing a little is thinking that one knows much; and the second is boasting of it.

### How the disciple who stays in his own house often learns more than the one who leaves it in search of foreign Masters. Chapter XVI.

**E**mong so many varieties of youths who feign to give themselves to the study of Music, there are some who, not content with sustaining themselves with the maintenance given to them by the Masters of their own places, leave their homes and go to foreign lands: often leaving wheat bread for rye; I mean to say, leaving good and certain Masters to go in search of the uncertain and base, with whom they lose at least two or three years of time. And when one seeks a thing while being in the midst of it and having it abundantly, it can be said to him:

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
*You seek water in the sea.* <note>original: "In mari aquam quæris"</note> The fruit they produce outside their homes, and the profit they receive from these "chosen" Masters, no one knows until they return to where they started: for then there are some idiots who, just upon seeing one of these men, without testing them, say with a full mouth: "He must know much, because he was for so many years in such a city, where there is so-and-so and such-a-one, who are very great Musicians and excellent Masters."

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
There are others who *at first sight, or from the first source* <note>original: "Prima facie, seu prima fonte"</note> know white from black; these, speaking with these "new Orpheuses," <note>A reference to the mythical Greek musician Orpheus, used here sarcastically for those pretending to be masters.</note> gather like so many flowers the first words that happen to fall from their mouths: and this is to conjecture the fruit that such a tree is to produce. Once they have known him, pushed by the truth, they say laughing:

<margin>Proverb: To go a calf and return an ox.</margin>
*If you went as a calf, you returned as an ox.* <note>A proverb meaning one has grown in size or age but not in wisdom or skill.</note> And the cause is because they know less than they knew before.

<margin>The youth who leaves his home runs the risk of losing his life and honor.</margin>
Besides wasting time, losing it with base Masters, they also stray from study because of the opportunity they have for bad company, wasting their money along with their time and life, and what matters much more, their honor. But what shall we say of the many notes and many letters that they send every moment to their parents and relatives, informing them of the profit they are making? Certainly they promise much more with words than they later satisfy with deeds: and all this so that they will more willingly send them money to be able to pursue their studies and reach what they pretend to seek. With these false appearances and new inventions they mock their friends and deceive their acquaintances: and sometimes they also cannot help but mock their own relatives;

<margin>A student mocks his father.</margin>
as we read of that student who, exhorted by letters from his father to spend well the money he sent him, the "good" son responded that he should not be troubled, since all the money he sent him was spent with Prudence, and not in any other way. At first, the father was very much comforted by the son's response and his good governance, as he had signified several times in letters. But in the end, his joy turned to sadness upon being advised that that "Prudence" with whom his son spent the money was the name of a woman of ill repute to whose power he had given himself. Here, leaving one's home to become a man of worth is not forbidden, for whenever this is done, it would be a proceeding against the natural appetite that man has for knowing, as is said in the 18th chapter; but it is forbidden that the very young should leave, nor those who are not yet...

<meta>This page continues Cerone’s discourse on the education of young musicians, specifically addressing the benefits and necessity of traveling abroad to study under different masters.</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>How the young man who leaves his home ought to be.</margin>
<margin>It is an honor and a benefit to leave one's own land.</margin>
...guided in the first principles. Since he is now of a calm and serious mind, it is fitting and highly praised to leave his home to increase his worth, in the manner described in chapter 35 regarding what Palestrina <note>original: "Prenestina"; referring to the famous composer Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina</note> did. I also confess that the little I know (which is very little indeed) I have learned outside of my own land, during the twenty years I was absent from it, supporting myself in diverse provinces and different kingdoms. But I also say that when I left it, I was not such a beginner that I could not compose moderately well, both sacred and secular compositions. That leaving one's home is more praised than staying in it forever—living the life of a hen, which never leaves its yard nor moves away from its dungheap—is told to us by various writers. Among them, the one who wrote most familiarly was the witty Mantuan poet <note>Baptista Mantuanus, a popular Latin poet of the Renaissance</note>, who said:

<margin>Macaronic Poem 12.</margin>
->*They resolved to see the paths of the sea and the land,*<-
->*For it is a joy to travel the world to see new things:*<-
->*For it is more praiseworthy to see new things,*<-
->*Than never to leave the village of one's own land.*<-
<note>The original text is in Macaronic Latin, a mixture of Latin and vernacular Italian/Spanish endings.</note>

Beyond this, necessity is what sometimes drives us from our home, making us travel through foreign lands at the risk of our lives; and as the Venusian Poet <note>Horace, born in Venusia</note> says:

->*Fleeing poverty through the sea, through rocks, through fires;*<-
->*The restless merchant runs to the farthest Indies.*<-

<margin>From experience, prudence is born.</margin>
<margin>Ethics, Book 6, chapter 9.</margin>
<margin>Inexperienced youth.</margin>
<margin>Tobit, chapter 12.</margin>
<margin>The Devil, because he knows much.</margin>
<margin>St. Augustine in The City of God, Book 9.</margin>
<margin>Devil and demon are the same: and from where this word "Demon" derives.</margin>
<margin>Petrarch.</margin>
<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
If it is true that the length of time creates experience, and if it is true that from experience prudence is born, it is also likely and consistent that young men, by defect of age and inexperience, cannot be well-advised or prudent in their actions. That it is true that young men are without experience is stated by the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>: *Young men (he says) are not experienced.* He says further, and in the same place: *A young man cannot be wise: because prudence requires experience, which needs time.* All this being so, we shall say surely with Tobit: *In the ancient is wisdom, and in much time is prudence.* From here was born that so frequent saying: "The Devil knows much because he is old." To whom was given the name <term>Demon</term> <note>Demon: Cerone explains the etymology from the Greek 'daimon'</note>, a term derived from a word, *Daemon*, which in Greek means "Knower"; which name was given to him because of the great knowledge he has of many things. Although what has been said is true, nevertheless, through good self-governance, a young man can also in a certain way show prudence, provided he leaves behind youthful thoughts, life, and customs, and attends to things of honor and reputation; speaking such words and doing such works that possess gravity and modesty. Thus he may proceed in such a way that it can be said of him, as the Tuscan Poet <note>Petrarch</note> wrote: *Gray-haired thoughts in a youthful age.* And concluding, I say with the sentence of the Latins: *Become an old man early, if you wish to be an old man for a long time.*

<margin>Exhortation of the author to those who leave their homes to learn in foreign lands.</margin>
Let us return to ourselves. What I can say regarding young men leaving their homes to learn from foreign Masters is that it seems to me it would be better and more successful if they all stayed in their own lands (provided they have sufficient Masters there) and that with the money they must spend every day on the postage of letters and the rent of an inn, they should pay their Masters liberally. They should not think to satisfy them by giving them three or four reals a month; for all this is a mockery and a thing of nothing. It is well known what craftsmen do at the end of the day when they work for a daily wage, compared to what is accomplished in the same time by those who do the work at their own expense.

<margin>Here it must be noted that in all things there is an exception.</margin>
Just so (saving the good ones) are the Masters who give lessons for a price: because if they are paid so much a month, entire days often pass without benefit to the disciple. But to those who at all times, without waiting for the month to end, are given money, not a single day will pass that they do not show more to that student in one hour than to another in a week. Whoever does this can be certain he will learn very quickly: on his part so as not to spend much money, and on the Master's part so as not to lose just as much. And also because:

<margin>French Proverb.</margin>
<margin>All things obey gold.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiastes, chapter 10.</margin>
*Money (says the Frenchman) does everything*; gold is that which does it all; and it corresponds to the saying of Solomon: *All things obey money.* For what a man does not do for the interest of money, he does not do for anything; and he is so attached to the affection for money that sometimes he does things that are not meant to be done. With money, then, a man is tested in diverse...

<summary>Pietro Cerone discusses the necessity of travel and experience for a young musician's maturity, while also advising that paying teachers generously is more effective than traveling far to save money.</summary>

# Which concerns Ornaments and moral harmonies.

<margin>Man does for money things that ought not be done.</margin>
...diverse actions: listen: *As the touchstone tests gold, so gold tests the man.* <note>original: "Quod index auro, id aurum homini."</note> Just as gold is proven on the touchstone, so is a man proven by gold. For this reason, I let you know that if you let the sound of money be heard in the Master's ear, many profitable precepts will immediately flow from his mouth; and according to the tone of that money, he will compose his voices, forming a very sweet song. <margin>There are Masters who are like jasper.</margin> Because just as jasper (as the naturalists say) has no virtue unless it is encased in silver: so it seems that mercenary Masters do not know how to show their skill unless they are "silver-plated" <note>A play on words meaning paid in silver coins.</note>: and it is commonly said in my land: <margin>Italian Proverb.</margin> *It is in vain to fish if the hook has no bait*; <note>original: "In van si pesca, se l' hamo non hà l' esca."</note> that is, it is useless to fish if the hook is without lure. The same point was intended by that lawyer who said to the litigants who came to ask his opinion so he might advocate for them: <margin>Plutarch in the Life of Pericles.</margin> *He who has need of a lamp, let him pour in oil.* <note>original: "Qui lucerna agent, infundunt oleum."</note> <margin>Parents who are stingy with deeds and liberal with words toward the masters who instruct their children.</margin> There are many parents who make Masters lose the heart to show anything good <insert>to</insert> their children, because in fact they do not deal with them in terms of reason, but only with words. Certainly they would succeed more if the fasteners they have on their purses were on their mouths; keeping the latter closed and the former open at all hours: being always more generous with their hands than with their mouths. Many promise much and do little; they give free rein to words and expand themselves in compliments, but when the time for deeds comes, they shrink inward more than a snail in its shell. Their promises are clouds that appear pregnant with water, but in the end the wind carries them away, and everything turns to air. I say that it often happens that where service lives longest, there the reward dies soonest: verifying that well-worn saying: <margin>Latin Proverb.</margin> *Every no-one*; <note>original: "Omnis nemo."</note> that is to say: <margin>Sentence.</margin> *Everyone wants to know, no one wants to pay the price.* <note>original: "Scire vult omnis, mercedem soluere nemo."</note> Let all the reasons stated above serve.

->To him who discards one chair,<-
->Without having another better.<-

To whom I also give warning, <margin>Look out.</margin> keep your eyes wide open, so that what happened to another does not happen to you; which is, that he took off his tunic to jump further, and jumped less.

## Concerning those who diminish their knowledge: knowing less every day. Chapter XVII.

<margin>Two kinds of medicines.</margin>
**B**ecause the medicine that preserves us from illness is no less necessary than that which increases our health, it is fitting that we give some advice to our youth so that in the first years of his study he may lay a good foundation of <term>musical precepts</term> <note>The fundamental rules of music theory and composition.</note>: so that the musical edifice he builds upon it may be firm and endure; so that neither the winds of losing his Masters, nor the clouds <margin>Occasions that lead a good student to ruin.</margin> of laziness and idleness, nor the rains of sensual vices can tear it down. For we see that there are disciples who give a great showing of themselves, and great hopes of becoming Masters themselves: (for according to the proverb: <margin>Latin Proverb.</margin> *That which is to bear fruit appears immediately.* <note>original: "Protinus apparet, quæ fructum plantatura est."</note>) but afterward they turn back: they have better beginnings than endings: they are better colts than horses: and they are according to the Italian proverb: <margin>Italian Proverb.</margin> *Good goslings, and bad geese.* <note>original: "Buoni paueri, e cattiu' oche."</note> Upon leaving the guidance of their Masters they begin to grow distracted: they fall into the ravines of ignorance, whereby they lose their reputations and let their names be eclipsed. They go backward every day, from bad to worse; in the end they remain like so many buffalo heads to be hung as signs at the doors of inns. <margin>Plutarch on the growth in virtue.</margin> This was touched upon by Menedemus, who used to say he had noticed many times that some sailed to Athens to study almost as <term>Philosophers</term>, who in time became <term>Rhetoricians</term>, giving themselves only to "chatter" <note>original: "chiarlerias" (Italian: chiacchieria).</note>: and they did not move on from there, remaining in effect like so many cucumbers. <margin>Comparison.</margin> Because of this, virtuous Masters come to feel great sorrow; for just as a painter feels great discontent to see spoiled the image he painted with great artifice, and in which <margin>Seneca reproves a youth who had left school, giving himself to a vulgar and vicious life.</margin> he wished to excel and show his genius: so the Master feels, seeing his disciple negligent, whom he taught with great industry and labor; and in whom he so poorly employed his teaching. How many and how many are there who are bastard sons of their fathers; I say bastards, to use the saying of Socrates; who, seeing a beautiful youth, son of a virtuous father, who used to go to school, playing one day publicly with certain lost souls at cards,

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>How Aristophanes signified one who has strayed from school.</margin>
...feet, he said in the presence of everyone: "Nature, O youth, works great miracles; for just the other day your father held you as legitimate, but from here on, due to your discouragement and laziness, you shall be held as the bastard of the house." And he continued telling him that he did an injustice to three things. Firstly to nature, which had created him so beautiful, only to be disfigured by vice; secondly to his father, who was so virtuous, by not following in his footsteps; and finally to his Master, so wise and learned, by fleeing his precepts and disciplines. And note that when Aristophanes <note>Likely referring to the Greek playwright or a philosopher cited in Renaissance commonplaces</note> wished to signify one of those who leave school, becoming in this manner a stray, he painted a Minerva <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and weaving</note> with her face turned away, with a vessel of brackish water in her hands: for Minerva turned elsewhere denotes him who leaves and flees school; and the brackish water denotes sterile operations without fruit, to which he gives himself as a gift; for salt is a thing that makes the earth where it is cast unfruitful.

<margin>Another painting according to the meaning of the Egyptians.</margin>
For this reason the Egyptians, when they wanted to draw to the life a man who had strayed, painted him with the figure of Pallas <note>Another name for Athena/Minerva</note>, with eyes closed, and with a rod in hand, and upon the tip of it was seated an owl: as if the goddess of wisdom could not look upon such a one; nor with the rod (correcting him) teach him in the sciences and disciplines: because it was necessary that he remain for the whole time of his life an owl, deprived of wit and understanding. Truly, he who leaves school and the study of good disciplines loses very much: for this reason Aristotle, weeping with lamentation over the little study of the Athenians,

<margin>Idleness causes a shortage of wheat.</margin>
said: That since those people paid little attention to study, the wheat and the flour were diminishing every day; because according to the books that should have been their pasture <note>i.e., their spiritual/intellectual nourishment</note>, it was necessary that cakes and porridges and puff-pastries, and a thousand other kinds of dough, should be their entertainment.

<margin>Pasture of noble souls.</margin>
With what has been said, the sentence of Simandyo <note>Likely Osymandias/Ramses II</note>, King of Egypt, greatly conforms; who called books the "pasture of noble, honest, and liberal souls." With whom Marcus Tullius Cicero agrees in the *Tusculan Disputations*, while speaking of the ingenious Archimedes, saying: *His mind was nourished by agitating and investigating reasons, with the delight of cleverness, which is the sweetest pasture of souls.* <note>original: "Eius mens rationibus agitandis, exquirendisq; alebatur cum oblectatione solertiæ, qui est suauissimus pastus animorum."</note> Which pasture is not known to those who have their eyes covered with the veil of ignorance, which does not let them in any way know their own good.

<margin>The reason why children determined to do no more good do not leave their practice immediately upon leaving the master.</margin>
It is no cause for wonder if the disciple (having left the Master) does not abandon all at once the exercise he learned; and it is because he still seems to feel entering his ears those ordinary warnings of his Master; and because the movement of the admonitions he gave him still lasts within him. Because just as a wheel moved with great impetus for a long space afterward, even though the mover ceases, it moves by itself by virtue of that impetus the arm put into it, until little by little the movement goes on ending:

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
so the disciple in his first study is moved by the warnings of his Master: and although later, either by absence, or by the death of the Master, or by any other cause, the warnings cease; he still moves by himself to pursue his study, by that impetus of the Master: until little by little that good movement goes on undoing itself; which being finished, the disciple remains entirely negligent and careless: hating entirely what he previously loved excessively.

<margin>Plato, Book 11 of the Laws.</margin>
<margin>Aristotle, Ethics Book 3.</margin>
<margin>Plato, On the Laws.</margin>
Plato says: *The morals of youth are often changed daily and in various ways.* <note>original: "Iuuenum mores, sæpe indies variæque mutantur"</note> The cause seems to be because: *Boys and beasts follow pleasures, etc., and the first sense of boys is pleasure.* <note>original: "Pueri ac bestiæ sequuntur voluptates &c. & primus puerorum sensus est voluptas."</note>

<margin>Youth is mutable.</margin>
Certainly the chameleon is not so mutable in its colors, nor the weathercocks of the bell towers so easy to move to all winds, as the age of the youth to all the winds of the world and disordered passions; which make him depart from a good beginning and lead him to a bad end; and all this for lack of firmness: for in the morning he wants one thing, and another in the afternoon: whereby he gives place to the proverb:

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
*Like a many-leaved plant.* <note>original: "Tanquam polijfolium." Likely referring to a plant that changes appearance or has many faces.</note> So that it does not seem to be my invention, or that I say all this from my own head: let us see what Solomon says on this matter; in his proverbs he says:

<margin>Solomon, Proverbs 30.</margin>
> *Three things are difficult for me, and a fourth I am entirely ignorant of: The way of an eagle in the sky, the way of a serpent upon the earth, the way of a ship in the midst of the sea, and the way of a man in his youth.* <note>original: "Tria sunt difficilia mihi, & quartum penitus ignoro: Viam aquilæ in cælo, viam colubri super terram, viam nauis in medio mari, & viam viri in adolescentia."</note>

Three things (he says) are difficult for me to understand, but the fourth passes me by entirely, and surpasses my understanding. The first is the path of the eagle through the

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies.

air; the second, the way of a serpent upon a rock; the third, the track of a ship in the midst of the sea; and the fourth, the way of a man in his youth <note>original: "el camino del varon en su mocedad." A reference to Proverbs 30:18-19.</note>: and although this last sentence has other meanings, I nevertheless wish to take it in the sense that serves our purpose. <margin>Parents should keep their eyes open and not place so much hope in something that is not certain, but doubtful.</margin> I have wished to warn those unlearned parents who, seeing their sons as boys of good wit, very studious, and diligent in Music, form a high opinion of them and hope they will prosper. Thus they build a thousand castles in the air and begin to make designs for the Mastership <term>Magisterio: the position of Chapel Master or director of music</term> of one church, or the Organist's post of another, before the boys have the skill necessary to achieve what they pretend to. Such parents make a feast and rejoice over an uncertain and doubtful thing, judging the following day to be serene and clear, when many times it turns out cloudy and with tempestuous waters. But the prudent, because they see that the hope they offer is still in the blade <note>i.e., like young green grass</note> <margin>The prudent take account of their children, always fearing for them.</margin> and can be lost to the mist of pampered idleness, remain fearful and with an anxious heart until they see them reached a mature age; always fearing and never fully trusting them.

## In which things the principal study should be placed: and how we should occupy ourselves with curiosities. Chap. XVIII.

<margin>Harmful curiosities.</margin>
**T**he vice of curiosity greatly hinders one from reaching perfection quickly. This happens in many ways: for there is a curiosity for making counterpoint based on the positions of the hand; for singing one part while signaling another; for making one voice follow another; for composing based on the buttons of a doublet, and fifty thousand other kinds of sophistries <note>original: "sophisterias." Refers to overly complex or "clever" musical puzzles that lack substance.</note> which those who wish to be held as great and singular Musicians (without being so) invent at every moment. Because of this, they fall into great blunders and suffer notable losses of time; although later, experience makes such a <margin>Necessity is the inventor of remedies.</margin> change in their thoughts that it makes them change their opinion. Necessity, the inventor of remedies, teaches them to follow good rules and excellent Masters, and to take advantage of their advice. I say that some waste months and years in trying to learn certain curiosities that are entirely vain and superfluous, and no less ridiculous than <margin>The foolish student.</margin> the singular curiosity of that other stubborn man, who turned and overturned so many volumes of books, both ancient and modern, Greek as well as Latin, only to find out when Aeneas the Trojan disembarked in Italy, which foot he set on land first, the right or the left. Regarding this, he would never have been at peace in all his life, had he not been told that from the prow of the galleon he jumped onto the shore with feet together, and thus placed both on the ground at once. With this futile lie, the futile and very curious (not to say mad and foolish) student was entirely satisfied. Curiosity, besides occupying a person in vain and false skills, creates fantasies in his head, by which he loses the good opinion of wise Musicians; for he occupies himself in something that is neither a masterpiece of art, nor of any use for divine worship (which is the principal end for which Music was ordained in the church), nor for those who desire to know this profession. And although it strikes fear and admiration into simple boys, who receive it as very good and fine gold, nevertheless, to <margin>Curiosities are like "singing gold" (brass), and good rules are like fine gold.</margin> experienced men it gives occasion for laughter, taking it for "singing gold" <note>original: "oro cantarino." A term for brass or tinsel that looks like gold but makes a noise; a hollow imitation.</note>—something that has the appearance of gold but is not—an exercise of much study and little profit. A foreigner in Lacedaemonia <note>Sparta</note>, standing upright on one foot while he put his shoe on the other, said to a Lacedaemonian: "I think you could not stand as long as I can." The Lacedaemonian replied: "I cannot; but of the geese, there is <margin>Plutarch in the Apophthegms of the Lacedaemonians.</margin> not one that cannot do it," laughing at him, who with much practice had learned an art that brought no benefit to the republic. The use and exercise of a thing in which the cost is greater than the gain is to be abandoned and left behind, if a man wishes to be held as prudent <margin>Graceful reply of a Lacedaemonian.</margin> and discreet. What I say is understood by that common proverb which says: *Labor without profit, to leave it is profitable.* And using here the comparison of Zeno the philosopher, I say: that just as the bushel can measure the ripened wheat, and can...

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
And just as one can measure empty straw and poisonous things, so our understanding can be occupied in knowing solid and substantial things, or light and vain things, or harmful and prejudicial things. But it is up to us to keep it within honest limits and occupy it with profitable things. For just as hunters do not allow their pointer dogs to scent and follow every odor they smell, but rather keep them held back with their leashes to preserve their scent and trail pure and whole—so that in the proper time and season they can, with greater instinct and sagacity, follow the tracks of the beasts—so too is it necessary to restrain and withhold curiosity, so that it does not run and scatter itself wandering to see and hear just anything, but rather is saved for what is necessary and profitable. Who does not know that occupying oneself in such curiosities is a labor without fruit, and an occupation without reward or merit? And who is he that would wish to tire himself without fruit and without prize? Certainly
<margin>Note.</margin>
none of sound mind; for every man hopes for a reward and tires himself willingly; for we already see how much sweat a man spends for a small gain. The principal study, then, that the youth must undertake is to play, sing, <term>counterpoint</term> <note>the art of composing melodic lines against a given melody</note>, compose, and direct a musical group or Chapel, and this according to the talent and natural inclination he may have. But beyond what has been said, for entertainment and recreation, he may occupy himself in some curiosities, but not as a primary study. Because just as he who is thirsty first drinks, and afterwards contemplates the beauty and craftsmanship of the vessel: so I hold for myself, that every youth desirous of knowing Music must first taste the necessary advice of the art, and afterwards consider (if he wishes) the vain craftsmanship of curiosities. I say again to everyone universally: I remind you to place more study upon the precepts and advice of the art than upon the singularities and refinements of curiosity; for many times, by paying heed to the sweetness of the latter, the concept and the goal of what is being read and what one desires to know is abandoned, leaving the understanding unable to penetrate what is appropriate for it, because it seeks what is inappropriate.

<margin>The benefit of rules is not known by those who base themselves on curiosities.</margin>
The benefit of the rules is not understood by those who base themselves on flowery curiosities, without attending to the substance of good musical precepts; they want nothing more than the bark and appearance, without taking account of the interior and essential. To such people, if anyone dares call them diligent, I shall dare call them negligent, and perhaps ignorant: because he knows little of walnuts who leaves the kernel for the shell, and little of apples who throws the core away to keep the peelings. How many there are who,
<margin>Fable.</margin>
by wanting to know things that seem great to them, know neither the great nor the small?
<margin>Leaving the meat for the shadow.</margin>
And leaving the substance for the shadow, they are left with nothing; like Aesop’s dog, who, deceived by the size of the shadow he saw in the water caused by the meat he carried in his teeth, dropped the meat to take the shadow, and was left without the one and without the other.

<margin>Another worse curiosity.</margin>
There is another curiosity, which is that of those who, with only the appetite for wanting to know a few uncommon little things, set themselves to reading treatises on Music—not with the desire to reach true knowledge through them, but rather with this artifice they go about picking up some curious points that they can vainly teach to others, without taking anything for themselves.
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 21.</margin>
These have hearts like a sieve or a sifter that casts away the fine flour and keeps only the straw and the bran; for so these people let the profitable advice pass through, which they should have kept, and they remain with the straw and bran, which is to say with childish trifles, upon which they wish to feed like goslings. And it is true that many times, not without shame, they themselves confess the same: but not before, but rather after having lost, together with their time, the "beard of their reputation" <note>an idiom for losing one's dignity or honor</note>, under the pretext of wanting to know some very "important" curiosities. They are like that curious scholar who, reading in a book of natural secrets where it said that a man who has a wide beard was a sign of a very foolish person, took a candle in his hand to look at himself in a mirror (because it was night), and through carelessness burned almost half of his beard; because of which he then wrote in the margin of that same book: *It is proven* <note>original: "Probatum est"</note>.

<margin>The worst curiosity.</margin>
There is a third curiosity worthy of reproach, and it is that of those who, without being requested: *Set foot in another's choir* <note>original: "In alieno choro pedem ponunt"; a Latin proverb meaning to meddle in other people's business</note>; and they spend all their time only visiting the studios of the neighborhood and examining the compositions of others with eyes of...

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

He uses allegories, such as Aesop's dog and a foolish scholar, to illustrate how seeking vanity leads to the loss of actual substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ethical and practical dimensions of music, specifically focusing on the dangers of "curiosity"—here meaning an obsessive focus on trivial technicalities or the faults of others rather than fundamental mastery.</meta>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Consonances.

<margin>Proverbs. Plutarch in his Commentary on the Symposiasts.</margin>
...of Argus <note>original: "Argo"; referring to the hundred-eyed giant of myth</note>, without taking care to examine their own, even with a single half-blind and bleary eye. In such a way that of each of these men, so wise in the study of others and so ignorant in their own, one can say with Horace:

<margin>Proverb.</margin>
> *Caring for the affairs of others, while neglecting one's own.* <note>original: "Aliena negotia curans, Excussus proprijs."</note>

Or thus, metaphorically and proverbially: *He plows another's field; he leaves his own family's land uncultivated.*

<margin>The Fable of Actaeon.</margin>
Such curiosity is often dangerous, and how harmful it may be is shown by the ancient fable of Actaeon, son of Cadmus, who was torn to pieces by his own dogs for having looked with excessive curiosity at the naked Diana while she was washing herself in a nearby fountain. And what is worse, there are among these people some who are very subtle and diligent in searching for *the hair on the egg* <note>A Spanish idiom meaning to look for flaws where none exist.</note>, as the common folk say; or as the Latins say: <term>They seek a knot in a bulrush</term> <note>original: "Nodum in scirpo quaerunt"; a metaphor for finding difficulty where there is none, as a rush is smooth.</note>. I say that they very often go looking for defects where there are none, while they themselves are full of confusion and shames, yet they see them not in the least. To these men the saying of Sophocles is well suited: <term>Seeing far off, but seeing nothing close at hand</term> <note>original: "Procul videns, sed cominus videns nihil."</note>.
<margin>Pausanias in his Boeotics.</margin>
<margin>Proverb.</margin>
<margin>Sophocles.</margin>

<margin>Opposition.</margin>
It may well be that some curious person might tell me that I cannot deny that subtleties are of great profit; and that just as the sharpness of wit with its sophistical subtleties profits much for the knowledge of the sciences, so too the refinements and curious singularities of the Musician profit much in perfectly attaining Music.

<margin>Response.</margin>
To whom I respond: although they seem to be of profit, they are not. Rather, I hold them to be harmful. Singular curiosities are as bothersome to every good Musician as vain sophistries are greatly bothersome to every good Philosopher. This is what the ancient gentiles meant to signify when they said that the spider was hateful to Minerva:
<margin>The spider is hateful to Minerva.</margin>
Because just as the spider makes its web with great subtlety and sharpness, and once made it serves for nothing but to catch flies and dirty the house; so the verbose and vain sophist, and the curious and singular Musician, when they occupy themselves in profitless things, warp and weave the web of their arguments and refinements with sharp subtleties. These serve for nothing but to deceive boys and to dirty the memory. And so, seeing one who fatigues himself with great anxiety and much labor in a frivolous thing, without fruit and of no value, you may safely say that such a one <term>weaves spiders' webs</term> <note>original: "Aranearum telas texit"; a metaphor for useless, intricate labor.</note>.
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
<margin>Proverb.</margin>

<margin>Minerva with the spear in hand, and its significance.</margin>
I confess that a good wit must have sharpness, subtlety, strength, and speed, but this must be for the knowledge of profitable things, not useless ones. Thus the gentiles meant to signify (as Martianus and Pierio <note>Likely referring to Martianus Capella and Pierio Valeriano, authors on allegory and hieroglyphics.</note> interpret it) when they painted Minerva, goddess of wisdom, with a strong spear of sharp iron in her hand, with which she fought lightly. For just as eagles and lions, when they walk, retract their claws inward so as not to spoil their edge and sharpness, so too should we think that curiosity has an edge and sharpness to apprehend and know many things; but let us take care not to waste or blunt it on base things of little value.
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
Those who set themselves to knowing useless singularities, and those who occupy themselves in studying only refinements without profit, are like those who wished to marry the noble and chaste Penelope; who, seeing they could not reach her, married some of her maidservants and were content with that. Just so do some who guess they cannot profit as they wished in Music: for as many as begin to study this delightful profession, all or nearly all pretend to reach its summit. Many of these, seeing how little they can ascend, content themselves with knowing how to sing and play an instrument passably; to poorly compose a motet; to invent some sophistry to stupefy novices, and to make a net of childish trifles to entangle those who do not understand.
<margin>The equinoctial line of those who are friends of curiosities.</margin>
This is (I say) the equinoctial line <note>The limit or boundary beyond which they cannot pass.</note> where such people arrive, and from here they go no further. And since with curiosities time is lost—a thing so esteemed by the studious man—I know not what reason there is to give such excessive praise to one who is far from deserving it; to whom one can say with a full mouth: <term>You waste your labor and your oil</term> <note>original: "Operam, & oleum perditis"; an ancient proverb for wasted effort, referring to the oil in a scholar's lamp.</note>. It is fitting then that he who intends to profess Music, before he sets himself to wanting to know curiosities and subtleties, should know how to give a very good account...

<summary>This page warns musicians against focusing on trivial technicalities and the faults of others, using classical allegories like Actaeon and Minerva to argue that true mastery requires focusing on profitable knowledge rather than "spiders' webs" of sophistry.</summary>
<keywords>musical ethics, curiosity, Actaeon, Minerva, Penelope, sophistry, musical education, Horace, Sophocles</keywords>
