<margin>When we ought to occupy ourselves with curiosities.</margin>
<margin>When knowing is ignorance; and ignorance is knowing.</margin>
<margin>Note.</margin>
well the precepts that are of substance: leaving curiosities, although they may be profitable, for the completion of one's study and for rest from one's labors; for they are a pasture of great pleasure and much recreation. And note that I said, "although profitable"; because there are some curiosities that it is necessary to leave aside entirely, as knowing them is of no benefit. I hold for myself that sometimes not knowing is knowing: because knowing things that are not of benefit (and are instead harmful) is ignorance; and to be ignorant of them is to know. <note>Cerone is playing with a common Renaissance paradox: that true wisdom lies in knowing what to ignore.</note> With all this, let the reader be advised that it is not my intention to condemn extraordinary curiosities entirely, but rather to prefer the precepts of the art over them.

## On how recreation is very necessary to one who desires to perfect themselves in a science or liberal art; and of the harm received from continuous study. Chapter XIX.

<margin>All the children of Adam are friends of seeing and knowing secret and new things.</margin>
**S**eneca says in the book *On the Happy Life*, and the Prince of Philosophers <note>A common title for Aristotle.</note> in the first book of the *Metaphysics*, that: *All men by nature desire to know.* <note>original: "Omnes homines natura scire desiderant."</note> They say that nature itself imprinted in our souls a natural desire to know secret things: which is why many men sail and go wandering through very distant regions, solely for this interest in knowing hidden things. With this natural appetite is joined the nobility of the exercise, and the sweetness that is in it:

<margin>The exercise most worthy of the nobility of man is letters.</margin>
because in truth it does not seem that there is any other exercise more worthy of the nobility of man than to employ oneself entirely in perfecting that most noble part within him, which is reason: which becomes more perfect every day with the continuous use of studies: and this we shall say of all virtuous exercises, and among them we shall place that of Music. It seems impossible that man should

<margin>Parts that invite man to study.</margin>
be so mortified and so constant that he could resist these such sweet exercises. For on one hand, the natural appetite for knowing invites him; on the other, the natural delight of study; on another, the nobility of the exercise; and on another, the desire for honor, which is attained through this. With all this, whoever desires to receive some benefit, it is fitting that they leave aside that superfluous continuation:

<margin>Too much studying is harmful.</margin>
because if study is not taken with temperance, it is undoubtedly a great impediment to becoming a perfect Musician. And it is no wonder that a thing so laudable can come to be harmful if it is not taken with temperance: for it is no new thing for the excess of all things to be harmful, even if they are in themselves good and necessary: *Every excess of things (says the Philosopher) either harms or profits nothing.* <note>original: "Omnis excessus rerum... vel nocet, vel nihil proficit."</note> What thing is more necessary than eating, and drinking, and moderate exercise,

<margin>Politics, book 7.</margin>
and bodily medicines? All these things are very good and necessary; but if they are not taken with temperance we see that they are very harmful; particularly drinking, as we are to see at length in the chapter that follows. Well, we say this same thing of study and the excessive appetite for knowing, which without doubt is a very great impediment to reaching perfection; when study is not taken with rest.

<margin>Aristotle, 7th Politics & 15th Ethics. Cicero, 1st On Duties.</margin>
For it is to be known that, according to the doctrine of Aristotle and Cicero: *Everyone who labors needs rest: Rest and play seem to be necessary in life, etc. Seasoned with salt and wit, Caesar, etc.* <note>original: "Omnis laborans requie indiget... Sale vero conditus, & facetiis Caesar." Cerone is quoting classical authorities to justify the need for leisure.</note> Recreation is as necessary for the man who lives

<margin>St. Thomas, 2nd part of the 2nd part, question 168, articles 4 & 3.</margin>
in the world as salt is for the food that is to be eaten. Because (says Saint Thomas) man is composed of body and soul, and his virtue is limited, and for that reason he cannot work continuously: for which some relief and rest is necessary.

<margin>The cause why recreation is necessary for man.</margin>
For just as the body tires with physical labor, so does the soul with spiritual labor; and therefore both have need of relief and recreation to return to work: noting that, as Marsilio Ficino says: *The labors of the soul are heavier than those of the body.* <note>original: "Graviores sunt animi labores, quam corporis."</note>

<margin>Marsilio in Plato. Plutarch on the education of children. Comparison.</margin>
Plutarch calls this rest the "conduit of labor": because just as green reeds keep growing, but from time to time they make knots as a rest, in which it seems that nature reposes; not to stay there, but to turn upward again with greater strength: so the youths disciplined in labor (using the precept...

<summary>Domenico Pietro Cerone argues that while the pursuit of knowledge is a noble human instinct, excessive study without rest or "temperance" is counterproductive. He cites various classical and theological authorities to prove that recreation is essential for both physical and intellectual growth.</summary>

## Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>Book 1 of *Duties* <note>original: "Lib. 1. officio." referring to Cicero's *De Officiis*</note></margin>
<margin>In the 2nd of *On the Orator*; in *Hercules Furens* <note>Seneca's tragedy</note></margin>
<margin>Scipio and Laelius recreate themselves gathering little shells.</margin>
from Cato, who says: *Interpose joys sometimes amidst your cares* <note>original: "Interpone tuis interdum gaudia curis"</note>; and from that other Tragedian <note>Seneca</note>: *After much labor, virtue is wont to be relaxed.* <note>original: "Post multa virtus opera laxari solet"</note>) they go about interposing rest into their troubles at times, like knots upon which they may lean: not to take physical idleness as an end in itself, but rather to be able to suffer the continuous fatigues of study with greater strength, and to take up honorable exercises with more eagerness. And so says Marcus Tullius Cicero in the first of his *Duties*: *Indeed, it is permitted to use play and jest; but just as one uses sleep and other rests.* <note>original: "Ludo autem, & ioco, vti illis quidem licet; sed sicut somno & quietibus cateris."</note> In such a manner we must use games and recreations as we use sleep and rest: because being refreshed in this way, we rise with greater strength to work; as we read in the aforementioned author, and in Seneca, regarding what that most illustrious Captain Scipio Africanus (the second of this name) used to do. He would sometimes leave Rome with Laelius, as if leaving a prison; and both would go to unburden themselves by the sea, where they walked gathering little shells and other small things on the solitary beaches of Gaeta, despite both being most grave men and columns of the Roman Republic. I say that those most powerful hands of Scipio, destroyers of Numantia and Carthage—cities of war and, by human judgment, impregnable—humbled themselves to trifles; so that with that brief period of leisure, the understanding might be refreshed, and the tired spirit might catch its breath and recover strength for great business, entire labors, and high enterprises.

<margin>In the *Life of Cato*. Cato the Censor recreates himself.</margin>
Plutarch also relates of that famous man Cato the Censor, that many times in the courtyard of his house, he would run on a wooden horse with a cane in his hand, teaching his sons how they were to run, and how to take the lance in their hands and throw it with spirit.

<margin>In the 8th of *On Leisure*.</margin>
<margin>Socrates takes pleasure with his son.</margin>
<margin>G. Augustus plays with boys.</margin>
<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
<margin>Epistle 4.</margin>
Aelian and Valerius Maximus relate the same of Socrates, who at times, when he wished to take some honest recreation, enjoyed himself in the same manner with his son Lamprocles. To this end, Caesar Augustus also (as Suetonius relates) would set himself to play at dice with some boys. And why not? Since the bow that is always drawn does not shoot very far? For this reason they say: *Apollo does not always bend his bow.* <note>original: "Arcum non semper tendit Apollo suum."</note> From this it seems the Latins took occasion to say proverbially: *A stretched bow breaks* <note>original: "Arcus tensus rumpitur"</note>; and with reason Ovid said:

->*That which lacks alternating rest is not durable.*<-
<note>original: "Quod caret alterna requie durabile non est."</note>

<margin>Epistle 15. On the education of children.</margin>
<margin>Book 9 of *Ethics*, chapter 8.</margin>
<margin>Apelles rebuked himself, saying he was too continuous in fatigue, since he never stopped painting.</margin>
From this Plutarch came to say: *Leisure is the seasoning of labor; and this we see happening not only in living creatures, but even in those things which lack a soul: for we unstring bows and lyres so that we may be able to stretch them again: and in short, the body is preserved by need and replenishment, but the soul by relaxation and labor.* <note>original Latin provided in text</note> Whereupon Seneca commands Lucilius that, having left behind grave studies, he should sometimes recreate himself: since in games and honest recreation (as Aristotle affirms) virtue is placed. Protogenes greatly praised Apelles, saying he surpassed and beat all others in the art of painting, proclaiming him perfect in every particular touching upon it. Apelles responded that although he was held to be an excellent Master in 그 profession, nevertheless, among the others, he recognized that he had one very great defect: and it was that he never stopped painting; signifying (which is what we are saying) that diligence used consecutively more than is due in one thing causes harm. Because the wit is destroyed and worn out by oppressing it and tiring it more than is due continuously in one exercise: and diverting it for some space of time to diverse purposes profits the principal purpose much.

<margin>St. John recreates himself with a bird. Cassian, last book, chapter 21.</margin>
<margin>Abbot John takes his monks out to recreate in the field.</margin>
But because the examples set down above (though of illustrious and wise persons) are all of gentile and profane persons; it is well that we confirm what we are saying at least with a pair of examples of Catholic persons and Saints, so that recreations and games may be taken with greater satisfaction; it being understood always that they must be such that they are not foreign to honest actions: and they will be better if in them is found some light of good wit; such as recreating oneself in honest talk, from which pleasures can be drawn without prejudice to anyone. The example I wish to relate is of Saint John the Evangelist, who (according to what Cassian writes in the life of Saint Augustine) took pleasure with a little bird he held in his hands. Also the Abbot John (says the same writer) took his monks out sometimes to the field; and being once with them, a traveler passed by with a bow in his hand; and when he saw them, he was scandalized to see them enjoying themselves. Then the holy man called him and said: Look,

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

brother, just as if you were to overstretch the bow you carry, you would end up breaking it: so too, if I were to overstretch the spirit of my monks with continuous labor, I would end up breaking them; and for this reason, it is necessary that I sometimes take them out for recreation, as you see now.
And with the saying of this, the traveler came to his senses and departed entirely satisfied and consoled.

<margin>Another benefit follows from recreation.</margin>
Besides rising after recreation with greater strength and with more desire for our usual work of study, sometimes resting makes obscurities easier for us; because it sharpens our understanding and opens the path for us to understand things that before

<margin>Seneca, On Moral Virtue.</margin>
were difficult for us. On the contrary, continuous study blinds our understanding and dulls our wit. *Just as plants are nourished by moderate waters, but are suffocated by too much; so the spirit is refreshed by moderate labor, but overwhelmed and crushed by too much.* <note>original Latin: "Quemadmodum plantæ modicis aquis aluntur, plurimis autem suffocantur; sic animus mediocri labore vegetatur, nimio demersus oprimitur."</note>

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
Just as herbs are grown and sustained with moderate water, but if it is too much and excessive they drown: so wits turn green again and sharpen with moderate work, but if it is excessive, it dejects and stupefies them. And so (dearly beloved reader) you will see many times that what you did not know how to understand in a day of continuous study and labor, by resting, you will suddenly come to understand very well: and what before seemed very difficult and obscure will seem quite easy and clear to you.

<margin>The recreation of Music is the most noble.</margin>
And note that among all recreations, that of Music is the most noble, the most worthy, and the one that works the greatest effects: and for this (among other causes) it was placed among the liberal arts: and thus in this more than in any other thing should he who is of a noble and virtuous spirit recreate himself. Plato says:

<margin>Politics, Book 8.</margin>
*The ancients placed music among the disciplines because nature seeks not only to be able to engage rightly in business, but also to be able to spend leisure time laudably;* <note>original Latin: "Veteres inter disciplinas musicam collocarunt, ex eo quod natura quærit, non solum in negotio rectè, verum etiam in otio laudabiliter versari posse"</note> and in another place he says:

<margin>As above.</margin>
*Music should not be used for the sake of a single utility, but for many; namely for the sake of learning, and purification, and thirdly for the sake of spending leisure, for the sake of quiet, and so that cares may be released.* <note>original Latin: "Non unius utilitatis gratia Musica uti oportet, sed multarum; nam doctrinæ gratia, & purificationis, tertio gratia degendi otij, quietisque causa, & ut curæ remittantur."</note>

<margin>What the recreation of Eutrapelia may be.</margin>
It remains now to warn you to take care not to exceed the proper limit of recreation and honest play, which the moral philosophers call <term>Eutrapelia</term> <note>A virtue described by Aristotle as the "golden mean" of wit and playfulness, situated between buffoonery and boorishness.</note> (which consists of the middle of two extremes; one is called foolish joy, and the other rusticity; both are vicious and to be <unclear>avoided</unclear>) but rather with the circumstance of time appropriate to such virtuous recreation; so that the day is not lost in games and mockeries: seeing as there are some who in this go from one extreme to another, which is that wanting to flee melancholy and rusticity, they become dissolute and rogues: where Horace, in the second of his satires, says:

<margin>Book 1, Sermon Satires 2.</margin>
> *While fools avoid vices, they run into the opposite ones.* <note>original Latin: "Dum vitant stulti vitia, in contraria currunt."</note> Regarding which it must be noted that: *Play should be for the sake of rest:* and that: *Leisure is never approved; except*

<margin>Aristotle, 8 Politics.</margin>
*that which is granted both for refreshing bodies and for lightening spirits:* and for this second end

<margin>Giovanni Pontano in Charon.</margin>
it is granted particularly, because (as I said) *The labors of the spirit are heavier than those of the body.* <note>original Latin: "Grauiores sunt animi labores, quam corporis."</note> But we must likewise note that recreation must be used

<margin>Marsilio Ficino on Plato.</margin>
not as ordinary sustenance, but for the restoration of life: taking into account the

<margin>Philostratus, Book 1 of the Sophists.</margin>
ancient saying of Dionysius of Miletus; *Honey is to be tasted with the tip of the finger, not with the hollow of the hand:* <note>original Latin: "Mel summo digito, haud caua manu delibandum est"</note> he says that it is necessary to taste honey with the tip of the finger, and not with the whole hand.
And having consideration for the authority of Solomon, who in the 25th of his pro-

<margin>Tasting honey with the tip of the finger.</margin>
verbs says; *Have you found honey? Eat what is sufficient for you, lest perhaps being sated you vomit it up.* <note>original Latin: "Mel inuenisti? comede quod sufficit tibi, ne forte satiatus euomas illud."</note> Not forgetting those sententious verses, which say:

-> *Whatever is excessive is unpleasant: for it was said of old,* <-
-> *Even honey, if the abundance is immoderate, will become bile.* <-

From here they put

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
into use the refrain, *To touch with the tips of the fingers;* <note>original Latin: "Extremis digitis attingere"</note> to say that pleasures, games, recreations, and pastimes must be tasted with parsimony. So that by observing

<margin>Epistle 15.</margin>
this, one also comes to obey the precept of Seneca, who speaks in this manner: *An interval must sometimes be given to the spirit, yet in such a way that it is not dissolved, but relaxed.* <note>original Latin: "Dandum est aliquando interuallum animo, ita tamen ne resoluatur, sed remittatur."</note> This is said because there are some who so immerse themselves in games and recreations that when they wish, they do not know how to return to their exercise, as was said at length in the 11th and 14th chapters; and they remain so disordered that for many days they do not find themselves

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
fit for study. Just as salt, if it is moderate, seasons and gives flavor to the food that is to be eaten, and if it is excessive, makes it bitter and distasteful: so recreation, if it is moderate, makes the fatigues of life flavorful and bearable; but if it is excessive, it leaves men bitter and difficult for returning to their usual labors.

# Concerning Adornments and Moral Harmonies.
<sig>G 2</sig>

<margin>Comparison</margin>
Beyond this, we may say that some students are like the cormorant <note>original: "cueruo marino" (sea crow)</note>, which spends the whole day under water, but upon emerging, remains dry with a single shake of its wings. In the same way, although these students spend all day in public exercise among exquisite masters and persons highly skilled in the profession, upon leaving it for any small occasion, they lose their affection for it and everything they had gained while among people.

<margin>Comparison</margin>
Thus, just as we are accustomed to telling one who rides a malicious beast to keep a tight rein—namely, neither too tight nor too loose, so that it neither turns back nor walks in danger—so must we ensure that our study proceeds: moderated, and not forced; with care, and not with fatigue and anguish. He who tunes a <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed ancient instrument used to determine musical intervals</note>, or a lute, or any other stringed instrument, must not raise the strings so high that they break, nor leave them so loose that they make no music. Likewise, it is not fitting for our disciple to be so continuous, so lengthy, and so elevated in his study that he loses his mind; nor so slow, so brief, and so relaxed that it is of no benefit to him. Rather, he must take care to approach his study with delight and with due labor, not spending the whole day in the same study and always with excessive occupation; for everything requires a middle ground and a virtuous moderation.

<margin>Proverbs</margin>
<margin>All extremes are vicious.</margin>
And in memory of this, let that ancient proverb always be kept in mind: *Nothing in excess* <note>original Latin: "Ne quid nimis"</note>; which is a saying that all excess is vicious and evil; never forgetting the saying of Cleobulus: *Moderation is best* <note>original Latin: "Mediocritas optimum"</note>. He must also note that pastimes and recreations should be few, honest, and at their proper time; measured with the yardstick of reason, and so composed that they do not untune the concerted music of life. For this we have the advice of Marcus Tullius Cicero, who says: *We do not grant children every license to play, but only that which is not foreign to honest actions. For that which is becoming is honest, and that which is honest is becoming* <note>original Latin: "Pueris non omnem licentiam ludendi damus; sed eam, quæ ab honestatis actionibus non sit aliena. Nam & quod decet, honestum est; & quod honestum est, decet."</note>.

<margin>In the 1st book of his Duties.</margin>
<margin>Virtuous leisure and vicious leisure.</margin>
Possessing these qualities, games and pastimes will be lawful, not insofar as they are idle, but insofar as they give strength to alleviate the labors of study, the continuation of which (as has been said) human weakness cannot endure. And this leisure is called virtuous, in contrast to the other mentioned in the thirteenth chapter above, which is vicious.

<margin>Masters and parents.</margin>
<margin>Proverbs ch. 25.</margin>
<margin>On the education of children.</margin>
<margin>Book on moral virtues.</margin>
Let masters and parents take care, then, to permit some honest recreation to their children, and give them time to rest, and not press them so hard that they faint; attending to what the proverb says: *He who wrings the nose too hard brings forth blood* <note>original Latin: "Qui multum emungit, eiicit sanguinem." The author adapts this to an analogy about milking breasts.</note>; considering what Plutarch writes: *Leisure is the seasoning of labor* <note>original Latin: "Laboris condimentum est otium"</note>; and putting into practice what Seneca says: *A certain breathing space must be given to children from constant labors* <note>original Latin: "Ab assiduis laboribus danda est pueris quædam respiratio."</note>.

## Concerning the damages and evils caused by wine. Chapter XX.

<margin>Wine is more harmful than beneficial to the human race.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 12, 15 to 10.</margin>
<margin>Book 2, ch. 14 Divine Institutes.</margin>
<margin>Who invented wine, when and how.</margin>
<margin>Some claim Canaan planted it.</margin>
<margin>St. Jerome against Jovinian.</margin>
<margin>In the book on natural causes.</margin>
**I** do not know if it was for our benefit, or to our great detriment and harm, that the vine was transplanted and cultivated for mortals. For if we place in a just balance all the inconveniences and evils that wine brings with it, and in the other the benefits derived from it, we will undoubtedly recognize those to be incomparably more grave and pernicious than these others are useful to the human race. And so we see that the Creator of the world, knowing the vine to be more harmful than necessary to us, hid it from us when He produced the other plants, until the time of Noah; who, according to Holy Scripture and Lactantius Firmianus, planted a vineyard and was the inventor of wine. Consequently, he, before any other, began to feel its grave inconveniences. All this was in the time when men began to eat meat, which was shortly after the universal flood: namely, 1,662 years after the creation of the world according to the reckoning of the Hebrews, and 2,262 according to the reckoning of the Greeks. For that long the world remained without knowing what wine was, and without knowing its good and bad effects.

<margin>The method Noah saw for making wine.</margin>
Josephus, the ancient author, says that the method Noah used to make wine was that, having found the wild vine (commonly called the <term>brusca</term> <note>A wild, bitter grape vine</note>) and tasting that its grapes were unsavory, to sweeten them he took the blood of four animals: namely, a lion, a lamb, a pig, and an ape or monkey. Having mixed this, he poured it over the roots of the wild vine, and with this secret, it turned from bitter to sweet.

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of moderation in a student's life, advocating for a balance between rigorous study and virtuous leisure, before introducing a chapter on the history and dangers of wine, beginning with Noah's discovery.</summary>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Wine makes some of us strong like lions.</margin>
<margin>Others, gentle like lambs.</margin>
<margin>Others imitators, like monkeys.</margin>
<margin>Others lustful, like pigs.</margin>
<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
<margin>Alessandro Alessandri in the 3rd book of Genial Days. Proverb 31.</margin>
<margin>St. Augustine to the brothers in the Desert. Latin Proverb.</margin>
This story is not entirely out of place, for we know that its juice—so well known in all parts of the world—when taken in abundance makes men take on the nature of these four animals. For it makes some strong and angry like the lion; others gentle like lambs, because in that hour when they are occupied by wine, a tiny child can take a great giant by the nose—even one who acts like <term>Gradasso or Orlando Furioso</term><note>Characters from chivalric epics known for their immense strength and rage</note>, a "killer of all"—and lead and steer him as if he were a gentle lamb. Others, through the imagination they have of knowing something, at that point want to reveal that they know much; and by wanting to imitate everyone, and doing everything they see others doing before them, they make themselves so many monkeys; for their natural instinct is to mimic everything they see others do. Finally, others take on the nature of the pig, as every man knows; whose properties I leave in the black ink of my pen, so as not to martyrize pure and chaste ears. The Roman Censors used to cast out of the Senate those who drank much, and condemned them as infamous and unworthy of government, since: *Wisdom is obscured by wine* <note>original Latin: "Sapientia vino obumbratur"</note>; and the Athenians killed the Princes who became drunk. But this doctrine that Princes should not drink wine was first written by Solomon in his proverbs, saying: *Do not give wine to Kings: because there is no secret where drunkenness reigns: lest they drink, and forget the judgments, and change the cause of the children of the poor* <note>original Latin: "Noli Regibus dare vinum..."</note>. From here, with good reason, these profitable words are registered in the decrees: *It is foreign to a wise man to occupy himself with feasting, drinking, and drunkenness* <note>original Latin: "Alienum est à sapiente..."</note>; and it is because: *Drunkenness blinds the mind, and deprives it of right counsel and judgment. Wisdom is obscured by wine. A drunk pilot of a ship or of any other thing overturns everything, whether it be a ship, a carriage, an army, or whatever else is governed by him* <note>original Latin: "Ebrietas mentem obcaecat..."</note>.

---

<margin>Plato, Book 1 of the Laws.</margin>
<margin>The Romans kissed their wives to prove if they had drunk wine.</margin>
<margin>Temulentia, from where it was formed. Pliny book 14, chapter 13.</margin>
<margin>Domitius deprived a woman of her dowry for having drunk wine.</margin>
<margin>Valerius Maximus book 6. Alessandro Alessandri in the Genial Days.</margin>
<margin>Pierio in the Hieroglyphics. Egnatius killed his wife because she drank wine.</margin>
<margin>Papirius makes a vow to offer a cup of wine to Jupiter.</margin>
<margin>In the book of the Roman Triumph. Deeds of the Romans, chapter 18.</margin>
The use of wine was so hateful and reproached in the ancient Roman Republic that women were punished for having tasted it just as if they had committed an infamous adultery. As proof of this, husbands used to practice, upon coming in from outside, going to them mouth to mouth to smell and kiss them. This test they called *Temeto* <note>referring to "temetum," an archaic Latin word for intoxicating drink</note>; from which word the Latins took their term: *Temulentia*, which is drunkenness; and *Temulentus*, a drunken thing. And so we read in Pliny: that Gnaeus Domitius judged a woman who drank more wine than was appropriate in secret from her husband, depriving her of her dowry. In the same place one reads that Egnatius Mecenius killed his wife with a club because he found her drinking wine; he, according to the account given to us by Valerius Maximus, Alessandro Alessandri, and Pierio, was acquitted of the homicide by Romulus, the first King of the Romans. Because one of his laws was that the woman who drank wine should be punished with the same penalty as adultery; so says Andrea Fulvio in the book of the antiquities of Rome. It is true that in 그당시 there was not such an abundance of wine as there is now; then it was not found except in the apothecaries for certain necessities, and a little in the houses of some principal and powerful lords. It was held in such great esteem that once, when Lucius Papirius was about to give battle to the Samnites, he made a vow to offer to Jupiter a cup of wine (as a very precious thing) if he gave him the victory. The aforementioned law of Romulus lasted until the year 633 from the founding of Rome; at which time (as Biondo recounts) the custom of keeping wine in cellars began there; and even then they considered it an affront for anyone in good health to drink it. But in the event that men drank it out of some necessity, even so, it was in no way lawful for women to drink it; except in the time when they were in labor, to those who were of weak complexion, it was permitted to drink a little wine for the eight days after childbirth out of necessity; and this was by the particular consent of their husbands. And even if this liberty circulated among women, that German Emperor did not wish to consent to his wife drinking it; and it is said that when the doctor told him that because the Empress his wife did not drink wine, she did not become pregnant, and that he should let her drink it so that he would later have children to inherit the Empire, he said: *I would rather have a sterile wife than a drunk one*...

<margin>Evils caused by wine.</margin>
It would be well if people imitated in part that sobriety and abstinence of ancient times: and considered the damages that follow from the excessive drinking of wine: and determined to leave that so harmful abundance. Firstly, because too much [drinking...]

# Of Adornments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>Seneca in the book *On Anger*.</margin>
...wine produces an excessive heat, and excessive heat consumes the virtue of the body; furthermore, it is the root of angers, prides, cruelties, and many other evils. Certainly, no greater harm, misfortune, nor greater disaster can befall a well-born man than to go about stumbling, speaking a hundred thousand disorders and follies, revealing his secrets to those who do not ask for them, igniting himself in a voluntary fire, and letting himself go with a loose rein after every kind of vice. It causes evils not only in the body, but in morals as well; and therefore the Venerable Bede said: <margin>Bede.</margin> *It is not fitting for him who desires to be filled with the spiritual must <note>original: "musto spirituali"; "must" refers to unfermented grape juice, here symbolizing the Holy Spirit</note> to be intoxicated with wine, in which there is debauchery.* It is not proper (he says), nor is it lawful for one who desires to be filled with the new wine of the Holy Spirit, to get drunk on the wine in which there is lust. Saint Paul exhorts us to the same, saying: <margin>To the Ephesians, chapter 5.</margin> *Do not be drunk with wine, in which there is debauchery:* and in the divine letters we read the same with these words: <margin>Proverbs 20:</margin> *Wine is a luxurious thing, and drunkenness is tumultuous.* Wine is a lustful thing, and drunkenness is noisy and riotous. That wine excites dishonest lusts is affirmed by Euripides, saying: <margin>Euripides.</margin> *When wine is absent, Venus is not present;* <note>Venus is the Roman goddess of love, used here to represent sexual desire.</note> by taking away the wine, dishonest love is taken away: and in the Greek collection we read thus: <margin>Book 6 of the Deipnosophists.</margin> *Venus is a dead thing without Bacchus and Ceres.* <note>Bacchus is the god of wine; Ceres is the goddess of agriculture/grain.</note> Cynicus, according to Athenaeus, relates the same sentiment in these verses:

-> *For in an empty belly there is no love of beauty;* <-
-> *Bitter is Venus to those whom dire hunger presses.* <-

And a little further down he adds this verse of Euripides, taken from Achaeus the Satirist:
-> *Venus is present to the full man, but to the starving man she is not present at all.* <-

<margin>Ibid. Book 2.</margin>
The aforementioned Athenaeus, in the first book of the *Deipnosophists*, relates a distich <note>A pair of poetic lines.</note> in which the same sentiment is comprised, saying:

-> *Venus is present to the satisfied, but to the unhappy* <-
-> *and miserable mortals, no Venus is present.* <-

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
From whence came the proverb: *Without Ceres and Bacchus, Venus freezes;* without Ceres and Bacchus, Venus grows cold; where by Ceres is understood eating, by Bacchus drinking, and by Venus incontinence. And because wine is that which maintains lust, for this reason Aristophanes called wine the milk and nourishment of Venus: saying in this manner; <margin>In Athenaeus, book 10.</margin> *Wine is sweet to the drinker, the milk of the Cyprian Goddess.* <margin>Wine is the milk of Venus.</margin> The ancients meant to signify the same when they said: That no one should plant a vineyard while the Moon is in the sign of Virgo, for it is contrary to the vines: giving to understand that chastity has no friendship with wine. <margin>What it signifies to not plant a vineyard in the sign of Virgo.</margin> Aristotle, in the book *On Sleep and Waking*, says: <margin>Aristotle, On Sleep and Waking.</margin> *Wine impels men toward insolence:* and Saint Augustine, in *On Sobriety and Virginity*, writes: <margin>Augustine, On Sobriety and Waking.</margin> *Drunkenness in both sexes always craves all evils, and commits unspeakable acts:* more to the point in the same place he says, writing in this manner: *Drunkenness cannot be a companion to shame, because chastity was never a companion of intoxication. And more particularly, when he said: Through wine chastity is submerged, and many women have been deceived by corrupters; many also, while wine was powerful, forgot their legitimate marriage beds and surrendered their modesty to adulterers, and even plotted with paramours for the death of their husbands.* <margin>Take note. Chapter 2 of Ecclesiastes.</margin> Solomon, divinely enlightened, says to this purpose: *I thought in my heart to withdraw my flesh from wine, so that I might transfer my soul to wisdom.* I proposed (he says) in my heart to separate myself from wine, to give my soul to wisdom. There is no doubt but that in wine reason is drowned, and wit suffers shipwreck, since drunkenness neither says nor does anything moderately and with consideration: from whence came the proverb; <margin>Proverb from Athenaeus, book 10, on drinking.</margin> *Wine lacks a nail;* <note>original: "clavo," meaning a rudder or tiller in this nautical metaphor.</note> meaning that wine has no rudder or helm; because those who drink much lack governance and prudence. And it is because drunkenness extinguishes the light of the rational soul, and with it the beastly power is strengthened; <margin>The reason why drunkards lack prudence and governance.</margin> and therefore the body remains like a ship in the middle of the sea without a pilot, or like a squadron of soldiers without a Captain: and so when intemperate men drink wine, it can more properly be said that the wine drinks them, rather than they the wine: for it swallows and steals their senses and makes them lose the essence of men. Not only from youths and common people does it take the senses and make them lose their judgment; but sometimes also from the very prudent and very wise; (and they let themselves be overcome all the more easily, the more advanced they are in days;

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Plutarch.

...than by the authority of Plutarch: <note>original: "Senes facilius quam iuuenes ebrij fiunt"</note> *Old men become drunk more easily than young men*; and this we know by everyday experience, and by the authority of Ecclesiasticus, which says: <note>original: "Vinum & mulieres apostatare faciunt sapientes; In toga faltantis personam inducit ebrietas"</note> *Wine and women make wise men fall away; drunkenness brings out the character of a dancer even in a formal gown.* The Latins say sententiously: <note>original: "Vinum senem etiam vel nolentem, saltare compellit"</note> *Wine compels an old man to dance, even against his will.* This is taken from that verse by Athenaeus:

->It is rightly said in the ancient proverb,<-
->That wines persuade the elderly, O father,<-

<margin>Book 1 of the Laws.
Genesis chapter 9.</margin>
So that here we can say: <note>original: "Itaque vt videtur, non solum senex fuerit bis puer, verum etiam temulentus"</note> *And so it seems, not only is an old man a child twice over, but even more so when he is intoxicated.* Furthermore, it uncovers things that would be better left covered; and this (says a modern author) is what Holy Scripture intended to signify when it said in Genesis that Noah, overcome by wine, lay stretched out in his tabernacle; because wine reveals shameful things that ought to be kept secret. With the heat of the wine, a man loosens his tongue to say whatever he fancies, and after the tongue goes the heart, for as Euripides said: <note>original: "Vina produnt pectora"</note> *Wines betray the breast*; wine is the manifester of the heart; and Ephippus says:

->The great power of wine forces you to speak much,<-
->Therefore they say that drunks speak the truth.<-

Seneca Epistle 84.
The cunning used by Captain Bonosus.
Latin Proverb.
Seneca Epistle 84.
Augustine on Sobriety and Virginity.
Truth is heard from three.</margin>
And thus we are more than certain that: <note>original: "Nullum secretum est ubi regnat ebrietas"</note> *There is no secret where drunkenness reigns*; where there is much drinking, there is no secrecy. Just as the tempestuous wind stirs and disturbs the sea, showing the deep abysses: so too does excessive wine madden and confound the drinker, making him discover the secrets from the depths of his heart. The proverb says: <note>original: "Quod in corde sobrij, id in lingua ebrij"</note> *What is in the heart of the sober man is on the tongue of the drunkard.* Plato says: <note>original: "Vinum & cum pueritia & sine pueritia est veridicum"</note> *Wine, both with childhood and without childhood, is a teller of truth.* Seneca writes: <note>original: "Cum vis nimia vini possedit animum, quicquid male latebat, emergit"</note> *When the excessive power of wine possesses the soul, whatever was evilly hidden emerges*; and Plutarch: <note>original: "Vinum ad loquendum lacessit, & inter loquendum multa aperiuntur, ac deteguntur alias occulta"</note> *Wine provokes speaking, and during the speaking many things are opened and uncovered that were otherwise hidden.* Seneca on another occasion said: <note>original: "Mero onerati quemadmodum non retinent vino redundante; ita ne secretum quidem quod suum est & alienum pariter effundunt"</note> *Those burdened with pure wine, just as they do not hold back the overflowing wine, so they pour out secrets, both their own and those of others alike.* Captain Bonosus <note>A 3rd-century Roman usurper known for his incredible capacity to drink without losing his wits, using it to spy on others.</note> knew this trait very well; he was accustomed, when ambassadors of the Barbarians came to him, to banquet them magnificently and give them excellent wines, so that warmed by them, they would reveal to him everything they held in their breasts. This knowledge has gone so far, and is so manifest to all, that it is now said proverbially: <note>original: "In vino veritas"</note> *In wine there is truth*; that the truth is in the wine. And well considered, it is no extraordinary thing, for we know that: <note>original: "Animus ebrietate deuictus, non est in sua potestate"</note> *A mind conquered by drunkenness is not in its own power.* Saint Augustine approves the same with the very same words, saying: *He who is conquered by drunkenness cannot be in his own power*; and in conclusion they say that nowadays the truth is not heard except from three types of people, which are: *Children, drunks, and madmen.*

<margin>Athenaeus 14. Ginosophists.
Why Dionysus is depicted as mad.</margin>
In addition to all that has been said, they have a certain quality, which is that as they are touched and heated by wine, they incite riots and rumors, appearing like Steropes, Brontes, and Pyracmon: Cyclopes and ministers in the forge of Vulcan, god of fire, who fashions the lightning bolts for Jupiter and arms for the other gods. For this reason, Athenaeus the Philosopher in the 14th book of his *Ginosophists* <note>Likely a reference to the Gymnosophists or a section of the Deipnosophistae.</note> proposes this question: for what cause is Dionysus (who is Bacchus) depicted as mad by the Poets? To which question he himself responds in the first chapter with these words, saying: <note>original: "Dyonisium amice Tymocrates insanientem complures idcirco finxerunt, quod y qui vino immoderatius vtantur, tumultuosi fiant"</note> *My friend Tymocrates, many have depicted Dionysus as insane for the reason that those who use wine immoderately become tumultuous.* The same was also touched upon by Ovid, and with much grace, speaking in this manner:

<margin>Ovid.</margin>
->Beware especially of quarrels stimulated by wine,<-
->And hands too easily moved to fierce wars.<-

# Concerning Adornments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 31.</margin>
<margin>Ibid.</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 23.</margin>
<margin>Ibid.</margin>
That it makes men angry is certified to us by the author of Ecclesiasticus <note>Also known as Sirach</note>, saying: *Much wine drunk causes irritation, and anger, and many ruins.* <note>original: "Vinum multum potatum, irritationem, & iram, & ruinas multas facit."</note> Much wine drunk creates indignation, wrath, and many evils. And elsewhere: *Much wine drunk is bitterness of the soul.* <note>original: "Amaritudo animæ vinum multum potatum"</note> Solomon writes in the Proverbs: *To whom is woe? to whose father is woe? to whom are ditches? to whom are wounds without cause? to whom is bloodshot eyes? Is it not to those who linger over wine and study desirable chalices?* <note>original: "Cui vœ? cuius patri vœ? cui foueæ? cui sine causa vulnera? cui suffossio oculorum? nonne bis qui commorantur in vino & student calicibus optandis?"</note> He says: where are misfortunes found? Who is the father who lives without them? Where are quarrels, and pits to fall into, and wounds without cause, and blurred vision, if not in those who give themselves much to wine? Furthermore he says, and it is this: *Your eyes shall behold strange things, and your heart shall speak perverse things:* <note>original: "Oculi tui videbunt extraneas, & cor tuum loquetur peruersa"</note> and it is because drunkenness makes one thing appear as another, and shows two where there is but one. From here arose the proverb: *From two he sees three*, <note>original: "E duobus tria videt"</note> which is always said to mean that one is a drunkard or cross-eyed. Here that verse of Virgil <note>Publius Vergilius Maro</note> harmonizes well:

*And the sun appeared double, and Thebes showed itself twice:* <note>original: "Et Solem geminum, & duplices se ostendere Thebas"</note> nor does that other verse from Juvenal disagree:

*And the table rises up with double lamps.* <note>original: "Et geminis consurgere mensa lucernis"</note>

<margin>Herodotus.</margin>
<margin>Xenophon.</margin>
Herodotus says to this purpose: *When wine descends into the body, evil words and insanities are brought forth.* <note>original: "Vino in corpus descendente mala verba, & insanientia educuntur."</note> Likewise the wise Xenophon, needing to give healthy advice to the great Captain Agesilaus regarding temperance and abstinence from wine, said: *Abstain from drunkenness as if from insanity;* <note>original: "Abstine ab ebrietate atque abinsania"</note> making no difference between a drunkard and a madman; because the vapors of the wine, rising to the brain, take away a man's sight, his knowledge, and his judgment: and in an instant it oppresses all the most noble powers of the rational soul.

<margin>St. Ambrose in the book on Fasting.</margin>
<margin>Plutarch on Garrulity.</margin>
<margin>What drunkenness is.</margin>
<margin>Seneca, Epistle 84.</margin>
<margin>Ammianus, Book 15.</margin>
<margin>Plato, Dialogue 9 of the Republic.</margin>
St. Ambrose touches upon this property very aptly, saying: *When they are drunk they dispute about continence; where each one recounts his battles, there he preaches of brave deeds; soaked in wine and dissolved in sleep, he knows not in his mind what his tongue brings forth:* <note>original: "Cum ebrij fuerint de continentia disputãt..."</note> and Plutarch says: *A drunkard is talkative, for he lacks mind and prudence.* <note>original: "Ebrius est loquax, mente enim ac prudentia caret."</note> Drunkenness, according to Cato, is a species of voluntary madness: Seneca said the same in his 84th epistle: *Drunkenness is nothing (he says) but voluntary insanity.* <note>original: "Nihil est ebrietas quam voluntaria insania."</note> Ammianus Marcellinus and Plato say that: *A drunken man carries a tyrannical mind:* <note>original: "Vir ebrius tyrannicum gerit animum"</note> the drunken man carries within himself a tyrannical spirit. Juvenal expresses another great harm in his first Satire, saying:

> From here come sudden deaths, and an intestate old age:
> A new story goes about, not a sad one, through the anointed elders:
> A funeral to be applauded by angry friends is spoken of.

From here was born that well-known saying, *That a man at the table does not grow old;* This is not to say that while he eats and drinks his days and years do not increase as he advances in age, as the idle and "good companions" <note>A sarcastic reference to heavy drinkers</note> interpret it; but rather because by being at the table all day eating, drinking, and debauching <term>crapulando: from 'crapula', the sickness or hangover resulting from excessive drinking</term>, he dies quickly; and he who dies young does not grow old.

<margin>Plutarch on Preserving Health.</margin>
<margin>Augustine on Fever and Virginity.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus, chapter 37.</margin>
That this is so can be gathered from what Galen, Plutarch, Saint Augustine, and Solomon wrote. Galen says: *Gluttons can neither live long, nor be healthy.* <note>original: "Gulosi nec viuere possunt diu, nec sani esse."</note> Plutarch says: *Drunkards are like old men, and for this reason those devoted to wine grow old quite early, and baldness and gray hair occupy them before their time.* <note>original: "Ebrÿ sunt similes senibus..."</note> Saint Augustine wrote more clearly, saying: *He who, in constant madness, day and night, slumbers or drinks while reclining at the table, though still placed in this life, is buried daily.* <note>original: "Qui assidua amentia diu noctuque discumbendo dormitat aut bibit..."</note> And Solomon much more so, for he says briefly and determinately: *Because of debauchery many have died.* <note>original: "Propter crapulam multi obierunt."</note> Therefore, he who desires to eat much, let him eat little: for by eating little he shall eat much <note>Meaning he will live longer to enjoy more meals over a lifetime</note>.

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Plato, Laws, on power.</margin> The depraved live only to eat and drink; but good men eat and drink so that they may live. <note>original: "morati ideo viuunt, vt edant & bibant..."</note> The first point concerns those idolatrous eaters: *Whose God is their belly;* <note>Philippians 3:19</note> who live to eat; who (to use the common proverb) are accustomed to fill themselves <margin>Athanasius, On Virginity.</margin> *Up to both ears;* and who do not wish to understand that: *The sum of wine, food, and sleep should be small;* they do not wish to approve that: *Temperance will provide good bodily health more easily than medicine;* they do not wish to know that: <margin>Pythagoras.</margin> *Sobriety heals diseases, dries up catarrhs, puts demons to flight, expels wicked thoughts, renders the mind clearer, makes the heart pure, sanctifies the body, and finally places man before the throne of God.* <margin>Comparison.</margin> To tell the truth, I never laugh with more pleasure and sincerity than when I hear that these Epicureans and gluttons are tucked between two sheets, with their room adorned with enemas and basins, along with everything else that is usually seen and smelled on such occasions. And it is because just as pilots, who out of greed overload the ship, must afterwards be continually at the pump, drawing out and throwing away the seawater that the vessel has taken on; so also men, who out of gluttony stuff, refill, and overload the body, afterwards always have work to do in purging and evacuating it; sometimes resorting to the help of enemas and medicines to clean themselves thoroughly.

<margin>Note.</margin> It is true that just as some sailors are grieved when there is a storm to be wasting time in port; and after the wind forces them back into it, they stay there with more shame, vomiting and shouting; so those who beforehand feel and see the illness of the body, and consider it a shame and weakness to stay in bed for a day, and not sit at the table as they usually do, observing a little abstinence and diet; afterwards they stay for many days lying in bed with more shame, while they are purged and anointed and have plasters applied. And what is more laughable, they flatter, respect, and sometimes (oh, what pleasure and delight) with tears in their eyes, they beg the doctors not to restrain them when they ask for wine or cold water: doing or saying many shameful and nonsensical things, either out of pain or out of fear.

<margin>In the beginning.</margin> But let them do as they wish, for they will have to leave it sooner than they think; to whom I say now with the Prophet Joel: *Weep and howl, all you who drink wine in sweetness, for it has perished from your mouth;* <note>Joel 1:5</note> and perhaps while holding the cup in one hand and the flask in the other, the pain they feel will be all the greater; whereby they will come to prove that: <margin>Proverb.</margin> *There is much distance between the mouth and the morsel;* or as the Greeks say: *Many things fall between the cup and the upper lips.* We have many examples of learned men and great and courageous Princes whose heroic virtues were greatly obscured by this black vice of wine, which, taking them out of themselves, compelled them to say and do a thousand base things and commit a thousand enormities. One of these <margin>In the Life of Aesop. Xanthus told the philosopher he would drink the whole sea.</margin> was told of that great philosopher Xanthus; which is that while at the table with some of his disciples, and having his eyes already lit with the sweet fire of Bacchus, he said (oh, what foolishness!) that he alone would have drunk the entire sea; wagering with one of them a poor little house he owned if he did not drink it. Both wagered on this, putting up their rings as a sign; although later, through the advice and cunning of his servant Aesop, he was freed from the error with his honor and reputation intact.

<margin>Alexander the Great kills his friends.</margin> Also Alexander the Great, when he was taken by wine, with a very beastly fury over the slightest little occasion, amidst flasks and cups, would kill his greatest and most intimate friends; over whom, after returning to his senses, he would weep bitterly: watering them with infinite tears: and he remained most sorrowful for having taken the lives of those who, for him, had risked death. For this reason, Anacharsis <note>The text says "Antrocide," likely referring to the philosopher Anacharsis or Androcydes.</note>, a very wise and learned man, was moved to write him this healthy reminder: *King, when about to drink wine, remember that you drink the blood of the earth: just as hemlock is poison to man, so is wine.* <margin>Belshazzar, King of Babylon, is deprived of life.</margin> If we wish to give faith to ancient histories, the son of that most courageous Queen Tomyris, buried in wine and sleep, in an instant lost himself and his entire army. Belshazzar, King of Babylon, would not have been deprived of his life and kingdom if on that night in which Kings Cyrus and Darius killed him, <margin>Hannibal defeats the Africans with adulterated wine.</margin> he had been sober. Hannibal, a skillful and crafty Captain, defeated the violent Africans with nothing else but wine infected with the juice of the mandrake. And in the first book of the Maccabees we read in this manner: *And when Simon was drunk...*

# On Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

...and his sons, Ptolemy rose up with his men, and they took up their arms, and entered into the banquet, and slew him, and his two sons, and certain of his servants.
<margin>1 Maccabees, chapter 16.</margin>
<margin>Muhammad forbade wine to the Turks.</margin>
Wherefore (these things being known, and the dangers of wine recognized, and how easily Monarchies are lost because of its powerful vapors) that Muhammad, capital enemy of the Holy Christian name, forbade wine to his followers: and this not so much out of respect for religion (from which he was very far removed) as because, being more a crafty soldier than a true prophet, he wished to make use of them in deeds of arms; for which drunkenness was not suitable, as he had already known by experience: it being more than certain (since the Turks are very sensual) that his wicked sect would quickly come to an end if they drank wine.

<margin>Plato ordained in his decrees that wine should not be drunk in the army.</margin>
Many lost their being and status, and allowed themselves to be defeated by their enemies most shamefully; for having been first conquered by wine, which relaxes the strengths of the body and weakens the virtue and vigor of the mind. Which the divine Plato, having well understood, ordained among his decrees that men of war should not drink wine in the army. Homer approves the same, introducing Hector, who speaks with Hecuba in this manner:

> O mother to whom reverence is due
> Do not present me those sweet wines;
> Nor wish to blunt my potency,
> The strength, and the vigor, and the excellence
> Of the mind and the body so divine.

<margin>Emperors who were friends of drunkenness.</margin>
In everything contrary to Hector was Nero Tiberius, of whom it is found written that he was such a friend and such a seeker of drunkenness, that for this cause they spoke of him insultingly; which is that instead of saying, *Tiberius Claudius Nero*; they said, *Biberius Caldius Mero* <note>A Latin pun: "Biberius" from *bibere* (to drink), "Caldius" from *calidus* (hot), and "Mero" from *merum* (undiluted wine).</note>. Also so great was the liveliness <note>Used ironically here to mean "notorious reputation."</note> of the Emperor Bonosus, that it commonly came into a proverb in everyone's mouth: *Bonosum natum esse non ut vivat, sed ut bibat* <note>Latin: "That Bonosus was born not that he might live, but that he might drink."</note>.

<margin>Fr. Silvio in the Gryphon of Anthony.</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 31.</margin>
See here the honor that excessive wine does us: which being well considered, we can ask with the wise man: *Quae vita est ei, qui minuitur vino?* <note>Latin: "What life is there for him who is diminished by wine?" (Ecclesiasticus 31:33).</note> But there is more to say: Of Alexander the Great himself it is said, that after having captured Persepolis, a most celebrated city in Asia, one night over supper all full of wine, to satisfy the pleas of a certain famous harlot named Thais, who followed his army, he permitted her with a lit torch to set fire to that most noble and so celebrated royal house of Xerxes, mistress of all the East, and in which so many Kings and Princes had been raised.

<margin>Alexander the Great and Thais the harlot, both drunk, set fire to the royal house of Xerxes.</margin>
And not only did he consent to her doing such great wickedness, but he himself, made into a wineskin <note>A metaphor for being completely soaked in drink.</note> and falling all over himself following the drunken woman, with another torch in hand, helped to ignite that most ancient structure, which together with the city was thus converted into ash. Many other wicked deeds, besides the few I have mentioned, have been done by men who are friends of drunkenness, which are written at length in the works of both ancient and modern writers for our instruction. And all of them, not only with words but even with paintings, wished to warn us of the grave inconveniences that arise because of wine: to which painted image they gave the name Bacchus, which signifies nothing other than fury; and this because it makes furious those who take it without measure, and it is an ordinary passion that is discovered in drunkards.

<margin>Bacchus: what he signifies and how he is painted.</margin>
<margin>With horns.</margin>
<margin>With a horn in hand.</margin>
Some Gentiles, to make known more clearly the braveness and fury of much drinking, painted him with the horns of a wild bull. Others added a horn in his hand to give it to be understood that the drunkard, in the manner of a trumpet, makes his secrets reach the ears of others.

<margin>Naked. A boy.</margin>
They paint him naked, because whoever is touched by wine reveals everything and has nothing hidden. They paint him as a boy because his devotees are always without thought and without shame or discretion, like boys: and also because it makes men stutter and without distinct speech like children.

<margin>In a coach.</margin>
Drawn in a cart or coach, because in drunkards is found an instability and volubility, their brains turning continually like the wheels of the coach.

<margin>Crowned with ivy.</margin>
<margin>With a crown made of vine leaves.</margin>
With ivy on his forehead as a crown; because just as ivy breaks very old walls, so drunkards are ready for every sort of rupture, breaking very old friendships. Finally, others paint him crowned with vine leaves, to give it to be understood, that

H

<meta>This page continues the moral discourse on the dangers of wine and the virtue of temperance, following the previous discussion on the physical and social defects caused by drunkenness.</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Remedy against drunkenness.</margin>
...that intoxicated men returned to their senses with them. For just as the bite of a dog is healed with the hairs of the same animal that caused the harm <note>A reference to the folk belief "hair of the dog."</note>: so, neither more nor less, with grapevine leaves eaten raw and applied over the forehead and temples, drunkenness passes and is digested quickly, however grave and heavy it may be.

<margin>Remedies to avoid falling into the inconveniences caused by wine.</margin>
<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
<margin>On Psalm 36.</margin>
Now that we have seen the many defects and the diverse inconveniences that occur because of wine, what remedy is there to avoid falling into them? I see no better way than what we commonly tend to do in all things that are, or seem to us, difficult and harsh. For when the laborer refuses the toil of the work, and the merchant fears the dangers of navigation, and the soldier those of war, to force and encourage themselves, they usually place before them the interest of profit, and with this they throw themselves into the labors and dangers of life. And in this manner, using the proverb: *Clauum clauo pellere* <note>original: "Drive out one nail with another."</note>, with one nail they pull out the other; which is to say, one affection with another affection: because with the love of profit, they conquer the fear of labor: to which the saying of Saint Augustine warns us: *Si vis sustinere laborem, attende mercedem* <note>original: "If you wish to endure the labor, look to the reward."</note>. Well, in this manner we must govern ourselves here, each one keeping before the eyes of his understanding the principal harms of this black vice: so that with the fear for one's honor and estate, the delight of taste may be conquered: and thus principally we must give ear to the advice given to us by that great wise man in Ecclesiastes, saying; *Sanitas est anima & corporis sobrius potus* <note>original: "Sober drinking is health to soul and body."</note>: and in the Proverbs, where in one part it says; *Qui diligit epulas, in egestate erit, qui amat vinum & pingua, non ditabitur* <note>original: "He who loves banquets shall be in want; he who loves wine and fat things shall not be enriched."</note>. He who is a friend of feasts will live in poverty; and he who seeks delicate morsels and precious wines will never grow rich. In another part it says; *Noli esse in conuiuijs potatorum, nec in comessationibus eorum, qui carnes aduescendum conferunt: quia vacantes potibus & dantes symbola consumentur, & vestietur pannis dormitatio* <note>original: "Be not among the feasts of drinkers, nor in the revelries of those who bring meats to eat: because those who give themselves to drinks and social gatherings shall be consumed, and drowsiness shall be clothed in rags."</note>. Do not find yourself (he says) in the feasts of those who are friends of drinking wine and eating meat: that is to say, of the great drunkards; because those who give themselves to this vice, and here waste their estate, shall be confounded; and the sleep and laziness of such men shall end in poverty. Saint Augustine says the same; *Immoderatus vini excessus, & diuitibus pauperes reddidit & egenos* <note>original: "Immoderate excess of wine has made even the rich poor and needy."</note>.

<margin>Eccl. chapter 31.</margin>
<margin>Prov. c. 21.</margin>
<margin>Ibid. 23.</margin>
<margin>On sobriety & virginity.</margin>
<margin>Prov. 32.</margin>
<margin>Custom of the Lacedaemonians so that their children would loathe wine.</margin>
<margin>Cicero book 2.</margin>
<margin>Plautus.</margin>
<margin>Potentius.</margin>
<margin>Tibullus.</margin>
<margin>Learning from the misfortunes of others.</margin>
But give us another warning so that we do not let ourselves be deceived by wine so easily; it says then in the same place: *Ne intuearis vinum quando flauescit, cum splenduerit in vitro color eius: ingreditur blandè, sed in nouissimo mordebit vt coluber, & sicut regulus venena diffundet* <note>original: "Look not upon the wine when it is golden, when its color sparkles in the glass: it enters smoothly, but at the last it will bite like a snake, and spread its poison like a basilisk."</note>. It means to say: Do not look at the wine when it is golden, and when its color shines in the glass: because at the time of drinking it seems mild, but in the end it bites like a snake, and pours out its poison like a basilisk. Finally, it will be of much benefit to us to imitate that most laudable custom of the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note>; who not only kept their children away from wine, but also made the most wine-soaked slaves of the whole house, and those most inclined to drunkenness, appear sometimes before the table: whom they allowed to drink there until they were beside themselves, and after they were very drunk, they performed a thousand mocks and shames upon them, so that their children, seeing the ugliness and misfortune of the wretched ones, would flee from falling into similar ignominy. Well, we also for the same effect must do the same regarding the consideration and seeing of others' ugliness, reflecting it upon our own: for it is the greatest prudence for a man to take advantage of the cases of others, to be able to help himself in his own. *Decet ab aliorum erratis* (says M. T. <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note>) *suam vitam in melius instruere* <note>original: "It is fitting to instruct one's own life for the better from the errors of others."</note>: and then we profit from the madness and foolishness of others, when it serves us as instruction and warning. From here Plautus said; *Fœliciter sapit is, qui alieno periculo sapit* <note>original: "He is happily wise who is wise through another's danger."</note>: and Ann. Seneca in the proverbs: *Ex vitio alterius sapiens suum emendat* <note>original: "From the vice of another, the wise man corrects his own."</note>. Potentius in the 3rd book says:

> Happy is he whom the dangers of others make cautious.

And Tibullus in the 3rd of his Elegies, says in this other manner:

> -------happy is whoever, through the pain
> Of another, learns to be able to do without your own.

---

From whence the ancients made that most elegant proverb: *Optimum est aliena frui insania* <note>original: "It is best to profit from the insanity of others."</note>: because every wise man learns from the harm of others, from what cloth he ought to line his cloak; and from the fire lit in another's house, each one sees how from the heat...

<meta>This page continues Cerone's discourse on the physical and mental requirements for a musical composer, specifically focusing on the effects of diet and wine on the intellect.</meta>

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>Composers should take note.</margin>
...to the head certain thick and heavy vapors: which obfuscate and darken those virtues of our soul (which are called animal <note>In the medieval and Renaissance sense, "animal spirits" were the fluids or vapors that mediated between the soul and the body's senses.</note>) that serve the understanding in its operation. Through this, the understanding comes to work more imperfectly due to the defect of the instruments it must use for this purpose. Along with this, there is another reason that is easier to understand, and it is this: First, because that excess of wine occupies a good part of the soul's power in the work of digestion; in which nature itself, as if by justice, demands its right, and requires that all the power be employed for the time being in that work so necessary for life. From this arises the fact that men find themselves so heavy after they have exceeded in eating and drinking, for any task of study and attention. Second, because the very fumes and vapors of the food, like from a boiling pot, rise to the brain (where the seat of the powers that serve the work of contemplation, necessary for a Composer, is located) and cover all that part as if with a dark mist, which hinders the operation of those powers, and consequently that of the understanding, which makes use of them. To this purpose, Saint Jerome writes in an epistle: *A fat belly does not produce a thin sense.* <note>original: "Pinguis venter non gignit sensum tenuem."</note>

<margin>A belly full of sustenance does not engender a slender understanding. Greek Proverb.</margin>
Plutarch, regarding Aulus Gellius, goes on to say: *Boys with much food turn out to be of a stupider and slower disposition.* <note>original: "Pueros multo pastu stupidioris, ac tardioris ingenij euadere."</note> And Galen, against Thrasybulus, says: *And this is sung by almost all men, because it is the truest of all: That an obese belly does not produce a subtle mind.* <note>original: "Et hoc, ab omnibus prope hominibus canitur... Quod venter obesus non gignit mentem subtilem."</note> From whence was born that sentence of the Greeks: *An obese belly does not give birth to a subtle heart.* <note>original: "Subtile pectus venter obesus non parit."</note> A Poet gathered all this together, saying:

->That which satiety brings forth &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; From eating until the body is gorged<-
->Is either lust or laziness; &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; It is crippled, dull, and heavy<-
->Drunkenness and clumsiness &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; The intellect entangled,<-
->From the works of nature. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; And the appetite awakened.<-

And note that I have always said that an immoderate quantity of both food and wine (for all is sustenance) obscures and clouds the wit of the Composer, and not tempered wine. Rather, I say with the Poet Horace:

<margin>Horace, Epistles, Book 1, 19.</margin>
<margin>The compositions of those who drink water can be worth little. 2. Deipnosophists.</margin>

> *No poems can please for long, nor live,*
> *Which are written by water-drinkers -----------* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **also**

Juvenal says:
> *But if a boy and a tolerable lodging were lacking*
> *To Virgil, all the hydras would fall from his hair,*
> *The silent trumpet would groan nothing grave.* <note>A reference to Virgil's Aeneid; suggesting that without comfort and wine, even the greatest poet's inspiration would fail.</note>

Horace attributes poetic eloquence to wine, saying:

> *Whom have the flowing cups not made eloquent?* <note>original: "Fæcundi calices quem non fecere disertum?"</note> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Martial does**

the same,
> *I can do nothing sober; when I drink,*
> *fifteen poets come to my aid.* <note>original: "Possum nil ego sobrius, bibenti / Succurrunt mihi quindecim poetæ."</note>

<margin>Composers must drink wine to make good works.</margin>
Know then that for Poets and versifiers, wine drunk abundantly is favorable: for testimony of this we have the said Horace, who in various places made mention of this: once he says thus.

<margin>Epistle 59.</margin>
> *As soon as he decreed this, the poets did not cease*
> *To compete with wine by night, and to stink of it by day.* <note>original: "Hoc simul edixit, non cessauere poetæ / Nocturno certare mero, putere diurno."</note>

another time thus.
> *The sweet Muses usually smell of wine in the morning:*
> *By his praises of wine, Homer is proved a wine-drinker,* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **and another in**

this other way.
> *It is told that even the virtue of ancient Cato*
> *Was often warmed by wine.* <note>original: "Narratur, & prisci Catonis / Sæpe mero incaluisse virtus."</note>

<margin>Father of the poets. Horace, as above.</margin>
And Ennius, father of the poets, never went to sing of arms except after having drunk moderately: Listen to Horace.
> *Father Ennius himself never leaped forth to tell of arms*
> *Unless drunk; I will assign the forum and Libo's well* <note>The "Puteal Libonis" was a place in the Roman Forum associated with business and law, here contrasted with poetic inspiration.</note>
> *To the dry, and take away singing from the austere.*

<margin>Comparison.</margin>
Because just as plants are drowned with much and excessive water, and are raised and sustained with that which is tempered and necessary; but without it, they die and dry up: so our understanding is drowned and obscured with much and excessive drinking, and with that which is tempered and necessary it is sharpened and enlivened; but without it, it becomes foolish and dull. Listen to Boethius
<margin>On Scholastic Discipline, chapter 2.</margin>

# Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>To the brother in Herennius <note>original: "Ad fr. in Her." likely referring to the Rhetorica ad Herennium</note></margin>
**Roman:** *Wine taken in moderation sharpens the wit; but taken immoderately, it disturbs reason, dulls the intellect, weakens the memory, brings on forgetfulness, pours in error, and induces ignorance.* But because: *Wine and youth* (as Saint Augustine says) *are a double fire*, for this reason it should be forbidden to boys in every way; both watered down and pure. And this is not merely the custom of modern parents, but the doctrine of the ancient sages, such as Aristotle, who says:
<margin>Politics, Book 7</margin>
*It is good for children to be without wine.* And Galen:
<margin>On Preserving Health</margin>
*I would advise that children should not even taste wine, because it moistens too much, increases the heat of the body, and fills the head with vapors.* And Avicenna writes: *To give wine to children is like adding fire to fire upon weak wood.* Finally, Apuleius desires that:
<margin>On the regulation of water and wine, Florida 3</margin>
*Boys should remain entirely ignorant of the use of wine until their eighteenth year;* and he gives the reason, saying: *For one ought not to supply fire to fire in the body and soul before they have reached manhood and begin to undergo labors, for the furious temperament of youth must be guarded against.* This is said to the shame of those mothers who raise their children on milk and wine, giving them one breast of milk and another of wine; which later causes them to commonly turn out to be winos and drunkards. The rest concerning this matter is discussed in the chapter that follows.

---

## Concerning the benefits of wine: how it is necessary for the health of the human body; and other very important and necessary advice regarding this matter. Chapter XXI.

<margin>Good effects of wine.</margin>
**H**aving spoken of the many evils of wine—it being (as has been said) a voluntary poison when drunk without rule or given to those for whom it is not suited—it is well that we now turn the page and speak of the benefits that proceed from it. We affirm that when drunk with discretion and temperance, it is a very substantial and most healthy nourishment for the body, and likewise for the soul. For if we look closely at its effects and powers: it warms those who are cold, moistens those who are wasted away, fattens the thin, gives color to the pale, awakens dull and lazy wits, makes good poets and good composers, consoles the sad, cheers the melancholic, and makes grumbling old men good-natured. It is digested and distributed through the veins faster than all other things from which the body takes its refreshment; and in short, it is the unique support of human life.

<margin>Wine is a reconciler of friendships. Epistle 3.</margin>
Beyond the aforementioned graces, it has another worthy of being celebrated: wine is a reconciler of friendships (even though Seneca says in one of his epistles: *He errs who seeks a friend in the foyer and tests him at a banquet*; for that does not conflict with what we are saying). We have often seen two mortal enemies sit among other guests at a table, and after having toasted one another—though not from a good heart—at the end, love being kindled little by little by the wine and grudges being forgotten, they rise in great harmony and embrace tightly like bosom brothers.
<margin>Christ with his blood, under the appearance of wine, reconciled the human race with his Father.</margin>
Wherefore that omnipotent Creator of all things, wishing to join the hearts of his disciples into one, made his own blood from no other liquor but wine; through which he reconciled the entire human race with his Father.

<margin>A very useful saying of Cato. Ecclesiasticus 31. St. Augustine.</margin>
But let us leave all this now, and advise Musicians, Singers, and all kinds of people—I mean those to whom the name of drinkers and lovers of wine is attributed—that when they are invited to some place abundant in the "sweet tears of Bacchus," they remember that beautiful saying and profitable counsel of Cato: *Moderate yourself with wine* <note>original: "Vino te tempera"</note>; which says we must work toward sober and temperate drinking: tempering ourselves to the wine, and not (as some explain) merely mixing water into the wine.
<margin>When it was not permitted for the Gentiles to drink wine. What the Nephalia festivals are.</margin>
Furthermore, let them also remember what the Sage says: *Wine was created for joy, and not for drunkenness.* Saint Augustine writes the same in the treatise *On Sobriety and Virginity*: *The nature of wine* (he says) *was created for joy, not for drunkenness.* And note that in the sacrifices to Bacchus, the Gentiles celebrated "Nephalia" festivals (which means "watered festivals"), in which...
