<meta>This page continues the discussion on temperance and the physical effects of wine, likely following a section on the history of viticulture or ancient drinking customs.</meta>

# Of the Music of Cerone Book I.

<margin>Water drunk during a meal mitigates the strength of wine.</margin>
<margin>Alexander in his Problems.</margin>
<margin>3. flor.</margin>
<margin>Book 7. Natural History.</margin>
...for whom it was not lawful to drink wine? This was to accustom them to water, and to teach us that we should not always seek out wine. Water, not only when mixed with wine, but even if drunk by itself between helpings of wine, makes the wine less harmful. Thus, it is wise to accustom yourselves to drinking water one or more times during a meal, so that it may mitigate and soften the strength of the wine, and the body may become used to drinking water. Then, if a necessity arises, it will not cause illness, nor seem a strange thing, nor will the body refuse and reject it. There is another benefit, which is: as Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A Peripatetic philosopher who wrote commentaries on Aristotle</note> says, those who drink watered-down wine have their sight and senses more vivid than those who drink it pure. According to the opinion of Pliny, Staphylus <note>In Greek myth, the son of Dionysus; his name means "grape cluster"</note> son of Silenus showed how to water down wine so that it would not do harm; wherefore Plautus exhorts us that: *We should temper Bacchus, the drunken god, with the Nymphs, the sober goddesses.* <note>original: "Bacchum temulentum deum Nimphis sobriÿs deabus temperemus." This is a metaphor for mixing wine (Bacchus) with water (Nymphs).</note> The same is advised by the Greek Epigram of Meleager, speaking in this manner:

->*Bacchus, when as a boy he leaped from the flames,*<-
->*The Nymphs washed while he was still soiled with ash;*<-
->*Hence Bacchus is pleasing when joined with the Nymphs, but you will catch*<-
->*A burning fire, unless you mix him.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Bacchus, vt è flammis puer exiliÿsset..." This refers to the myth of Bacchus being born from fire and then cooled by water nymphs.</note>

<margin>In his Satires.</margin>
<margin>Wine that, being watered down, intoxicates, and why.</margin>
<margin>Watered wine enlivens the eyes and the other senses.</margin>
<margin>The drink of frogs is watered wine.</margin>
<margin>Quantities used by the ancients in tempering wine.</margin>
<margin>The amount of water that must be put in wine to temper it reasonably.</margin>
<margin>The method to be held in tempering wine so that it mixes better.</margin>
And if the proverb says: *You have ruined the wine by pouring in water* <note>original: "Perdidisti vinum infusa aqua"</note> (which, as Chamaeleon affirms, is taken from the Tragedy of Aristias, titled *Cyclopes*, where there is a saying that goes: *I have lost the liquor of the wine by pouring in water*), we must understand that it does not speak generally, but specifically of a sort of wine which, when drunk watered down, intoxicates. The reason is that when it is pure, being very full-bodied, its vapors are very heavy and thus cannot rise to the brain; but by watering it down, they become subtle, and consequently they rise to the head. Note that I say "watered wine" and not "wine-colored water"; for there is a great difference between ten parts of wine and two of water, versus ten of water and two of wine. And so, to those who temper the wine excessively, so that it seems they drink water and not wine—drinking according to the custom of frogs—one can say proverbially: *You serve wine to the frogs.* <note>original: "Ranis vinum preministras."</note> Hesiod, the Greek poet, wants three parts of water and one of wine. The most ancient Greeks, according to what Athenaeus writes, wanted five parts of water to be put into two parts of wine. Plutarch, in the ninth of the *Table Talk* questions of the 3rd book, wants two parts of wine to be put into three parts of water. But because today these rules would be very difficult to accept, it is necessary to mitigate them somewhat so they are received with greater will; and to do this we will flip the quantities—namely, those of the wine into those of the water, and those of the water into those of the wine—speaking in this manner: In three parts of wine, let two of water be put; that is to say, for every three cups of wine, two of water; and it will be a good tempering. It will be better putting two of water into five of wine; but much better, and more acceptable always, will be that in three parts of wine one puts one of water; referring myself in this to the <term>complexion</term> <note>The physical constitution or balance of "humors" in an individual</note> of each person. And it is to be noted that they did not temper the wine with water, but rather poured the wine over the water, Theophrastus affirming that in this way it mixes better; and it seems Pedro Mexía approves of this in the third part of his *Silva de varia lección* in chapter 12. Regarding this, one can say the same as the poet Euripides said, though to another purpose; and it is:

->I always hold your shelter<-
->But tempered to the taste,<-
->So that you may not fail me<-
->Nor I remain without you.<-

<margin>Let the married ones take note.</margin>
I do not know if it is out of reason to say immediately after this what our ancestors did not consign to oblivion: and it is that those who join with women to engender children should be totally free of wine; or at least they should have drunk it very temperately at the time of their union; because those whose parents happen to be drunk at the time they are engendered tend to be friends of wine and drunkards. For this reason, Diogenes, seeing a youth out of his mind and dazed by wine, said to him: "Youth, your father engendered you while being drunk"; so writes Plutarch in the precepts for the bringing up of children. The times one is to drink, it seems, should not be more than three, for as much as...

<summary>This page discusses the ancient practice of diluting wine with water for health and sobriety, citing various classical authorities on the proper ratios. It concludes with a moral warning regarding the effects of parental drunkenness on the character of conceived children.</summary>
<keywords>temperance, wine dilution, health, classical authorities, Plutarch, Pliny, procreation, sobriety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discourse on the moral implications of wine consumption, transitioning from the dangers of excess to the virtues of moderation and the symbolic relationship between Bacchus and the Lynx.</meta>

# Concerning Adornments and Moral Harmonies

<margin>How many times one must drink to drink in an orderly fashion.</margin>
The Greek poet Eubulus <note>original: "Eubolo"; a 4th-century BC writer of Middle Comedy</note> introduces Dionysus <note>The god of wine</note> saying: "To the prudent I shall give no more than three servings of wine; the first for health, the second for sweetness, the third for sleep; the rest will be disorder and drunkenness." All of this seems confirmed by Apuleius, who says: *The first cups pertain to thirst, the second to cheerfulness, the third to pleasure, the fourth to madness.* <note>original: "Prima pocula ad sitim pertinent, secunda ad hilaritatem, tertia ad voluptatem, quarta ad insaniam."</note> For a general conclusion of all that has been said on this matter, I say that wine is good and created by God, and that drinking is necessary and useful; and as David says: <margin>Psalm 103. Conclusion.</margin> *Wine gladdens the heart of man;* <note>original: "Vinum lætificat cor hominis"</note> it cheers <margin>Ecclesiasticus chapter 40.</margin> the heart of man: the book of Ecclesiasticus affirms the same, saying: *Wine and music rejoice the heart;* <note>original: "Vinum & musica lætificant"</note> but nevertheless, it is often accidentally the cause of diseases, mockery, and many other evils when taken in an immoderate <margin>Wine is naturally good, accidentally bad.</margin> and improper quantity: and even sometimes (as has been said) it is the cause of death, as the Holy Spirit told us in the same book of Ecclesiasticus. <margin>Ecclesiasticus chapter 37.</margin> *Because of surfeit (he says) many have died:* <note>original: "Propter crapulam multi obierunt"</note> and Propertius said: *By wine beauty perishes, by wine youth is corrupted:* <note>original: "Vino forma perit, vino corrumpitur ætas"</note> and that other author writes:
> *Be held by the love of neither Venus nor wine;*
> *For in one and the same way, wine and Venus cause harm.* <note>original: "Nec Veneris, nec tu vini tenearis amore; / Vno namque modo vina Venusque nocent." Venus here represents lust or sexual desire.</note>

<margin>Virgil on Venus and wine.</margin>
And Virgil, the celebrated poet, says to this purpose at the end of one of his epigrams:
> *As Venus unnerves the strength, so does an abundance of wine;*
> *And they challenge the gait, and weaken the feet.* and further down:
> *Wine settles thirst; let kindly Venus serve for the creation*
> *of children; to have leaped beyond these bounds is harmful.* <note>original: "Ut Venus enervat vires, sic copia vini; / Et tentant gressus, debilitantque pedes... Vina sitim sedent, natis Venus alma creandis / Serviat; hos fines transiluisse nocet."</note>

But so that it may maintain the health of our body, and rejoice our soul and heart, and cause good effects in us, it is necessary to drink it moderately, and Solomon warns us of this in his proverbs when he says: *Wine drunk moderately is the exultation of the soul and heart:* <note>original: "Exultatio animæ & cordis vinum moderate potatum"</note> and in Ecclesiasticus: <margin>Chapter 31.</margin> *Sober drinking is the health of soul and body:* <note>original: "Sanitas est animæ & corporis sobrius potus"</note> and Pythagoras approves that: *Wine taken in moderation makes men more cheerful.* <note>original: "Vinum mediocriter sumptum homines alacriores facit."</note> <margin>In the beginning of his Music.</margin> Finally, take note with Stefano Rossetti <note>original: "Esteuan Roseto"; a 16th-century Italian composer and writer</note>, and I say: *Food and drink taken in superabundance, or too meagerly, corrupt health; but moderate amounts cause, increase, and preserve it.* and he continues: *For nothing is so pleasant as food well digested; nothing so works for health or the sharpness of the senses. Nothing puts ailments to flight like moderate refreshment; for sufficiency with nourishment produces both safety and pleasure at once. But abundance brings troubles and gives birth to sickness. And so, fleeing the extremes, we approve that which is the middle and between both.* <note>original: "Cibi & potus superabundāter sumpti... Sicque extrema fugientes, illud quod medium est atque inter vtrumque, probamus."</note>

<margin>Addition.</margin>
Having learned the good effects that wine produces, and the metamorphoses of men who give themselves over with loose reins into the arms of Bacchus, and having learned how the ancients used to paint this monster, it will not be out of place—rather, it will be very reasonable—before we exit this so long and pleasant digression, that I should say for what cause the same ancients painted that animal called the Lynx alongside Bacchus: especially since today there are many and diverse persons who have need of knowing it and even more of putting it into practice. <margin>Natural property of the Lynx.</margin> Naturalists say that the Lynx has very sharp sight, and is an animal so forgetful that while grazing, if it happens to turn its eyes elsewhere, it forgets the pasture it has before it and goes to look for another; and thus by the Lynx they understood "forgetfulness": <margin>Why the ancients dedicated the Lynx to Bacchus.</margin> and to say that the ancients dedicated and attributed the lynx to Bacchus is to say that they dedicated and attributed forgetfulness to him. Giving it to be understood that it was not fitting for men to remember those things that were done or said while eating or drinking, heated by the wine: because they are not worthy of being done, nor of being remembered after they are done or said. Because among the flasks and cups (as the understanding is moved, half-turned, and perhaps entirely disturbed) men often say they will do things far removed from all reason, which, even if they are permitted then, it is not good that they remember them later to carry them out. <margin>Another significance.</margin> They also wished to signify by that, that the things friends and guests said to one another in banquets should not leave that place: because it is not good to reveal the things discussed there, since, altered by wine, they say things they later repent of: And so in such places it is not lawful to rebuke anyone, because of the danger that one of the parties is heated by wine; <margin>Chapter 31.</margin> *In a banquet of wine (says Ecclesiasticus) do not argue* <note>original: "In convivio vini... non argua"</note>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of moderation in drinking, citing classical and biblical authorities to show that while wine can gladden the heart, excess leads to madness and ruin. It also explains the symbolic link between Bacchus and the Lynx as an emblem of the "forgetfulness" that should follow drunken behavior and private banquet conversations.</summary>
<keywords>moderation, temperance, Bacchus, Lynx, wine, forgetfulness, Ecclesiasticus, Virgil, social etiquette, drunkenness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Cerone's discourse on the social conduct of musicians, specifically focusing on the ethics of speech and the dangers of gossip, before transitioning into a new chapter regarding those who falsely claim the title of 'Musician.'</meta>

# On the Music of Cerone, Book I.

<margin>When insults are not insults. 5th Book of Ethics <note>Refers to Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics.</note></margin>
...reprove your neighbor. Let them say what they wish; no notice should be taken of it, since it is a well-known thing that the insults, lies, and reproaches uttered by boys, jesters, masked revelers, fools, and drunks are not taken into consideration as an affront. For according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>: *That is not to be called an injury which is not inflicted with the intent and will to cause injury* <note>original Latin: "Ea non est appellanda iniuria, quae non infertur animo; ac voluntate iniuria afficiendi."</note>. To say that a man should not take notice of the arguments that occur in conversation while eating and drinking, they say proverbially:

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
*No one should be ashamed at the table: for there, matters divine and human are discerned* <note>original Latin: "Verecundari neminem apud mensam decet: Nam ibi de diuinis atque humanis cernitur."</note>; and so it is fitting to consign them to perpetual silence, without keeping any memory of them. Let Musicians, Singers, and all sorts of people take heed, then, that if by chance they find themselves in a place where they see someone who is not very steady on his feet, or who says something quite out of the ordinary—or to put it more plainly in the vernacular, who is drunk—that they say nothing of those things which would be better kept silent; and that they do not reveal anything that deserves to remain hidden: because by doing otherwise, they will be held as infamous.

<margin>The bold custom of the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> at their banquets.</margin>
Just as these same people were held as such by the Lacedaemonians, who were accustomed, whenever they gathered for their banquets, to have one of them say in a loud voice: "Let nothing that is discussed here leave through this door." And if any of them remembered something afterward to reveal it, he was held as infamous and as a base man. From whence came the common proverb:

<margin>Ancient Proverb.</margin>
*I loathed the guest with a good memory.* I do not give this advice without cause, assuming what I understood in Lisbon to be true. What it was, I keep silent; for so it is fitting: it suffices to say that this secret, along with the others that are now hidden from us, we shall know on the day of the universal judgment, where the faults of all shall be manifested.

<margin>On the day of judgment all the faults of the world will be known.</margin>
And even if a similar case does not happen every time, nevertheless it is always good to remain silent, and not to say in earnest or in jest—not a small word nor a large one—of those things said between flasks and cups; insofar as they are the origin of various bad opinions and the beginning of much dishonor. And hold it for certain, that fame in goods and virtues is an Echo; and in evils and vices, it is Seven-voiced <note>Septivoca</note>. I mean to say that just as the Echo of many words represents only the last ones, and a little of them: so we do not recount the skill, excellence, and honor of famous Musicians, singular Singers, and honored persons, except for the very end; and having much to say, we touch only upon a little.

<margin>When fame is an Echo, and when it is Seven-voiced.</margin>
I also mean to say that in evils, fame is Seven-voiced: because just as that ingenious little channel of Olympia, a city in Greece, from one loud word spoken into it, seven sounded (wherefore the Greeks came to call it *Heptaphonon*, and the Latins *Septivoca*, which is as much as to say "of seven times": of which Lucretius makes memory, saying:

<margin>Naturally we are more prone to dishonor and defame than to praise and honor.</margin>
> *I have even seen places return six and seven voices.* <note>original Latin: "Sex etiam ac septem loca vidi reddere voces."</note>

So we, recounting another's error that we heard, add to it as many more, so that for one we say seven; and according to the Latin proverb:

<margin>Latin Proverb.</margin>
*We make an elephant out of a fly* <note>original Latin: "Elephantum est musca facimus." Equivalent to the modern "making a mountain out of a molehill."</note>: from a naked mosquito we make an armed Elephant, loaded with its castle full of men, etc. This is what occurred to me to say on this so sweet a matter, in which I know there was much more to say; but because the pilot, after being tired from the long navigation, finding an opportune place, drops anchor to rest: so I, tired now from the long digression, wish to drop anchors for my pen, and lower the sails of my words.

### How there are some who usurp the name of Musician, not being worthy of the name of Singer. Chap. XXII.

<margin>Pan, God of the shepherds.</margin>
There is no Sacristan nor choir boy who does not willingly adorn himself with the honorable title of Musician. This bad habit was noted by Ganasfa <note>Alberto Naselli, a famous Commedia dell'arte actor</note>, the Italian comedian, when in the last plays he performed in the city of Barcelona in Catalonia, he represented a Pastoral; where among the various other characters, he introduced some shepherds who danced, others who played the bagpipe and other rustic instruments, and others who sang verses of praise to their God: and among them was a swineherd who played a horn, who, discussing who had done it better,

<summary>Cerone warns musicians against gossiping about words spoken in drunkenness, using classical examples to emphasize how rumors multiply. He then begins a critique of unqualified individuals who claim the prestigious title of 'Musician.'</summary>

# Page 65
## Concerning Ornaments and Moral Harmonies

...better, he said: *“We musicians deserve garlands woven by the hand of our god Pan.”*
<margin>Note the impudence of a swineherd.</margin>
Certainly, Ganassa <note>Alberto Ganassa (c. 1540–1584), a famous leader of a Commedia dell'arte troupe.</note> gave a great slap then to the singers of today, who so easily usurp the name of Musician; by introducing into his comedy a vile swineherd playing a horn, who so shamelessly usurped the name of Musician. It seems to me that this bad habit has reached such a point that the only thing missing is for these ignorant people to join themselves in community with the virtuous Masters and excellent Musicians; and that in their conversations and talks they come out with a preamble of *“We Musicians,”* equating the song of the crow to that of the nightingale, and comparing the knowledge of the dull donkey to that of the prudent and wise fox. Without making, I say, any distinction between the professions, although they are diverse; in the manner of that farrier <note>A craftsman who shoes horses and treats their ailments; here used to contrast with a learned physician.</note>, who, being called
<margin>Stupidity of a farrier.</margin>
by a very excellent doctor to cure a mule that had a sore, and on the second visit, when the doctor put two silver coins in his hand, he returned them saying: *“Lord Doctor, take them back; Your Mercy knows that we are not in the custom of taking money from those of the same faculty.”* Who doubts that today those who know how to sing nothing more than a few tunes on an out-of-tune guitar, and all those who know how to play nothing else but the *vacas* and the *zarabanda* <note>Popular, often rowdy, dances and ground bass patterns of the era.</note>, want to be called Musicians? And it is because the vainglorious, for playing these and other similar trifles, and for singing a thousand filthinesses and obscenities, think they sing and play musical things: in the manner of that student who very poorly played a vihuela <note>A guitar-like string instrument popular in Renaissance Spain.</note>;
<margin>Ignorance of a student.</margin>
to whom, when a Lady said to him in mockery that he should do her the pleasure of singing something "of art" <note>Meaning a formal, learned composition.</note>, he began to sing the nominative cases <note>He literally sang his Latin grammar lessons (nominative, genitive, etc.) thinking it sounded "scholarly."</note>: the poor little musician imagining he had hit the bullseye. To recognize one of these wild Musicians is very easy: it is that he accompanies himself with others of his own value, knowledge, and condition easily: because one hugs the other and makes signs of contentment and joy as apes do among themselves, seeing themselves of the same worth. To this purpose we can make use of that
<margin>Proverb.</margin>
ancient proverb: *“To a donkey, a donkey is beautiful, and to a pig, a pig.”* <note>original: "Asinus asino, & sus sui pulcher."</note> From whence Alcimus (as seen in Laertius) took occasion to form these verses:
<margin>Alcimus.</margin>

->*It is no wonder if I speak thus,*<-
->*And we are mutually pleasing to ourselves,*<-
->*And if we seem beautifully born; for even a dog*<-
->*Seems most beautiful to a dog, an ox to an ox,*<-
->*A donkey is beautiful to a little donkey, and a pig to a pig.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Res mira non est, ista si sic proloquor..."</note>

<margin>Theocritus.</margin>
Theocritus wished to infer the same in the ninth Idyll, although with different words, when he said: *“To an ant, an ant is pleasing; to a cicada, a cicada,”*
->*“A hawk is pleasing to a hawk.”*<-
<note>original Latin: "Formicæ grata est formica, cicada cicadæ..."</note>

<margin>Aristotle, Ethics, books 8 & 9.</margin>
Nor is this a marvel, because nature itself teaches and wills that; *“Every like”* (as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle.</note> says) *“seeks its like: All flesh shall be joined to its like: Every man shall associate with his like: Equal delights equal: Like rejoices in like: Like is a friend to like: Likeness is the mother of love.”*
<margin>Ecclesiasticus, chapter 13.</margin>
It wills, I say, that every like should desire, crave, embrace, and accompany itself with another like itself; as for example a monk with another monk, a lawyer with another lawyer, a musician with another musician, a soldier with another soldier, a gambler with another gambler, a shoemaker with another shoemaker,
<margin>Proverb.</margin>
and finally (as Suidas says) *“Simon with Simon,”* because Simon knows no one but Simon.
->*To the old, the tongue of the elderly is most pleasant.*<-
->*To a boy, a little boy: a woman is suited to a woman.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Seni senilis lingua iucundissima est..."</note>

<margin>Advice to the discreet reader.</margin>
Leaving this and coming to the conclusion of the first point, I say that you must take great care not to be very quick to usurp the title of Musician, nor pride yourself on being called a Musician if you are not one: because he who is called a Musician without being one receives no less an insult than a porter would receive if they called him Prince or Duke; for he knows for
<margin>Watch out.</margin>
certain that they call him so to mock him and to make fun of him. Notice on the other hand, do not be very easy or very ready to call every sort of person who professes Music a "Musician"; for you will reveal yourself as having little practice, and you will show ignorance in your speech: for just as that Sacristan or that shoemaker has none of the parts that are necessary in a man to be a Musician; he is as far from being worthy of such honor as he who calls him so is near to being worthy of reprimand. No more: it is enough to have touched...

# From the Music of Cerone, Book I.

I have touched the pulse of this neglected arm; for wanting to extend myself further, the expense does not allow it, and thus I leave the rest to the pen. Whoever desires to know the proper title that should be given to each person who professes Music, let him read chapter 11 of the *Curiosities* <note>original: "Curiosidades," likely referring to another section or work by Cerone regarding musical titles and ranks</note>.

## How at the beginning we must be very attentive to the manner of singing, so as not to fall into some defect. Cap. XXIII.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 18.</margin>
<margin>Medicine that preserves us from illness should be held in as much esteem as that which restores our health.</margin>
<margin>Aristotle, text 33.</margin>
<margin>Evil is taken up quickly, and good only slowly.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus chapters 3 and 19.</margin>
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
<margin>Comparison.</margin>
<margin>In the beginning, defects taken up through inattention are easy to remedy; but they become difficult if they are already rooted.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus cap. 10. Aristotle, 7th Ethics, cap. 14. St. Augustine on Psalm 30. Latin Proverb, 6th Ethics.</margin>

**T**ruly, if the youth in the beginning does not take care to adopt a good manner and good advice in singing or playing—whatever it may be—but instead trains himself in his profession with a thousand types of vices, whether by the fault of his Masters or by his own negligence, I certainly do not know how such a person can turn out to be a perfect Singer or an excellent Player. For this reason, it is fitting that before setting himself to internal matters, the new disciple take care not to adopt some vice (or to speak more properly, some defect) through inadvertence or carelessness. And because there are some vices that can be easily discarded at the start, but after a man is burdened with them he cannot cast them off, it is necessary that we give some warnings to smell the dangers from afar, and to protect oneself in time before the blow arrives; and (as the wise man says) to prepare the medicine before the ailment: *Apply the medicine before the languor* <note>original Latin: "Ante languorem adhibe medicinam"</note>. Note that the medicine which preserves us from diseases is no less necessary than that which increases our health. And this need not be proven by arguments; for the discreet reader will find many examples every day of Singers who, by being incautious at the start in not carefully watching the manners they adopted in singing, later came to feel very late what they should have remedied early. Let the youths remember that for want of a nail a horseshoe is lost, and for a horseshoe a horse. Let them guard against those who usually say, "this is not a defect," or "it doesn't matter much, since it isn't something that shows." Let such people know that: *A small error in the beginning becomes the greatest in the end* <note>original Latin: "Paruus error in principio, in fine fit maximus"</note>; and that according to the Latin proverb: *The pot itself makes the herbs* <note>original Latin: "Ipsa olla olera facit," a proverb suggesting that the vessel—or the habit—flavors everything within it</note>. Let them notice that although the defect does not appear immediately, with time the damage is discovered: for bad habits are quickly taken up and ignite like dry tow <note>the coarse fibers of flax or hemp</note>, but good qualities are slow to stick because they find, as they say, green wood. Furthermore, it is fitting for them to know that they are very near a fall: because it is clear that a venial sin is a disposition for a mortal one; that a small error in the beginning becomes large in the end; that from one inconvenience many follow; and that from a small spark a great fire is made. And so (as Ecclesiasticus says): *He who loves danger shall perish in it. He who despises small things shall fall little by little* <note>original Latin: "Qui amat periculum, peribit in illo. Qui spernit modica paulatim decidet"</note>. He who goes in search of danger will fall into it. He who scorns small things will, little by little, find himself in great ones. Just as throwing a stone into a large well makes a great circle in the water, and from it proceeds another larger one, and this larger one makes another more extended; and after that comes another and others, each time larger almost unto infinity: so from one error or defect another is born, and this brings with it a larger one, and after that come many others, each time larger almost unto infinity, if they are not cut off right at the beginning. It would be an easy thing to cut off a river right at the beginning by blocking the spring where it is born, or turning it another way; but after other and more streams enter it, and with the entry of many rivers it becomes powerful and deep, there is no one who can resist it. And the Holy Spirit says: *A prolonged languor burdens the doctor; a brief languor the doctor cuts short* <note>original Latin: "Languor prolixior grauat medicum: breuem languorem præcidit medicus"</note>. An old illness of many years puts the doctor to work, but one of a few days is the one most quickly cured. And the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says: *It is difficult to resist habit* <note>original Latin: "Difficile est resistere consuetudini"</note>. Which Saint Augustine approves, saying: *The fight to overcome habit is hard* <note>original Latin: "Dura est pugna consuetudinem vincere"</note>. And the proverb goes intoning: *A twisted log is never straight* <note>original Latin: "Nunquam rectum tortile lignum"</note>. Let no one, then, allow themselves to be overcome by inadvertence to some defect; and now that it is time to open one's eyes, let everyone think on what might happen to them if they are not very attentive, and remember that according to the proverb: *In time of peace, one must think of war* <note>original Latin: "Tempore pacis cogitandum de bello"</note>. Whoever does this will show signs of prudence; for Aristotle says: *Prudence is employed in those things in which counsel has a place* <note>original Latin: "Prudentia in illis versatur in quibus consilium locum habet"</note>; and to the

<summary>Pietro Cerone warns music students that small technical errors or "vices" adopted at the beginning of their studies can grow into insurmountable habits. He uses various classical and biblical metaphors—such as ripples in a pond or a growing river—to emphasize the importance of early discipline.</summary>
